Working status: incomplete. We claim no priority. Each numbered statement below carries
its own status. Unresolved requested assertions are kept distinct from proved reductions.
Conventions.
We ﬁx a universe U and let Cat denote its small categories.
We put
BX = |NX|, and W∞consists of functors inducing weak homotopy equivalences on nerves.
Unless Part F says otherwise, a fundamental localizer means Cisinski’s deﬁnition: weak
saturation, the terminal-object axiom, and relative Theorem A. The latter says that for
a triangle A
u−→B →C, if A/c →B/c belongs to W for every c ∈C, then u ∈W. A
category X is W-aspherical when X →e belongs to W. A sieve is closed under sources,
and A/c uses arrows from the image of A to c. Homotopy coﬁbres of unpointed maps carry
their cone basepoint; suspension spectra of pointed spaces are reduced. All mapping spaces
and homotopy limits and colimits are derived. Statements include empty and disconnected
categories unless an explicit hypothesis excludes them.

The sphere-null counterexample
We write Sn
(2) for the nilpotent 2-localization of Sn, and set
N = {X ∈S∗: Map∗(X, Sn
(2)) ≃∗for all n ≥2}.
The mapping space in this deﬁnition includes all its components. Vanishing only of the
positive homotopy groups of its constant component would be a weaker condition.
A.1 (Proved). Desuspension, including the bottom dimension. For every pointed
CW complex X, ΣX ∈N implies X ∈N.
Proof. The precise classical input is the 2-local EHP homotopy ﬁbre sequence
Sn
(2)
E
−→ΩSn+1
(2)
H
−→ΩS2n+1
(2)
,
n ≥1.
Here E is adjoint to suspension and H is the James–Hopf map. We opened its statement in
Boyde [25, Theorem 2.2], whose convention immediately before the theorem is that all spaces
are 2-local. The same statement, including the even-dimensional case, appears in Ravenel
[72, Theorem 1(ii), with p = 2]. We use an actual homotopy ﬁbre sequence in all dimensions,
not merely the metastable exact sequence of homotopy groups.
Apply the derived pointed mapping-space functor Map∗(X, −). It preserves homotopy
ﬁbres. Its values on the second and third terms are
Map∗(ΣX, Sn+1
(2) ),
Map∗(ΣX, S2n+1
(2)
),
both weakly contractible by hypothesis for every n ≥2. Their homotopy ﬁbre is consequently
weakly contractible.
For n = 2 the sequence is speciﬁcally S2
(2) →ΩS3
(2) →ΩS5
(2);
no dimensional exception or limiting argument is involved.
This proves the assertion
simultaneously on components and on every homotopy group.
A.2 (Proved). The closure properties actually needed. The class N is invariant
under pointed weak equivalence, contains the point, and is closed under retracts, all pointed
homotopy colimits, and all three two-out-of-three implications in a pointed homotopy coﬁber
sequence. Moreover X ∈N if and only if ΣX ∈N. Every member is connected.
Proof. For a pointed diagram Xi, mapping out of its homotopy colimit gives the homotopy
limit of Map∗(Xi, Sn
(2)). A diagram of weakly contractible spaces has weakly contractible
homotopy limit. This proves the colimit assertion; invariance and retracts are immediate
from the same mapping-space deﬁnition. Suspension is a pointed homotopy colimit, and its
converse is A.1.
For a coﬁber sequence X →Y →C, apply Map∗(−, Sn
(2)). If X, Y ∈N, its ﬁbre sequence
shows C ∈N; if X, C ∈N, it shows Y ∈N. If Y, C ∈N, apply the ﬁrst implication to
2

the next coﬁber sequence Y →C →ΣX and then A.1. This last use of desuspension is
indispensable.
If X has a component not containing its basepoint, choose a point in that component.
Evaluation there has a section S2
(2) →Map∗(X, S2
(2)), obtained by making a map constant on
that component and equal to the basepoint on every other component. Components of a CW
complex are open and closed, so these maps are continuous. The noncontractible space S2
(2)
is thus a retract of this mapping space, contradicting X ∈N.
A.3 (Proved). The coﬁber construction gives a reversible fundamental localizer.
Let A be a weak-equivalence-invariant class of pointed spaces containing the point, closed
under pointed homotopy colimits, retracts, and two out of three in homotopy coﬁber sequences.
Deﬁne
WA = {u : A →B in Cat : C(Bu) ∈A},
C(f) = Y ⨿h
X ∗.
Then WA is a fundamental localizer in the relative-Theorem-A convention and is reversible.
Its aspherical nonempty spaces are exactly the underlying pointed spaces in A. In particular
this applies to A = N, giving WS.
Proof. The coﬁber of an identity is contractible. The pointed pushout-pasting law gives a
coﬁber sequence C(f) →C(gf) →C(g) for composable unpointed maps. One can see it by
applying the pointed coﬁber construction to f+ and g+ and collapsing the ﬁrst cone inside
the iterated cone. Thus two out of three in A gives two out of three for WA.
Functoriality of homotopy coﬁber sends an arrow retract to a pointed homotopy retract,
so WA is closed under arrow retracts. This directly checks the retraction rule in the proposed
note. More explicitly, if i : X →Y , r : Y →X, ri = idX and ir ∈WA, then the arrow
r : Y →X is a retract of ir : Y →Y : on sources both maps are identities and on targets
they are i and r. Hence r ∈WA, and i ∈WA follows from ri = idX. No asserted splitting of
a suspended coﬁber is needed.
A terminal object contracts the nerve of its category, checking the terminal-object axiom.
For relative Theorem A take a triangle A u−→B →D. The diagrams d 7→A/d and d 7→B/d
are covariant, and there are natural weak equivalences
hocolim
d∈D
B(A/d) ≃BA,
hocolim
d∈D
B(B/d) ≃BB.
For completeness the projection from the Grothendieck construction of d 7→A/d to A has
the left adjoint a 7→(v(a), a, idv(a)). The nerve homotopy-colimit comparison therefore gives
the ﬁrst displayed equivalence, and likewise the second, naturally in u. The homotopy coﬁber
of the induced map is the pointed homotopy colimit of the coﬁbers C(B(u/d)). To check
the basepoints, apply (−)+ to both diagrams and then take their pointed coﬁbers: both
operations preserve colimits. Equivalently the section of cone vertices in the unpointed
construction is collapsed. If every u/d belongs to WA, closure of A under pointed homotopy
colimits now proves u ∈WA.
The coﬁber of X →∗is its unreduced suspension; for nonempty pointed X it is pointed-
homotopy-equivalent to its reduced suspension. The equivalence X ∈A ⇐⇒ΣX ∈A follows
by applying coﬁber two out of three to X →∗→ΣX. Hence u ∈WA if and only if C(Bu)
is WA-aspherical. For a homotopy-pushout square the coﬁbers of its two parallel edges are
naturally equivalent by pushout pasting. The preceding criterion therefore gives the reverse
sieve-cover implication.
This also checks the precise part of POLES B.2 and B.5 used here. For a cover by two
full sieves the nerve is literally the union of their nerves over the nerve of the intersection:
every string of arrows lies in the piece containing its last vertex. The resulting pushout is a
3

homotopy pushout because both inclusions are simplicial monomorphisms. In the converse
direction, POLES’s category K(u) is the two-layer mapping collage with one cone point on
the source layer; its two full sieves are the mapping collage and the source cone. The mapping
collage retracts onto the target by an adjunction. Applying the reverse sieve implication to
that square gives the coﬁber criterion, with the actual functor u recovered by this retraction.
If the source of f is empty, then C(f) = Y+. If both source and target are empty, this
is the one-point space. The construction and relative-Theorem-A argument include these
cases. For the class N, a nonempty Y+ is disconnected and so does not belong to N by A.2;
in particular the empty category has not inadvertently become aspherical.
A.4 (Proved). The unstable separating witnesses. We have RP ∞∈N and RP 2k /∈N
for every k ≥1. Consequently RP ∞→∗belongs to WS, whereas RP 2 →∗does not, and
WS is nontrivial.
Proof. The exact input from the attached Miller paper is Theorem C on page 40: if a nilpotent
space Z has bounded mod-p cohomology, then Map∗(BZ/p, Z) is weakly contractible [68,
Theorem C]. Each Sn
(2), n ≥2, is simply connected and has mod-2 cohomology only in degrees
0, n. The theorem applies directly; an arithmetic-square argument is unnecessary. Since
BZ/2 ≃RP ∞, this proves the ﬁrst assertion.
For a CW complex of dimension d and a (d −1)-connected target Z, cellular obstruction
theory identiﬁes based homotopy classes into Z with Hd(−; πdZ). Indeed maps on the
(d −1)-skeleton are uniquely null up to the required lower homotopies; choices on d-cells are
a cellular cocycle, and homotopies change it by a coboundary. With d = 2k this gives
[RP 2k, S2k
(2)]∗∼= H2k(RP 2k; Z(2)) ∼= Z/2.
The last calculation uses the cellular cochain diﬀerential 2 into the top even cell. It is nonzero.
A.2 and A.3 translate these two membership tests into the claimed functors. Independently
the identity of S2
(2) shows that S2
(2) is not aspherical.
A.5 (Proved). The stable subcategory generated by the inﬁnite projective space.
In the stable homotopy category,
loc(Σ∞RP ∞) = loc(S/2).
Every reversible fundamental localizer containing RP ∞→∗therefore contains RP 2 →∗after
applying RT. In particular WS ̸= RT(WS).
Proof. Put V = Σ∞RP ∞and L = loc(V ). Every localizing subcategory of spectra is a
tensor ideal: for ﬁxed V ′ ∈L, the spectra E with V ′ ∧E ∈L form a localizing subcategory
containing the sphere, and sphere cells generate spectra. This conﬁrms the step of POLES
F.13 used below.
The Snaith splitting we use is
Σ∞QX ≃
_
j≥1
(Σ∞X)∧j
hΣj
(X connected and well pointed).
We opened Snaith [76, Theorem 1.1 and the ﬁltration of Section 2]. Its n = ∞conﬁguration
space in degree j is contractible with free Σj action; its jth ﬁltration quotient is thus
EΣj+ ∧Σj X∧j. This identiﬁes the theorem’s summands with those in the displayed formula.
The tensor-ideal and homotopy-colimit properties give Σ∞QRP ∞∈L.
4

The Kahn–Priddy input is a section of spaces. Its opened formulations are Kahn and
Priddy [57, Theorem 3.1], the unnumbered main theorem on page 103 of Kahn and Priddy
[58], and Snaith [77, Theorem 7.5]: if f : RP ∞→Q0S0 induces an isomorphism on π1, its
inﬁnite-loop extension ef : QRP ∞→Q0S0 admits a map in the opposite direction whose
composite with ef is a 2-local equivalence. Localizing and composing with a homotopy inverse
makes (Q0S0)(2) a retract of (QRP ∞)(2) as a pointed space. We neither assert nor need an
inﬁnite-loop section. Localization of these connected simple spaces induces localization of
their reduced suspension spectra, so
Σ∞(Q0S0)(2) ∈L.
We spell out the passage from this space to a spectrum. If a connected grouplike H-space
Z has Σ∞Z ∈L, the same is true of its bar construction BZ: the reduced suspension
spectrum of each Zr is a ﬁnite wedge of smash products of Σ∞Z, and the bar realization is a
homotopy colimit. This remains true at every iterated bar stage for an inﬁnite-loop space.
For E = τ≥1S(2), these stages are Ω∞ΣrE. There is a canonical equivalence
E ≃hocolim
r≥0
Σ−rΣ∞Ω∞ΣrE.
For a ﬁxed homotopy group the comparison becomes an isomorphism once r is large, by
Freudenthal applied to the increasingly connected spaces Ω∞ΣrE; the maps are the adjoints
of the spectrum structure maps. Thus E ∈L.
The same bar argument starting with BZ/2 gives all K(Z/2, r) and then, by the displayed
stabilization construction, HZ/2 ∈L. Smash the canonical triangle τ≥1S(2) →S(2) →HZ(2)
with S/2. Its ﬁrst and third terms belong to L; hence so does its middle term S/2.
For the reverse inclusion, V [1/2] = 0. Indeed its integral homology is 2-primary torsion,
and a connective spectrum with zero integral homology is zero: its ﬁrst nonzero homotopy
group, if any, would be detected by the stable Hurewicz theorem. The kernel of (−)[1/2] is
loc(S/2). One explicit veriﬁcation is that S/2r is built by extensions of S/2, while the ﬁbre
of E →E[1/2] is the telescope of the spectra Σ−1E/2r. Tensor-ideal closure then gives every
object in that kernel. This proves the equality.
Finally, if W is reversible and contains RP ∞→∗, then T(W) contains V , since the coﬁber
of this map is a suspension of RP ∞. It therefore contains S/2 and the reduced suspension
spectrum Σ∞RP 2 ≃Σ(S/2). The deﬁnition of R implies RP 2 →∗∈RT(W). This conﬁrms
the speciﬁc Galois step of POLES F.4–F.6: T(W) ⊆L is equivalent, by the generators in its
deﬁnition, to W ⊆R(L); the spectrum assigned to a functor is exactly the reduced suspension
spectrum of its unpointed homotopy coﬁber. A.4 supplies the separating nonmembership for
WS.
A.6 (Proved).
The odd-prime desuspension repair.
For an odd prime p put
Jp−1(S2n) = bS2n and let Zp consist of all S2n−1
(p)
and all bS2n
(p), n ≥1. Its null class Np
is closed under desuspension. The resulting reversible localizer contains BZ/p →∗and
excludes M(Z/p, 1) →∗.
Proof. The two Toda ﬁbre sequences, with all spaces p-localized, are
bS2n −→ΩS2n+1 −→ΩS2np+1,
S2n−1 −→ΩbS2n −→ΩS2np−1.
We opened their explicit statement in Bödigheimer and Henn [15, p. 80, item (ii)]; the ﬁrst
also appears as Theorem 1(ii) of Ravenel [72]. Apply Map∗(X, −) exactly as in A.1. If ΣX is
null for the entire displayed family, both loop-space terms of each sequence have contractible
5

mapping space, proving nullity of each ﬁbre. The n = 1 case includes S1
(p) and Jp−1(S2)(p)
and therefore supplies the bottom step explicitly.
Each target is nilpotent and has bounded mod-p cohomology: the James stage has
ﬁnitely many cells before localization, and an odd sphere, including the circle, has bounded
cohomology. Miller’s Theorem C therefore makes BZ/p null for all of them. The target
Jp−1(S2)(p) is simply connected with π2 ∼= Z(p), so obstruction theory on the two-dimensional
Moore space gives
[M(Z/p, 1), Jp−1(S2)(p)]∗∼= H2(M(Z/p, 1); Z(p)) ∼= Z/p.
It is not null. The rest of A.2–A.3 applies unchanged. This is the precise modiﬁcation
required by the Toda sequences: at an odd prime their even-dimensional ﬁbre is a truncated
James construction, not the even sphere. We do not replace that ﬁbre by a sphere without
an additional argument.
A.7 (Proved). The stable comparison at every prime. For every prime p,
loc(Σ∞BZ/p) = loc(S/p).
Consequently the odd-prime localizer of A.6 is not RT-ﬁxed, and its bounded-subcomplex
version of B.2 gives an accessible such example.
Proof. Put Lp = loc(Σ∞BZ/p). We justify the only new input to A.5. The exact all-prime
splitting statement in Caruso et al. [28, Theorem F] supplies maps of pointed spaces
Q0S0 j′
p
−→QBΣp
s′
p
−→Q0S0
whose composite is a p-local equivalence. The section j′
p is not asserted to be an inﬁnite-loop
map. The original paper [58, unnumbered main theorem, p. 103] states the same splitting
and explicitly permits BZ/p in place of BΣp.
Here is a derivation using the displayed form, which also checks the change of group.
The covering BZ/p →BΣp has degree (p −1)!. Its stable transfer, followed by the covering
projection, induces multiplication by this degree on ordinary homology; the compatibility of
the stable and ordinary transfers is Kahn and Priddy [57, Proposition 2.1 and Remark 2.3].
The maps on reduced suspension spectra are obtained by removing their sphere summands.
After p-localization this composite is an integral-homology equivalence of connective spectra
and hence a stable equivalence, by the stable Hurewicz argument of A.5. Thus (Σ∞BΣp)(p)
is a retract of (Σ∞BZ/p)(p) and belongs to Lp. We have used a homology equivalence of the
composite, not the stronger and unnecessary assertion that the composite is multiplication
by the degree as a stable map.
The Snaith splitting of A.5 now puts (Σ∞QBΣp)(p) in Lp. The displayed section puts
Σ∞(Q0S0)(p) there. Iterated bars and stabilization give τ≥1S(p) ∈Lp. Iterated bars of BZ/p
give HZ/p ∈Lp. Smashing the connective-cover triangle of S(p) with S/p then puts S/p
in Lp. Conversely (Σ∞BZ/p)[1/p] = 0, so the telescope argument of A.5 gives the reverse
inclusion. Finally Σ∞M(Z/p, 1) ≃Σ(S/p), and A.6 excludes this Moore space from the
unstable aspherical class. The same countable witnesses belong, or fail to belong, to its ℵ1
bounded-subcomplex version.

2
Accessibility by bounded subcomplexes
We use CW complexes with a distinguished zero-cell as basepoint. The size of a CW complex
in this section means its number of cells. This convention avoids confusing the cell cardinality
of a CW complex with the generally larger cardinality of its singular simplicial set.
B.1 (Proved). The bounded-subcomplex closure lemma. Let A be a class of pointed
CW homotopy types containing the point, closed under pointed homotopy colimits and all
three two-out-of-three implications in homotopy coﬁber sequences. Let κ be an uncountable
regular cardinal in U. Deﬁne Aκ by the condition that every pointed subcomplex of fewer than
κ cells is contained in a pointed subcomplex of fewer than κ cells belonging to A.
This deﬁnes a class of homotopy types, is contained in A, contains every member of A
with fewer than κ cells, and has the same closure properties. It is closed under desuspension
and retracts. It is generated under pointed homotopy colimits by a U-small set, namely
representatives of the members of A with fewer than κ cells.
Proof. We ﬁrst record the cardinal bookkeeping. The image of a subcomplex with fewer than
κ cells under a cellular map is contained in a subcomplex with fewer than κ cells. For each
closed cell its image is compact and hence lies in a ﬁnite subcomplex; taking their union and
all faces gives the assertion. The same statement holds for a cellular homotopy, since each
closed cell times the unit interval is compact. Countably many repetitions still involve fewer
than κ cells. Regularity also permits taking unions of fewer than κ such subcomplexes.
For X ∈Aκ, let PX be the poset of its small pointed A-subcomplexes. This poset is
κ-ﬁltered: the union of fewer than κ objects is small and can be enlarged by the deﬁning
property. Its union is X. This is also its homotopy colimit. Indeed a sphere or homotopy in
the union meets ﬁnitely many cells and hence lands in a common member. In the bar model
for the homotopy colimit it meets ﬁnitely many cells and index strings; a common upper
bound contracts this ﬁnite support into a single member. These two observations prove that
the natural comparison induces isomorphisms on all homotopy groups. Thus X ∈A. A small
X ∈A satisﬁes the condition by taking X itself.
We next prove independence of the CW model. Let f : X →Y and g : Y →X be
pointed cellular homotopy inverses, and choose cellular homotopies from gf and fg to the
respective identities. Suppose X ∈Aκ and start with a prescribed small pointed subcomplex
Y0 ⊆Y . Repeatedly enlarge small subcomplexes on the two sides so that the next ones
contain the images under f, g and the tracks of both homotopies of the preceding ones. At
each stage additionally enlarge the X-side to a small member of A. The cardinal bookkeeping
above permits all choices. At the countable union we obtain small subcomplexes X∞, Y∞,
containing the initial data, on which f, g and their inverse homotopies restrict. The chosen
A-subcomplexes on the X-side are coﬁnal in its sequential union, so X∞∈A. Hence Y∞∈A
as well. This proves Y ∈Aκ. A pointed weak equivalence of CW complexes is a pointed
homotopy equivalence, so the class is well deﬁned on homotopy types.
For closure under pointed homotopy colimits, use the cellular bar model of a strict pointed
diagram F : I →CW∗. Its cells are indexed by ﬁnite strings of arrows of I and cells of
the corresponding F(i); faces use the structure maps. A functorial realization of singular
complexes, if needed, makes all structure maps cellular without changing their homotopy
types, and the preceding paragraph transfers Aκ to those models.
Begin with a small
subcomplex of the bar realization. The cells it meets specify fewer than κ objects, arrows,
and cells in the diagram. The subcategory J ⊆I generated by those arrows has fewer than κ
arrows, since its morphisms are ﬁnite words in the chosen arrows.
For every object of J, enlarge the prescribed small subcomplex of F(i) to a small A-
subcomplex. Enlarge again to include all images under arrows in J, then again to small
7

A-subcomplexes, and repeat. There are fewer than κ objects and arrows, and only countably
many rounds.
The resulting unions F ′(i) ⊆F(i) are small members of A and form a
J-subdiagram. Their pointed bar construction is a CW subcomplex of the original bar
construction: each face remains in the chosen diagram, degeneracies add no new cells, and the
basepoint section is a subcomplex before it is collapsed. It contains the prescribed cells, has
fewer than κ cells, and belongs to A by homotopy-colimit closure. This proves the required
local smallness of the original homotopy colimit.
We give the simultaneous coﬁber argument in detail. Model a pointed coﬁber sequence by
a CW subcomplex inclusion X ⊆Y and quotient C = Y/X, with quotient map q : Y →C.
For a small subcomplex Y0 ⊆Y , put X0 = Y0 ∩X and C0 = q(Y0); then C0 = Y0/X0 with its
inherited CW structure. A prescribed small subcomplex of X can be included by adding its
cells to Y0. A prescribed small subcomplex of C can be included by lifting its non-basepoint
cells to their unique cells of Y \ X and adding their boundaries in Y . These boundaries meet
only ﬁnitely many cells for each characteristic map. This yields a small larger Y1, still with
X1 = Y1 ∩X and C1 = Y1/X1.
Suppose any chosen two of X, Y, C belong to Aκ. At stage r, enlarge the small subcomplex
in each of those two vertices to a small member of A, and then restore the three compatibility
equalities by the preceding operations. Repeat countably. At the union the triple is still
X∞= Y∞∩X,
C∞= q(Y∞) = Y∞/X∞.
In each of the two designated vertices, the chosen A-subcomplexes are coﬁnal in that union.
Both vertices therefore belong to A. Its coﬁber two-out-of-three property gives the third.
Starting with any prescribed small subcomplex of the third vertex, lifted if it is in C, proves
the deﬁning property for that vertex. This treats all three implications, in particular the
sometimes omitted implication from Y, C to X.
Here is also a direct desuspension check. If ΣX ∈Aκ, begin with a small X0 ⊆X and
choose successively
ΣX0 ⊆K0 ⊆ΣX1 ⊆K1 ⊆ΣX2 ⊆· · · ,
where each Kr is a small member of A. A small subcomplex of a cellular suspension is
contained in the suspension of a small subcomplex, so the intervening Xr exist. At the union,
ΣX∞= S
r Kr ∈A; coﬁber two out of three for X∞→∗→ΣX∞gives X∞∈A. Its size is
still less than κ. Thus X ∈Aκ.
Retracts follow from homotopy-colimit closure: if Y is a pointed homotopy retract of X,
the mapping telescope of the corresponding homotopy idempotent of X is equivalent to Y .
Finally all CW complexes with fewer than κ cells have underlying sets of cardinality at most
max(κ, 2ℵ0). There is a U-small set of their CW structures and maps up to isomorphism,
hence also a set of their homotopy-type representatives. The ﬁltered presentation by PX
and the already proved closure show that the representatives lying in A generate exactly Aκ
under pointed homotopy colimits.
B.2 (Proved). An accessible reversible sublocalizer. With the hypotheses of B.1, the
class
W κ
A = {u : C(Bu) ∈Aκ}
is an accessible reversible fundamental localizer. A generating set consists of category-of-
simplices models of all maps between CW complexes with fewer than κ cells whose homotopy
coﬁbers belong to A.
Proof. B.1 and A.3 prove that it is a reversible fundamental localizer. There is a U-small set
Sκ of representatives of the indicated arrows, by the last paragraph of B.1. Applying the
8

singular functor and then its category of simplices gives actual functors in Cat; the natural
realization comparisons preserve the speciﬁed arrows and their coﬁbers. The resulting set is
still U-small, even though its categories may have more cells or simplices than the original
CW complexes. Every member is in W κ
A.
Conversely let a map f have C(f) ∈Aκ. If its source is empty and its target is nonempty,
C(f) = Y+ has S0 as a pointed retract. Then S0 ∈Aκ, and pointed homotopy-colimit closure
makes this class all pointed spaces: cells build every pointed CW complex from S0. The
localizer is total, generated by ∅→∗. The empty identity causes no diﬃculty. We may
therefore assume the source is nonempty. Replace f by the inclusion X ⊆Mf of one end of
its cellular mapping cylinder; its target deformation retracts onto the original target. Put
C = Mf/X and include a ﬁxed source point in every subpair. Given any small subpair, its
quotient image is a small pointed subcomplex of C. Enlarge this image to a small C′ ∈A
and lift the noncollapsed cells of C′ to Mf, including their boundaries and the prescribed
source cells. If Y ′ is the resulting subcomplex and X′ = Y ′ ∩X, then
Y ′/X′ = C′,
not merely a space mapping to C′. Thus X′ ,→Y ′ is a small arrow belonging to Sκ up to
its chosen model. Such subarrows are κ-ﬁltered and coﬁnal among all small subarrows of
X ⊆Mf. Their colimit, and their homotopy colimit as an arrow, is X ⊆Mf.
Every fundamental localizer is closed under homotopy colimits of arrows of spaces after
passage through categories of simplices. This is the relative-Theorem-A argument checked
in A.3, or explicitly the Grothendieck-construction argument of POLES B.2. Hence every
fundamental localizer containing Sκ contains f. It follows that W κ
A is exactly the fundamental
localizer generated by the stated small set.
The use of arbitrary arrows in this generating set matters. Replacing them by the object-
to-point maps would generally produce the proper nulliﬁcation with the same aspherical
spaces, and would not prove the asserted equality of localizers.
B.3 (Proved). The accessible question has a negative stable-classiﬁcation answer.
In ZFC, there exists an accessible reversible fundamental localizer that is not R(L) for any
localizing subcategory L of spectra. One example is W ℵ1
N .
Proof. The countable CW complex RP ∞belongs to N by A.4, hence to Nℵ1 by B.1. The
space RP 2 does not belong to N, and so does not belong to its subclass Nℵ1. B.2 makes
the associated localizer accessible and reversible. A.5 shows that any localizer of the form
R(L) containing the ﬁrst object-to-point map must contain the second. This contradiction
proves the assertion. The construction uses a ﬁxed regular cardinal and explicit countable
enlargement arguments; no form of Vopěnka’s principle is used.
B.4 (Proved). Reversible closure of a small set is accessible. The smallest reversible
fundamental localizer containing any U-small set of functors is accessible. In particular the
smallest reversible localizer containing RP ∞→∗is accessible and is not RT-ﬁxed.
Proof. Take the intersection W of the reversible fundamental localizers containing the chosen
set S, and let A be its aspherical pointed spaces. Intersections preserve all the required axioms.
The coﬁber criterion and forward homotopy-colimit closure imply that A has the hypotheses
of B.1: pointed homotopy colimits of aspherical spaces are aspherical, and the coﬁber criterion
supplies all three coﬁber implications. Choose an uncountable regular κ ∈U exceeding the
cell cardinalities of models for the coﬁbers of every member of S. Then W κ
A ⊆W and it
9

contains S. It is reversible by B.2, so minimality gives the reverse inclusion. Thus W = W κ
A
is accessible.
For S = {RP ∞→∗}, A.3–A.4 give W ⊆WS and therefore exclude RP 2 →∗. A.5
puts that map in RT(W). All intersections may be taken as sets in the next universe; the
resulting generating set in B.2 belongs to U. Equivalently one can deﬁne the smallest closure
by well-founded set-sized derivation trees for the localizer and reverse-square rules, which
gives the same separation-based construction in ordinary class notation.
3
The reversible closure of the inﬁnite projective space
Let Wrev denote the smallest reversible fundamental localizer containing BZ/2 →∗, and
let Arev be its pointed aspherical class. By B.4 it is accessible. The statements below use
QX = Ω∞Σ∞X for connected pointed X, and RP ∞
k
= RP ∞/RP k−1 for k ≥1.
C.1 (Proved). An exact closure description and the R7 operations. The class Arev
is the least weak-equivalence-invariant class containing ∗and RP ∞and closed under pointed
homotopy colimits and two out of three in homotopy coﬁber sequences. It is closed under
retracts, suspension and desuspension, smash product with an arbitrary pointed space, ﬁnite
products, extended powers, and ΩrΣr and Q on connected spaces. Moreover
X ∈Arev
⇐⇒
ΩrΣrX ∈Arev
(r ≥1).
The converse with Q in place of ΩrΣr is false.
Proof. Let B be the least class speciﬁed by the ﬁrst sentence. A.3 constructs a reversible
localizer with exactly this aspherical class, so minimality gives Arev ⊆B. Conversely the
coﬁber criterion for Wrev supplies the two-out-of-three property, and relative Theorem A
supplies homotopy-colimit closure, as checked in A.3 and B.4. Thus B ⊆Arev.
We verify the additional operations rather than assuming them from R7. Retracts follow
from telescopes, and suspension and desuspension from X →∗→ΣX. For ﬁxed X in the
class, the functor X ∧−preserves pointed homotopy colimits. Build an arbitrary pointed
CW complex Y by sphere cells, using X ∧Sn = ΣnX; this gives X ∧Y in the class. The
coﬁber sequence X ∨Y →X × Y →X ∧Y then gives closure under ﬁnite products when
both factors belong to the class.
If a ﬁnite group acts on a space in the class, its pointed homotopy-orbit construction is
a pointed homotopy colimit and remains in the class. More generally F+ ∧G X∧j belongs
to the class whenever F is a free G-CW complex: ﬁlter F by free cells, whose successive
contributions are wedges of suspensions of X∧j. This includes the extended powers and the
conﬁguration-space quotients in the little-cubes ﬁltration. The free little-r-cubes construction
CrX has ﬁrst stage X and successive quotients
Cr(j)+ ∧Σj X∧j.
Its skeletal ﬁltration consists of coﬁbrations after well-pointed CW replacement. Two out of
three at each stage and sequential homotopy-colimit closure give CrX in the class. May’s
approximation theorem, [65, Theorem 6.1], states that its canonical map CrX →ΩrΣrX is a
weak equivalence for connected X, including r = ∞. This proves all the asserted forward
closures. For the ﬁnite-r converse, the adjunction triangle identity makes ΣrX a homotopy
retract of ΣrΩrΣrX; apply retract closure and desuspend r times. C.3 below gives the failure
of the Q converse.
The ﬁbre-sequence clause of R7 used below also holds: if F →E
p−→B is a homotopy
ﬁbre sequence, B is connected, and F is aspherical, then p ∈Wrev. Express the ﬁbration as
10

the homotopy colimit over the fundamental inﬁnity-groupoid of B of its ﬁbres; the ﬁbrewise
maps to points are all in the localizer, and their homotopy-colimit map is p. Equivalently, a
category-of-simplices model and relative Theorem A prove the same assertion. Two out of
three then gives E aspherical if and only if B is. This argument veriﬁes the speciﬁed map,
not just an abstract identiﬁcation of its ﬁbre type.
C.2 (Proved).
Eilenberg–Mac Lane spaces and the sphere’s inﬁnite-loop
component. The spaces K(Z/2, n) belong to Arev for all n ≥1, and (Q0S0)(2) belongs to
it. The discrete space K(Z/2, 0) does not.
Proof. The ﬁrst space is the generator. The bar construction of a connected grouplike space
is the realization of the simplicial diagram of its ﬁnite powers. C.1 gives closure under those
powers and their pointed realization, so iterated bars give every K(Z/2, n), n ≥1. C.1
also gives QRP ∞in the class. Its homotopy groups are the positive homotopy groups of
Σ∞RP ∞, which is 2-local: multiplication by any odd integer is a homology equivalence of
this connective spectrum and hence a stable equivalence. Thus the space is already 2-local.
The space retraction in the Kahn–Priddy theorem, in precisely the form checked in A.5,
puts (Q0S0)(2) in the class. Finally Arev ⊆N by A.3 and minimality, and A.2 excludes the
disconnected discrete two-point space.
C.3 (Proved). The stabilized ﬁnite Moore spaces become aspherical as spaces.
For every m ≥0, Q(ΣmRP 2) belongs to Arev, although RP 2 does not.
Proof. Set E = τ≥1S(2). Its zeroth inﬁnite-loop space is (Q0S0)(2), which belongs to the class
by C.2. Iterated bars therefore put
Zr = Ω∞ΣrE ∈Arev
(r ≥0).
All these spaces are connected. The coﬁber sequence of spectra E 2−→E →E/2 is also a ﬁbre
sequence, so applying Ω∞Σr gives a homotopy ﬁbre sequence Zr →Zr →Ω∞Σr(E/2). Its
base is connected, because E/2 is 1-connective. The ﬁbre-sequence rule proved in C.1 shows
that this base is aspherical.
Next smash E →S(2) →HZ(2) with S/2. The result is the ﬁbre sequence of spectra
E/2 →S/2 →HZ/2. For every r ≥1 its rth inﬁnite-loop spaces give a homotopy ﬁbre
sequence with connected base
Ω∞Σr(E/2) −→Ω∞Σr(S/2) −→K(Z/2, r).
The ﬁbre and base are aspherical by the preceding paragraph and C.2. The same ﬁbre
rule gives the middle term. Since Σ∞RP 2 ≃ΣS/2, that term with r = m + 1 is precisely
Q(ΣmRP 2). Nonmembership of RP 2 follows from Arev ⊆N and A.4. No inference from
stable membership to unstable membership has been used.

this holds for the mod-2 fundamental-class map in the question.

C.5 (Proved). Local spheres and an exact local-object test. Every Sn
(2), n ≥2, is
Wrev-local. A space Z is Wrev-local if and only if it inverts the set of small arrows in B.2
for A = Arev and a cardinal containing the generator. Such local spaces are closed under
homotopy limits, retracts, and cotensors; they are HZ-local and BZ/2-null.
Proof. If f : X →Y is in WS, its coﬁber and all suspensions of its coﬁber are in N. Use the
pointed arrow f+ : X+ →Y+, whose pointed coﬁber is C(f). The coﬁber exact sequence of
mapping spaces shows that Map∗(Σf+, Sj
(2)) is an equivalence for every j ≥2: its source and
target are grouplike loop spaces, the ﬁbre is null, and the next coﬁber term gives surjectivity
on components. Hence Map(f, ΩSj
(2)) is an equivalence. Apply the unbased mapping-space
functor to the EHP ﬁbre sequence of A.1 and compare the two domains X, Y . Homotopy
limits preserve these equivalences, proving Map(f, Sn
(2)) an equivalence.
This argument
includes empty and disconnected domains without choosing a preferred component. Thus
the spheres are WS-local and therefore Wrev-local.
The asserted set of arrows generates the localizer by B.2 and B.4. Mapping into Z detects
a fundamental localizer, so inverting the generators is necessary and suﬃcient. Mapping
spaces commute with limits in their target, giving homotopy-limit and retract closure; the
tensor–cotensor adjunction gives cotensors. Every reversible fundamental localizer contains
WZ, as checked by POLES B.6 and again by the literal-axiom argument in F.2, so every one
of its local objects is HZ-local. The deﬁning generator gives BZ/2-nullity. Nullity alone is
not substituted for the stated map-locality test.
C.6 (Proved). The ﬁnite-target locality question.
The 2-completion of every simply
connected ﬁnite CW complex is Wrev-local. The same is true of its 2-localization. The complete
proof is C.8–C.9 below.
The initial resolving-class approach alone did not establish this assertion. The theorem of
Strom [81, Theorem 8 and Corollaries 9 and 11] places suﬃciently iterated loop spaces of
ﬁnite complexes in a resolving class generated by spheres and gives an unlooped conclusion
in a strong resolving class. Local objects are not automatically closed under extensions
by arbitrary ﬁbrations. C.8 instead uses the strong resolving class deﬁned by nullity of
a ﬁxed source, and C.9 recovers actual locality by ﬁnite smash powers and a convergent
loop–suspension resolution. The distinction between nullity and locality is retained at every
step.
C.7 (Refuted). Finite fundamental group and bounded cohomology do not suﬃce.
A connected space with ﬁnite fundamental group and bounded integral cohomology need not be
Wrev-local, even when it is a ﬁnite two-dimensional complex and is BZ/2-null. The ﬁnite
category witnessing this assertion has 35 objects and 159 arrows.
12

Proof. Let P be the presentation complex
⟨a, b | a5b−3, b3(ab)−2⟩.
The two cellular boundary columns in dimensions two and one are (5, −3) and (−2, 1). Their
determinant is −1, so P is connected and integrally acyclic. Its fundamental group is the
binary icosahedral group SL2(F5) of order 120; the same presentation and order are recorded
in Hatcher [46, Example 2.38, p. 142]. We also verify the group identiﬁcation independently
below. In particular P is not weakly contractible, while its integral cohomology vanishes in
all positive degrees.
Every homology equivalence belongs to Wrev. Hence P →∗belongs to it. If P were
local, applying its mapping-space test to this arrow would make P weakly contractible:
more explicitly, the identity of P would be homotopic to a constant map, contradicting
its nontrivial fundamental group. Miller’s Theorem A nevertheless makes Map∗(BZ/2, P)
weakly contractible, since P is ﬁnite dimensional. Thus neither generator-nullity nor the
additional fundamental-group conclusion of R11 repairs the proposed locality assertion.
Here are the complete conventions for the ﬁnite witness, so that its veriﬁcation does not
depend on accepting PRETOT. Write the two words as w0 = aaaaaBBB and w1 = bbbBABA,
with capitals denoting inverses. Take vertices v, c0, c1, loop edges a, b at v, and radial edges
rki from ck to v, indexed cyclically by letters of wk. For the triangle tki at a positive letter e,
set its ordered faces to (e, rk,i+1, rki); at a negative letter set them to (e, rki, rk,i+1). All these
formulas satisfy the semisimplicial identities. Their realization is the speciﬁed presentation
complex, by contracting one radial edge for each relation disk. Let P be its face-occurrence
category: objects are simplices, and an arrow to a d-simplex is an increasing injection into
[d] with the speciﬁed source face. Composition is composition of injections. The nerve is its
barycentric subdivision and has the same realization.
The program CD_finite_checks.py constructs every object, arrow and composite, checks
all units and associativity triples, and computes the integral boundary matrices and Smith
normal forms. It obtains 35 objects, 159 arrows and integral homology ranks (1, 0, 0) with no
torsion. It also computes the complete Todd–Coxeter coset table of the displayed presentation
over the trivial subgroup, obtaining order 120. Finally it enumerates all determinant-one 2 by
2 matrices over F5, ﬁnds matrices satisfying the two relations and generating all 120 elements,
and thereby checks that the presented group maps isomorphically onto SL2(F5). The exact
tables, matrices and assertions are saved in code/outputs/CD_finite_checks.json.
This conﬁrms the particular PRETOT A.4/A.5 ﬁnite construction used here.
Its
stronger identiﬁcation with a punctured Poincaré homology sphere is unnecessary for the
counterexample, and no such identiﬁcation has been assumed.

local, proving both assertions. In particular this last step uses an ordinary loop–suspension
resolution of the already completed space when Z = K∧

4
Classiﬁcation: corrections and realization results
D.0 (Refuted). Desuspension-closed nullity does not characterize a localization.
Even for an accessible reﬂective class Z having all the closure properties of local spaces,
desuspension closure of {X : Map∗(X, Z) ≃∗for every Z ∈Z} does not imply reversibility
of its associated localizer. The requested classiﬁcation needs an additional condition on maps,
not only this condition on null objects.
Proof. Let S be representatives of all countable connected integrally acyclic pointed CW
complexes, and let P be nulliﬁcation at the set of maps A →∗, A ∈S. Existence is the
usual set-generated nulliﬁcation theorem, in the precise form of Bousﬁeld [19, Theorem 1.1];
alternatively the set of category-of-simplices maps deﬁnes the proper localizer in Cisinski [37,
Theorem 6.1.11]. Let Z be its full class of local pointed spaces.
Every connected acyclic CW complex is a ﬁltered pointed homotopy colimit of countable
acyclic subcomplexes. To see this, start with any countable subcomplex. Its homology has
countably many classes; choose in the ambient acyclic complex a ﬁnite cellular ﬁlling for each
class, add the ﬁnitely many cells of each ﬁlling, and repeat countably. The union is countable
and acyclic. Starting with the union of countably many prescribed subcomplexes proves
ﬁlteredness. Thus P kills every acyclic space. Conversely every nulliﬁcation generator is an
integral homology equivalence, so its generated localizer is contained in WZ and it kills only
nonempty acyclic spaces. Consequently the null class of Z is exactly the integral-acyclic class,
which is closed under desuspension by the suspension isomorphism in reduced homology. As
a set-generated local-object class, Z is reﬂective and closed under homotopy limits, retracts
and cotensors, and under suﬃciently ﬁltered homotopy colimits.
Nevertheless its localizer is strictly smaller than WZ. An explicit detecting local object is
BΣ3. A map from a connected acyclic space to this 1-type is classiﬁed by a homomorphism
from its perfect fundamental group to Σ3. Every subgroup of Σ3 is solvable, so every such
homomorphism is trivial. The based mapping space into a 1-type from a connected source
has contractible components; hence BΣ3 is P-local.
Let T be the presentation 2-complex with one vertex, loops a, b, and one 2-cell attached
by aba(bab)−1. Let u : T →S1 send both loops once around the circle; its attaching word
has total exponent zero, so it extends. The cellular boundary is the column (1, −1)t. Thus
H1(T; Z) = Z, H2(T; Z) = 0, and u is an integral homology equivalence. Its homotopy coﬁber
is connected and acyclic. But the homomorphism
a 7−→(12),
b 7−→(23)
from π1T to Σ3
satisﬁes the braid relation and is nonabelian. It cannot factor through π1u : π1T →Z,
even up to conjugacy. Hence Map(u, BΣ3) is not surjective on components and u is not
a P-equivalence. Its coﬁber is P-aspherical, so the coﬁber criterion fails. This proves the
assertion directly; it also exhibits the distinction underlying the proper-aspherical shadow
of Cisinski’s Theorem 6.1.11. The ﬁnite relation and boundary calculations are checked in
code/CD_finite_checks.py.
D.1.1 (Proved). The exact single-target test and the incorrect inﬁnite-loop lead.
For a pointed space Z, its null class is closed under desuspension if and only if
{X : Map∗(X, ΩZ) ≃∗} ⊆{X : Map∗(X, Z) ≃∗}.
(D.1.1)
Equivalently there is no pointed X whose mapping space into Z has weakly contractible
constant component and has another component. If Z is in the resolving class generated by
ΩZ, (D.1.1) holds. The suggestion that every inﬁnite loop space satisﬁes it is false.
17

Proof. The adjunction Map∗(ΣX, Z)
≃
Map∗(X, ΩZ) proves the equivalence with
desuspension closure.
The latter mapping space is the loop space at the constant
map of Map∗(X, Z), so weak contractibility means precisely that its constant component is
weakly contractible. A pointed mapping-space functor preserves homotopy limits, proving the
resolving-class suﬃcient condition. For the counterexample take Z = K(Z, n) and X = Sn,
n ≥1. Then Map∗(ΣX, Z) is weakly contractible, while π0 Map∗(X, Z) = Z. These Z are
inﬁnite loop spaces. The loss of components in passing to a loop space is the exact error in
the proposed suﬃcient condition.
D.1.2 (Refuted). A single integral or localized sphere does not have the property.
For every n ≥1 and every prime p, the null classes of the individual targets Sn and Sn
(p) fail
desuspension closure. This does not contradict the family statement A.1.
Proof. For n = 1, take X = S1 and use S1
(p) = K(Z(p), 1). Assume n ≥2 and put d = n
when n is odd and d = 2n −1 when n is even. Serre’s ﬁniteness theorem, opened in Hatcher
[45, Theorem 1.21], says that πdSn is Z plus a ﬁnite group, all groups above degree d are
ﬁnite, and the only possible additional inﬁnite group is πnSn = Z.
For the localized target put
X = Tel

The mapping space is the homotopy limit of the tower Ωd−1Sn

these towers are pro-zero, so both limit and derived limit vanish. On Z(p), the limit vanishes

For clarity, this formula follows from the diﬀerence map (xi) 7→(xi −pxi+1) on the product:

For the integral target use the telescope whose successive degrees are 2, 3, 4, . . .. Every
ﬁnite homotopy-group tower is now pro-zero. The corresponding derived limit of the integral
tower is bZ/Z ̸= 0, since the products of the bonding degrees are coﬁnal among the positive
integers under divisibility. The identical argument gives the integral counterexample. These
are countable CW complexes and actual phantom-component witnesses, not merely formal
failures to infer components from loops.
D.1.3 (Proved). The group targets. For any group G, including every nilpotent group,
the null class of K(G, 1) is desuspension-closed if and only if G = 1.
Proof. If G ̸= 1, take X = S1. The based mapping space into K(G, 1) is the discrete group
G, up to weak equivalence, whereas the mapping space from ΣS1 = S2 is weakly contractible.
For G = 1 the null class consists of all pointed spaces, so the property holds.
D.1.4 (Proved). The target BU. The null class of BU is closed under desuspension.
18

Proof. Bott periodicity gives Ω2BU ≃Z × BU, with the base component identiﬁed with BU.
The opened statement [48, Theorem 2.11] says that external multiplication by the Bott class
induces the double-suspension isomorphism in complex K-theory. Its representing Bott map
is consequently an isomorphism on every homotopy group of the indicated representing spaces,
and Whitehead’s theorem gives the space equivalence just used. Suppose Map∗(ΣX, BU) is
weakly contractible. Its loop space gives Map∗(X, Ω2BU) ≃∗. If X had a component other
than its base component, the discrete factor Z would already give nonconstant based maps
to Z × BU, a contradiction. Thus X is connected. Every based map from X to the discrete
factor is zero, so Map∗(X, Ω2BU) ≃Map∗(X, BU). This proves the assertion. The use of
periodicity, rather than inﬁnite-loop structure alone, is essential.
D.1.5 (Proved). The Moore-space classiﬁcation. For prime p and n ≥1, the single-
target null class of the usual Moore space M(Z/p, n) is desuspension-closed except when
(p, n) = (2, 1). The simply connected odd-primary and mod-2 cases are proved in D.1.7 and
D.1.8, respectively; the fundamental-group cases, including the explicit RP 2 counterexample,
are proved in D.1.9. The ﬁnite-H-space question is completely decided in D.1.6.
The telescope proof for spheres does not transfer to simply connected ﬁnite Moore targets:
their homotopy groups are ﬁnite p-groups, so it produces no nonzero lim1 component. The
aﬃrmative Moore results below instead use explicit loop-space retractions, collection-level
ﬁnite-wedge constructions, and a convergent simply connected resolution.
D.1.6 (Proved). The complete ﬁnite-H-space test. Let Z be an H-space having the
homotopy type of a ﬁnite CW complex. Its single-target null class
{X : Map∗(X, Z) ≃∗}
is closed under desuspension if and only if Z is weakly contractible. Homotopy associativity
or commutativity of the multiplication is not assumed. The assertion includes disconnected
H-spaces, with the unit as basepoint.
Proof. We ﬁrst suppose that Z is connected. A connected H-space is simple: its fundamental
group is abelian, and the action on its higher homotopy groups is trivial. Both assertions
follow by comparing concatenation with the multiplication, using the unit. The ﬁnite-type
theorem for nilpotent spaces [67, Theorem 4.5.2] then implies that every πiZ and every
integral homology group of the universal cover eZ is ﬁnitely generated. The rational H-space
theorem says that Z has the rational homotopy type of a ﬁnite product of odd-dimensional
spheres [67, Theorem 9.1.1 and Corollary 9.1.2]. This statement applies to H-spaces without
homotopy associativity: the source explicitly uses quasi-Hopf algebras for that reason.
Suppose ﬁrst that some rational homotopy group in degree at least two is nonzero, and
let d be its highest degree. Then d is odd and d ≥3, and
πdZ ∼= Za ⊕T,
a > 0,
T ﬁnite.
All πjZ with j > d, as well as πd−1Z, are ﬁnite. Deﬁne the countable pointed CW complex
X = Tel
 Sd−1 2−→Sd−1 3−→Sd−1 4−→· · ·
.
Mapping out of this homotopy colimit identiﬁes Map∗(X, Z) with the homotopy limit of the
tower Ωd−1Z whose maps on homotopy groups are successive multiplication by 2, 3, 4, . . ..
For a ﬁnite group in this tower the composites eventually vanish. Its inverse limit and its ﬁrst
derived inverse limit are therefore zero. For the free group Za, the inverse limit is zero and
lim 1 Za
2←−Za
3←−Za
4←−· · ·
 ∼= (bZ/Z)a ̸= 0.
(D.1.6.1)
19

Here bZ is the proﬁnite completion. To see the formula, write b0 = 1 and bi = Qi−1
j=0(j + 2), so
the subgroups biZ are coﬁnal among all ﬁnite-index subgroups of Z. For the diﬀerence map
on the product, D(x)i = xi −(i + 2)xi+1, the convergent proﬁnite series P
i≥0 biyi identiﬁes
its cokernel with bZ/Z. Telescoping shows that D(x) maps to x0 modulo Z; conversely the
divisibility of the successive remainders constructs a preimage whenever the series is integral.
The same calculation in each coordinate gives (D.1.6.1).
The homotopy-limit exact sequences now give
πj Map∗(X, Z) = 0
(j ≥1),
π0 Map∗(X, Z) ∼= (bZ/Z)a.
In the second formula the ordinary inverse limit of πd−1Z is zero; the ﬁnite summand of
πdZ contributes zero derived limit. For j ≥1, the derived-limit term comes from πd+jZ,
hence vanishes, and the ordinary-limit term vanishes even when it comes from πdZ. Since
d −1 ≥2, these are the usual group-valued loop-space exact sequences. Consequently
Map∗(ΣX, Z) ≃ΩMap∗(X, Z) is weakly contractible, while Map∗(X, Z) is not. This is the
required counterexample in the ﬁrst case.
It remains to handle the possibility that the only rational homotopy occurs in degree one.
The universal cover eZ inherits an H-space multiplication by lifting the multiplication of Z;
the unit homotopies lift as well. Its homotopy groups are torsion, so it is rationally acyclic.
Moreover its lifted CW structure has the same ﬁnite dimension as a ﬁnite CW model of Z.
Combining this bound with the ﬁnite generation already proved, its integral homology is
ﬁnitely generated in total.
We use exactly Browder’s Corollary 7.2 [26, p. 47]: for a connected H-space with each
integral homology group ﬁnitely generated and homology zero above degree m, if Hm is
nonzero then Hm ∼= Z. Its hypotheses require bounded ﬁnitely generated homology, not a
ﬁnite CW complex or a ﬁnite-sheeted covering. If eZ had any nonzero positive homology,
its highest such group would therefore contain nonzero rational homology. This contradicts
rational acyclicity. Thus eZ is integrally acyclic and simply connected, and is contractible by
the Hurewicz and Whitehead theorems.
It follows that Z ≃K(G, 1) for the ﬁnitely generated abelian group G = π1Z. This group
is torsion-free. Indeed, if it contained a subgroup Cp, the corresponding covering of the
ﬁnite-dimensional CW model of Z would be a ﬁnite-dimensional K(Cp, 1). This contradicts
Hj(Cp; Fp) ̸= 0 in every degree j ≥0, obtained directly from the two-periodic cyclic resolution:
its two diﬀerentials are zero with trivial Fp coeﬃcients. Hence G ∼= Zr and Z is a torus up to
homotopy. If r > 0, the space X = S1 has Map∗(ΣX, Z) ≃∗, whereas Map∗(X, Z) ≃G is a
nontrivial discrete space. If r = 0, then Z is contractible. This ﬁnishes the connected case.
Finally let Z have several components and let Ze be its identity component. This is
a ﬁnite connected H-space. If it is noncontractible, the preceding counterexamples have
connected source X, so both mapping spaces into Z are the same as the corresponding
based mapping spaces into Ze. If Ze is contractible but Z is disconnected, take X = S0:
then Map∗(ΣX, Z) = ΩZe ≃∗and Map∗(X, Z) = Z is not weakly contractible. In the
remaining case Z itself is weakly contractible and every pointed mapping space into it is
weakly contractible, so the desired closure holds.

Proof. Fix X and write
RX = {Y : Map∗(X, Y ) ≃∗}.
This class is closed under homotopy limits, loops and homotopy retracts. The proof has three
steps: obtain all suﬃciently high looped Moore targets; use a convergent loop–suspension
resolution to remove their loops; and use ﬁnite cone cubes to lower their dimensions. All the
closure statements concern the whole based mapping space, including its set of components.
The precise retraction input and the bottom-dimensional case. For odd p the smash
splitting is

CMN’s retractions are p-local statements. Every simply connected ﬁnite mod-p Moore space,
and every ﬁnite wedge or smash of these spaces, has ﬁnite p-primary homotopy groups by
the Serre-class theorem. These spaces are therefore already p-local. The retractions used
here consequently apply to their ordinary homotopy types. The displayed range a ≥1 was
checked on the printed page of Proposition 1.1, including its endpoint.

Here is a check that the second formula is independent of the chosen complementary summand.
Normalize the lower generator of j to u⊗u. Its upper generator then maps to c(v⊗u)+b(u⊗v)
with c −b = 1, since β(v ⊗u) = u ⊗u and β(u ⊗v) = −u ⊗u. The commutator sends this
combination to (c −b)(vu −uv). It sends u ⊗u to 2u2. Thus the two images also satisfy the
required Bockstein relation.
These images freely generate a tensor subalgebra. Indeed, with the lexicographic order
v > u, their leading words are uu and vu, respectively, with nonzero coeﬃcients. Both words
21

have length two. A concatenation of them has a unique division into two-letter blocks, so
distinct monomials in the two proposed generators have distinct leading words. This proves
injectivity. CMN Proposition 1.7 therefore makes ΩP 2d−2(p) a homotopy retract of ΩP d(p).
Its source and target are loop spaces of simply connected Moore spaces even when d = 3; the
proposition has no additional connectivity restriction here. Consequently an even-dimensional
looped Moore space belongs to RX whenever the original ΩP d(p) does.

We will need a slightly stronger consequence. If W is a ﬁnite wedge of Moore spaces
of top dimension at least N, every Hilton–Milnor factor of ΩW splits into looped Moore
spaces of top dimension at least N. A word of length q has minimum possible top dimension
Pq
i=1 ei −2q + 2 ≥q(N −2) + 2 ≥N. The factors’ connectivities increase with word length,
so the weak product supplied by Hilton–Milnor is weakly equivalent to the ordinary product.
Products and retracts are allowed in RX, proving ΩW ∈RX. The same conclusion holds
for a ﬁnite-type wedge: truncate it by top dimension, use the projections that collapse the
tails, and take the homotopy limit. In each ﬁxed homotopy degree this tower is eventually
constant, so its homotopy limit is the original wedge. This argument uses target connectivity,
not compactness of X.

Finally we spell out the desuspension mechanism, so that this step does not assume the
property being proved. If V is simply connected and every ﬁnite wedge of ΣV lies in a
homotopy-limit-closed class R, form the strongly homotopy cocartesian s-cube generated by
s copies of V →CV . Each noninitial vertex is a ﬁnite wedge of ΣV , including the empty
wedge. Let Ys be the homotopy limit of the punctured cube. Then Ys ∈R. The higher
Blakers–Massey theorem makes the comparison V →Ys increasingly connected as s increases.
The cone cubes have compatible projection maps, and hence V ≃holims Ys ∈R. This is the
Barratt argument in [82, Theorem 8, p. 7].
Apply this argument ﬁrst to every ﬁnite wedge of P N−1(p), whose suspension is a ﬁnite
wedge of P N(p). It puts all these wedges in RX. Repeat, one dimension at a time, down
to top dimension three. Together with the already proved high-dimensional assertion, this
proves (D.1.7.1). Taking e = d = n + 1 and using the suspension–loop adjunction proves the
claim.
The odd-prime hypothesis is used both in (D.1.7.2) and in the nonzero coeﬃcient 2 in
(D.1.7.4). Neither step gives the mod-2 Moore assertion by substitution.

(D.1.8.10)
It is the case n = 2, k = 1 of the formula ΩP n+1+k(2n−1)(2) →ΩP n+1(2) stated in [34,
proof of Corollary 1.4, p. 3258], where the printed range is n ≥2, k ≥1. We only need
this endpoint, not the broader prime-indexed product formula in that paper’s Theorem 1.3.
Applying (D.1.8.1) with d = 6 then supplies the required two equal summands. This use of
the paper’s explicit P 3(2) application does not identify an odd-degree telescope of RP 2 with
a CW complex having ﬁnitely many ordinary cells in each degree; that identiﬁcation would
be false, since the telescope has π2 = Z(2).
Why two equal summands suﬃce. Suppose now ΩP d(2) ∈RX, where d ≥3. We have
just obtained an integer D > d with
Ω(P D(2) ∨P D(2)) ∈RX.
Put K = P D−1(2). The Hilton–Milnor word containing q −1 copies of the ﬁrst letter and one
copy of the second makes ΩΣK∧q a retract for every q ≥2; the case q = 1 is either original
wedge summand. Consequently all these loop spaces belong to RX.
If V is any ﬁnite wedge of copies of K, the same theorem shows that the loops on every
ﬁnite wedge of spaces ΣV ∧m belong to RX: all its factors have the form ΩΣK∧q. Factor
connectivities increase with word length, so the weak product is weakly equivalent to the
25

product. The iterated James splitting (D.1.7.8), now used without any Moore smash splitting,
expresses Σ(ΩΣ)rV as a ﬁnite-type wedge of precisely these smash powers. Its truncation
tower has increasingly connected tails. Therefore all (ΩΣ)r+1V belong to RX. The convergent
simply connected resolution (D.1.7.9) proves V ∈RX.
We have proved nullity for every ﬁnite wedge of P D−1(2). The ﬁnite cone-cube argument
at the end of D.1.7 lowers this whole family by one dimension at a time, down to top dimension
three. In particular P d(2) ∈RX, as 3 ≤d < D. Moreover P D−1(2) and its loop space now
belong to RX. Repeating the same construction with d = D −1 increases the upper bound to
11(D −1) −15, and iteration makes these bounds unbounded. Thus every P e(2) with e ≥3
belongs to RX, proving the stronger assertion. Adjunction proves the requested desuspension
closure. The only homotopy limits taken in this proof are limits in the targets; the source X
has been arbitrary throughout.
D.1.9 (Proved). The Moore spaces with cyclic fundamental group.
For odd p,
the single-target null class of M(Z/p, 1) = P 2(p) is closed under desuspension, for arbitrary
source spaces. For p = 2 this assertion is false: P 2(2) = RP 2 admits an explicit simply
connected factorial-telescope counterexample. Consequently, among the usual Moore targets
M(Z/p, n) with p prime and n ≥1, the only failure is M(Z/2, 1).
Proof. Let p be odd, put Y = P 2(p), and suppose Map∗(X, ΩY ) ≃∗. We may replace X by
a CW model. This hypothesis forces X to be connected: otherwise S0 is a pointed retract of
X, and ΩY would be a retract of the contractible mapping space. That is impossible, since
π0ΩY = Z/p.
The universal cover V of Y satisﬁes

(D.1.9.1)
Indeed Y is the presentation complex of ⟨a | ap⟩, and its p-sheeted universal cover is a simply

integral homology is free of rank p −1 in degree two. Representatives of a homology basis
under the Hurewicz isomorphism give a homology equivalence from the displayed wedge; the
simply connected Whitehead theorem makes it a homotopy equivalence.
Since X is connected, its based maps to ΩY land in the base component ΩV . Thus
ΩV belongs to RX = {Z : Map∗(X, Z) ≃∗}. There are at least two sphere summands in
(D.1.9.1). Hilton–Milnor on these two summands gives a retract ΩSq+1 for every q ≥1: use
a word of length q with q −1 occurrences of one letter and one of the other when q ≥2, and

(d ≥2).

Here is the unlooping and ﬁnite-target step with its source hypotheses made explicit. If

Σ(ΩΣ)rW as a ﬁnite-type wedge of spheres. Hilton–Milnor and (D.1.9.2) put the loops on

gives W ∈RX. In particular every Sd, d ≥2, belongs to this class.
The class RX is a resolving kernel and is closed under extensions by ﬁbrations. We can
therefore apply [82, Corollary 12(a), pp. 8–9], which puts every simply connected ﬁnite complex
in RX as soon as the sphere dimensions include the indicated inﬁnite arithmetic progression.
Its proof uses the collection-level wedge theorem, the cone-length theorem, and ﬁnite cone
cubes. It has no ﬁnite-type hypothesis on X; the ﬁnite-type source hypothesis in that paper’s

Theorem 1 enters only for the stronger assertion about arbitrary ﬁnite-dimensional targets.

In particular ΩΣY ∈RX.
Consider any based map f : X →Y and the unit ηY : Y →ΩΣY . The composite ηY f is

π1Y −→H1(Y ; Z) ∼= H2(ΣY ; Z) ∼= π2ΣY = π1ΩΣY.

has only its constant component. Its loop space is the given contractible space Map∗(X, ΩY ),

D.2.1 (Proved). Every ﬁnite thick subcategory occurs. Let C ⊆Ho(Sp)ω be a thick
subcategory of ﬁnite spectra and set L = loc(C). Then
TR(L) = L,
TR(L) ∩Ho(Sp)ω = C.
Thus every Hopkins–Smith type function occurs for a reversible fundamental localizer. The
classiﬁcation input meant here is exactly Hopkins and Smith [51, Theorem 7]: a thick
subcategory of ﬁnite p-local spectra is one of the chromatic subcategories Cn, including zero.
Our realization argument does not require that classiﬁcation theorem.
Proof. Every ﬁnite spectrum is, after a suﬃciently large positive suspension, the suspension
spectrum of a ﬁnite pointed CW complex. This follows by realizing its ﬁnite list of stable cell
attachments after a common suspension; stable homotopies between the attaching maps are
realized after increasing that suspension once more. Hence L is generated by the suspension
spectra it contains, and the checked formula TR(L) = loc(L ∩{suspension spectra}) from
POLES F.5 gives the ﬁrst equality.
We recall the compactness argument for the second equality. A localizing subcategory
generated by a set of compact objects consists of ﬁltered homotopy colimits of their ﬁnite
iterated coﬁbers, shifts and retracts. One can verify this by cellular approximation: at
stage zero take a coproduct of shifts of the generators mapping to a proposed object, and
successively attach such cells to kill the kernel on maps out of every generator. The sequential
comparison is an equivalence within the generated subcategory. At a ﬁxed ﬁnite stage,
compactness lets each ﬁnite collection of attaching maps factor through a ﬁnite collection of
27

cells of preceding stages. Induction on the ﬁnite stage shows that the resulting object is a
ﬁltered homotopy colimit of ﬁnite constructions. Sequential colimits are themselves ﬁltered,
so this remains true at the limit. If a compact object lies in this subcategory, its identity
factors through one of these ﬁnite constructions. It is a retract of that object and therefore
belongs to the original thick subcategory. The reverse inclusion is immediate. Apply this to
the ﬁnite generators C.
D.2.2 (Proved). All nonzero torsion ﬁnite thick subcategories occur without
RT-ﬁxedness.
Every nonzero thick subcategory C of torsion ﬁnite spectra occurs as the
ﬁnite part of T(W) for an accessible reversible localizer W which is not RT-ﬁxed. In fact
T(W) = loc(C) can be imposed.
Proof. Choose a prime p for which C contains a nonzero p-primary ﬁnite spectrum. Use the
sphere family of A.1 if p = 2 and the Toda family of A.6 otherwise, and denote its reversible
null class by Np.
We need one consequence of the unstable Kahn–Priddy argument. For every ﬁnite p-
primary spectrum F, all suﬃciently positive inﬁnite-loop spaces Ω∞ΣrF lie in Np. To prove
it, the space form of Kahn–Priddy for BZ/p, explicitly stated in the main theorem of Kahn
and Priddy [58, p. 103], gives (Q0S0)(p) in the reversible closure of BZ/p. The proof of C.3,
with 2 replaced by p, then gives Ω∞Σr(S/p) for all r ≥1. Finite p-primary spectra form
the thick subcategory generated by S/p: alternatively, if paidF = 0, then F is a retract of
F/pa, which is built from S/p by ﬁnite cells and extensions. Such an exponent exists because
End(F) is a ﬁnite p-group. A ﬁnite construction by shifts, coﬁbers and retracts can be
suspended far enough that all its inﬁnite-loop-space ﬁbre sequences have connected bases. At
a coﬁber step the ﬁrst and second spaces are already aspherical; the ﬁbre rule in C.1 gives
the third. Shifts are absorbed in the common suspension and retracts are preserved. This
proves the consequence without asserting that arbitrary ﬁbres of equivalences are aspherical.
Write a ﬁnite torsion spectrum as the ﬁnite wedge of its primary summands; the stable
Chinese-remainder idempotents give this decomposition, and thickness puts each summand
of an object of C back in C. For each primary F ∈C choose a ﬁnite simply connected XF
and rF with Σ∞XF ≃ΣrF F. If F is p-primary choose rF suﬃciently large as above and use
the aspherical generator QXF →∗. For the other primes use XF →∗. These latter spaces
are null for the p-local target family because their p-localizations are contractible.
Let W be the reversible closure of this set of object-to-point maps. It is accessible by
B.4. All its generators lie in WNp, so W ⊆WNp. They also lie in R(L) for L = loc(C): in
the QXF case the Snaith splitting expresses its suspension spectrum as homotopy orbits of
smash powers of Σ∞XF , all in the tensor ideal L. Thus T(W) ⊆L. Conversely the ﬁrst
summand in the same splitting retracts onto Σ∞XF , and the other-prime generators already
have that spectrum. Therefore T(W) contains every generator of L and equals L.
Finally choose a nonzero p-primary F ∈C. Its ﬁnite space XF is not null for the target
family. Indeed the Spanier–Whitehead dual of its nonzero ﬁnite suspension spectrum is
nonzero, so some stable cohomotopy group of XF localized at p is nonzero. After suspending
the domain and taking a suﬃciently large odd-dimensional sphere as target, the stable class
is represented unstably. Such odd spheres occur in both target families. Hence XF /∈Np,
while Σ∞XF ∈L = T(W). The map XF →∗lies in RT(W) and not in W, as required.
D.2.3 (Proved). The full ﬁnite thick subcategory occurs without ﬁxedness. There
is an accessible reversible localizer W with T(W) = Ho(Sp) and W ̸= RT(W). Thus the full
ﬁnite thick subcategory also occurs.
28

Proof. Use G.5: the smallest reversible closure of BZ/2 →∗and BZ[1/2] →∗is contained
in the non-total completed-sphere localizer, but its stable part contains S/2 and S[1/2], which
together generate the sphere. The completed EHP comparisons and nonmembership of RP 2
are proved there.
D.2.4 (Open). The zero ﬁnite thick subcategory.
The remaining ﬁnite-part question
is whether a non-RT-ﬁxed reversible localizer can have T(W) ∩Ho(Sp)ω = 0. If the stronger
condition T(W) = 0 is imposed, the answer is no: W ⊆R(0) = WZ and every reversible
localizer contains WZ, so W = WZ. This does not settle a nonzero stable part having no
nonzero ﬁnite objects.
D.2.5 (Proved). A rational stable part cannot give the missing separation. If a
reversible fundamental localizer W satisﬁes
0 ̸= T(W) ⊆loc(HQ),
then T(W) = loc(HQ) and W = R(loc(HQ)). In particular it is RT-ﬁxed.
Proof. Write AW for its pointed aspherical spaces. The stable category loc(HQ) is precisely
the rational stable category: the rationalized sphere is HQ, and its shifts generate that
category. Since T(W) is nonzero, some X ∈AW has nonzero reduced suspension spectrum.
It is connective and rational, so its ﬁrst nonzero stable homotopy group is its ﬁrst nonzero
integral homology group, a nonzero rational vector space. All its reduced integral homology
groups are rational. Also X is connected, since otherwise its reduced H0 is a nonzero free
abelian group.
Suspend X enough that Z = ΣrX is simply connected and its ﬁrst nonzero reduced
homology occurs in an odd degree d ≥3. The rationalization criterion for simply connected
spaces [66, Theorems 6.1.1–6.1.2] makes Z rational. Choose a ∈HdZ and a rational-linear
functional ℓ: HdZ →Q with ℓ(a) = 1. Hurewicz realizes a by Sd →Z, which extends
through Sd
Q since Z is rational. The universal coeﬃcient theorem, using injectivity of Q,
realizes ℓby Z →K(Q, d). For odd d the latter target is Sd
Q, by [66, Corollary 6.7.3]. The
composite is the identity on the sole nonzero homotopy group, so Sd
Q is a homotopy retract
of Z. It belongs to AW .
For n ≥1, the natural rational homology comparison and simple connectivity give
ΣSn
Q ≃Sn+1
Q
; at n = 1 one may use the telescope model of K(Q, 1).
Suspension and
desuspension closure therefore put all positive rational spheres in AW .
We give the needed cellular step explicitly. Let Y be a simply connected rational space,
with a CW model having one vertex and no one-cells. Inductively rationalize the boundary
spheres of its cells. At a stage already constructed as a simply connected rational space, each
attaching map extends from its ordinary sphere to the rational sphere. Attach its rationalized
cone by a homotopy coﬁber, doing all cells in the dimension by a wedge. The new space is
simply connected and has rational integral homology, hence remains rational; comparison
with the ordinary skeleton is a rational homology equivalence by the coﬁber exact sequence.
At the sequential homotopy colimit the result is YQ ≃Y . All boundaries, wedges, coﬁbers
and colimits used are in AW , so Y ∈AW . For the two-cells the boundary is a wedge of
K(Q, 1)’s and its coﬁber is simply connected. No nilpotence assertion about that wedge of
circles is needed.
Finally, if U has rational reduced suspension spectrum, reduced H0 makes it connected, and
ΣU is simply connected with rational homology. The preceding argument and desuspension
give U ∈AW . This is exactly the aspherical class of R(loc(HQ)); the opposite inclusion
already follows from W ⊆R(T(W)). The reversible coﬁber criterion identiﬁes the localizers.
Rational spheres in their null class generate loc(HQ), giving the asserted stable part.

D.2.6 (Proved). A concrete nonzero stable part with no ﬁnite spectra. For every
prime p, put
Lp = loc
 Σ∞K(Z/p, 2)
.
Then Lp ̸= 0 and Lp∩Ho(Sp)ω = 0. The smallest reversible localizer containing K(Z/p, 2) →∗
is accessible and has stable part exactly Lp.
Proof. The exact form of Lee’s theorem used here is the opened [85, Corollary 1.4.1]: for a
simply connected π-ﬁnite space A, the collapse gives an equivalence S ≃F(Σ∞A+, S). Here
π-ﬁnite means ﬁnitely many components and ﬁnitely many nonzero homotopy groups, all
ﬁnite. Taking A = K(Z/p, 2) and splitting oﬀthe basepoint gives
F(Σ∞A, S) = 0.
The annihilator {E : F(E, S) = 0} is localizing, since mapping spectra take coproducts to
products, coﬁber sequences to ﬁber sequences, and shifts to inverse shifts. It contains Lp. A
ﬁnite spectrum in this annihilator has zero Spanier–Whitehead dual, hence is zero by ﬁnite
duality. On the other hand H2(A; Z) = Z/p, so the generator is nonzero.
Let W be the speciﬁed smallest reversible localizer. It is accessible by B.4, and W ⊆R(Lp)
implies T(W) ⊆Lp. Its aspherical generator gives the opposite inclusion. This proves all
assertions without asserting that W is nonﬁxed.
These results narrow D.2.4 but do not settle it. Neither a nonzero ﬁnite space nor BZ/p
can serve as a missing aspherical object for this stable part. Replacing the generator by
QK(Z/p, 2) also gives no separator: the original Eilenberg–Mac Lane space is an inﬁnite-loop
retract of its Q. The stable-cohomotopy claims in [20, Section 10.12 and Lemma 10.13]
additionally rule out B(Z/p∞) and CP ∞as naive replacement candidates. A genuinely
unstable separator is still required.
A ﬁnite-suspension test for the smallest reversible class
D.2.7 (Proved). Ordinary nulliﬁcation after a bounded suspension.
Let A be
a connected pointed space whose reduced integral homology is p-primary torsion. Suppose
that, for some integer r ≥1, A kills every r-connected generalized Eilenberg–Mac Lane space
(GEM) with p-primary torsion homotopy groups. Then the aspherical class of the smallest
reversible fundamental localizer containing A →∗is
Amin Rev(A) = {X ∈S∗: PA(ΣrX) ≃∗}.
(D.2.7a)
Here PA is ordinary unstable A-nulliﬁcation. In particular:
1. For A = K(Fp, n), n ≥1, one may take r = max{1, n −1}.
2. For A = K(Cp∞, n), n ≥1, where Cp∞= Z[1/p]/Z, one may take r = n.
3. If BZ/p is A-acyclic, one may take r = 1. Thus for every connected V with p-primary
reduced integral homology,
Amin Rev(BZ/p∨V ) = {X : PBZ/p∨V (ΣX) ≃∗}.
(D.2.7b)
The statements impose no bound on cardinalities or torsion exponents and use no p-completion.
30

Proof. Put CA = {Y : PAY ≃∗} and Nr = {X : ΣrX ∈CA}. Ordinary nulliﬁcation exists
for every space A [19, Theorem 1.1]. Its mapping-space universal property shows that CA
is closed under weak equivalences, retracts and pointed homotopy colimits. In a homotopy
coﬁber sequence, membership of the ﬁrst and second terms, or of the ﬁrst and third terms,
implies membership of the remaining term: apply the derived mapping-space functor into
every A-null space. These same properties hold for Nr, since suspension preserves pointed
homotopy colimits and coﬁber sequences.
Every A-acyclic space has p-primary reduced integral homology. Indeed, A →∗is an
HZ[1/p]-equivalence; every HZ[1/p]-local space is therefore A-null. The universal property
of PA implies that every A-acyclic space is HZ[1/p]-acyclic. Since Z[1/p] is ﬂat, its vanishing
reduced homology means exactly that every element of each reduced integral homology group
is killed by a power of p.
We prove the essential desuspension implication. Suppose ΣX ∈Nr, so that Σr+1X ∈CA.
The preceding paragraph and the suspension homology isomorphism imply that every eHi(X; Z)
is p-primary torsion. In particular X is connected, because eH0(X; Z) is free abelian. Hence
ΣrX is r-connected. By Dold–Thom, SP ∞ΣrX is an r-connected GEM with p-primary
torsion homotopy groups, and the hypothesis gives SP ∞ΣrX ∈CA.
The exact classical input is Bousﬁeld [19, Theorem 3.3]: for a connected space U and an
integer k ≥1,
⟨ΣU⟩= ⟨ΣkU ∨SP ∞ΣU⟩.
The brackets here denote ordinary nullity classes: equality says that the two displayed spaces
kill exactly the same spaces under ordinary nulliﬁcation. Apply this identity to the connected
space U = Σr−1X, with k = 2. Both summands Σr+1X and SP ∞ΣrX are A-acyclic, so their
wedge is A-acyclic. The identity gives ΣrX ∈CA, proving X ∈Nr.
For the third coﬁber implication, if Y, C ∈Nr in a coﬁber sequence X →Y →C, use
the next coﬁber sequence Y →C →ΣX and the already proved ﬁrst implication. It gives
ΣX ∈Nr, and desuspension gives X ∈Nr. Thus A.3 constructs a reversible fundamental
localizer with aspherical class Nr. It contains A →∗because every positive suspension of A
is A-acyclic.
For minimality, let W be any reversible fundamental localizer containing A →∗. The
ordinary localizer generated by this one map is contained in W, so every space in CA is
W-aspherical. If X ∈Nr, then ΣrX ∈CA; reversible desuspension, applied r times, puts X in
the aspherical class of W. The reversible coﬁber criterion identiﬁes the localizers themselves.
This proves (D.2.7a).
For the ﬁrst listed application, the opened statement of Bousﬁeld [20, Lemma 8.13]
says that K(G, n), for n ≥2 and an arbitrary nonzero p-torsion abelian group G, kills
each (n −1)-connected generalized polyGEM with p-torsion homotopy groups, subject to
the additional condition πnX/pπnX = 0 only when G/pG = 0. A GEM is a generalized
polyGEM. Taking G = Fp removes the additional condition, and gives r = n −1 when n ≥2.
For n = 1, BZ/p kills K(Fp, 2) by the simplicial bar construction: its levels are ﬁnite
powers of BZ/p, nulliﬁcation preserves ﬁnite products [19, proof of Theorem 1.7], and its
acyclic class is closed under geometric realization. Applying Lemma 8.13 to K(Fp, 2) gives
all simply connected p-torsion GEMs. This proves r = 1 and also proves the third listed
application by transitivity of ordinary acyclicity.
For G = Cp∞and n ≥2, the extra condition in Lemma 8.13 cannot be discarded:
G/pG = 0. It is automatically satisﬁed on n-connected GEMs, giving r = n. To include
n = 1, use the universal coeﬃcient injection
ExtZ(Cp∞, Fp) ,−→H2(BCp∞; Fp).
31

The group on the left is nonzero: the extension 0 →Fp →Cp∞
p−→Cp∞→0 does not
split, since a direct summand of a divisible abelian group is divisible. Thus K(Fp, 2) is not
BCp∞-null. By Bousﬁeld [20, Lemma 9.13], an Eilenberg–Mac Lane space K(Fp, m) is either
acyclic or local for any speciﬁed localization. It is therefore BCp∞-acyclic. Lemma 8.13 again
supplies all simply connected p-torsion GEMs, giving r = 1.
Finally these Eilenberg–Mac Lane generators have p-primary reduced integral homology.
One can use the nilpotent localization criterion away from p, or start with the ﬁnite cyclic
groups and use the bar construction, followed by ﬁltered colimits for Cp∞. This veriﬁes the
homology premise in every case.
Remark 4.1. For the zero-ﬁnite-part example in D.2.6, the exact remaining unstable test
becomes
Σ∞X ∈loc(Σ∞K(Fp, 2)),
PK(Fp,2)(ΣX) ̸≃∗.
Likewise (D.2.7b) turns the interval tests generated by BZ/p ∨V into ordinary-nulliﬁcation
calculations. Neither formula supplies the required separating space by itself; D.2.4 and the
strict-intermediate-element problem in D.3 retain their stated status. The initial suspension
is essential: Theorem 3.3 makes no assertion that the unsuspended ordinary BZ/p-acyclic
class is desuspension-closed.
D.2.8 (Proved). Finite primary space generators are RT-ﬁxed.
Let V be a nonzero
ﬁnite connected pointed complex with p-primary reduced integral homology, where “nonzero”
means Σ∞V ̸≃0. Then
min Rev(V →∗) = R
 loc(Σ∞V )
.
(D.2.8)
This equality concerns all pointed source spaces, without a ﬁniteness hypothesis on them.
Thus the ﬁnite primary generators themselves give ﬁxed localizers; the use of inﬁnite-loop
generators in D.2.2 is material.
Proof. Put W = min Rev(V →∗) and L = loc(Σ∞V ). The inclusion W ⊆R(L) follows from
the deﬁning minimum. It remains to prove the reverse inclusion on aspherical spaces.
Let Σ∞X ∈L. The spectrum HZ[1/p] annihilates Σ∞V , so it annihilates L. Consequently
X has p-primary reduced integral homology. Its reduced H0, being free abelian, is zero; hence
X is connected. The space Y = ΣX is simply connected and has p-primary integral homology.
The nilpotent homological localization criterion therefore gives Y ≃Y(p).
The nonzero ﬁnite p-primary spectrum Σ∞V has some ﬁnite type n + 1 ≥1. For each
0 ≤i ≤n, the telescope T(i) annihilates Σ∞V , and thus every spectrum in L. In particular
Y →∗is a T(i)-homology equivalence for all these i.
We use the exact ﬁnite-generator theorem stated and proved in Barkan et al. [6,
Theorem 4.1], attributed there to Bousﬁeld’s 1994 work. It says that, for an arbitrary map of
spaces f, being a T(i)-homology equivalence for every i ≤n is equivalent to
PΣV (Σkf(p))
being an equivalence for all suﬃciently large k.
Apply it to Y →∗, using Y ≃Y(p). We obtain PΣV (ΣkY ) ≃∗for some k. The ordinary
localizer generated by ΣV →∗lies in W, since V and its suspension are W-aspherical. Thus
Σk+1X is W-aspherical. Repeated reversible desuspension puts X in the same aspherical
class. The reversible coﬁber criterion now gives the equality of localizers in (D.2.8).
Only the acyclicity of ﬁnite type-(n+1) spectra for T(i), i ≤n, and the displayed primary
theorem are used. No identiﬁcation of telescopic and Morava-K localizations is assumed.
