is null for every positive and negative shift of C. The shift from Yi to Yi+1 in this paragraph
controls components as well as positive homotopy groups; a vanishing ﬁber alone would not
have suﬃced.
The cone-attachment construction of spectral nulliﬁcation puts each coﬁb(E →Yi) in
loc(C). Since E ∈loc(C), also Yi and Y∞belong to loc(C). A member of loc(C) that is
right orthogonal to all shifts of C is zero: its mapping spectrum out of every member of
loc(C) vanishes, including its own identity. Hence Y∞= 0. Equation (D.2.9e) with i = 0 now
gives Y0 ∈Ho(Sp)≤n−1. But nulliﬁcation by n-connective C preserves n-connectivity, since
attaching cones on nonnegative suspensions of C stays in Ho(Sp)≥n. Thus Y0 = 0, proving
(D.2.9a). This argument neither assumes C compact nor interchanges its mapping-space
functor with a ﬁltered colimit.

The connected inﬁnite-loop space Ar+1 is the delooping of Ar.

We compute the adjacent ordinary nulliﬁcation explicitly. First HFp ∈Ip: smash its
generator with HFp and split the resulting nonzero HFp-module into a wedge of shifts of
HFp. Its degree-two homology supplies a nonzero summand, and Ip is closed under retracts
and shifts. For r ≥1, set E = Σr−1Σ∞B. Its lower Postnikov section is
τ≤r+1E ≃Σr+1HFp.

C = Σ∞Ar and n = r + 2 to kill that ﬁber. The displayed lower section is already C-null by
t-structure orthogonality. Spectral nulliﬁcation preserves this coﬁber sequence because its
third term is C-null [19, Corollary 2.3]. Hence
P Sp
Σ∞ArE ≃Σr+1HFp.

second equality in (D.2.9c) follows. Thus an adjacent strictness question is reduced to the
membership of one speciﬁed Eilenberg–Mac Lane space, rather than settled by a connectivity
comparison.
Finally Ar kills its inﬁnite symmetric product, which has K(Fp, r + 2) as a homotopy

B; the spectral argument does not supply an inﬁnite-loop description of it. This is the exact
unproved step separating the reduction from a zero-ﬁnite-part counterexample.

For a prime q, multiplication by q is surjective by subtracting an integer with the prescribed

reduced suspension spectrum, so Σ∞C(f) ∈AS already forces C(f) to be connected. The
class AS is localizing, since F(−, S) takes homotopy colimits to homotopy limits. D.2.6 puts
its generator Σ∞K(Fp, 2) in this class, so Ip ⊆AS and the last inclusion follows.

Products and the generated class. Corollary B of Blans and Heuts [13] proves that
the cartesian product in Lie(Sp) preserves weakly contractible colimits separately in each
variable. This property passes to L. Indeed stable Bousﬁeld localization induces a reﬂection

 L(k) ∧X∧k ≃LE

made explicit for the K(n)-local reﬂection in [50, Proposition 4.26]. The reﬂection is left
exact: limits of algebras are created on underlying spectra, and stable localization is exact.
Apply it to the product-colimit comparison in ordinary spectral Lie algebras. Local colimits
are reﬂected ordinary colimits, and ﬁnite products are preserved, proving the assertion in L.

Local sphere shifts generate V under colimits: present an ordinary spectrum by sphere
cells and apply the localization. The free algebra functor preserves colimits, so every free Lie
algebra lies in C. Every algebra is the geometric realization of its free-forgetful monadic bar
resolution. Hence C = L.
The class of objects X with MapL(X, Z) ≃∗is closed under colimits and retracts. If it
contains all the trivial algebras, or just the countable family (D.2.11e), it therefore contains
every algebra. Taking X = Z makes the identity nullhomotopic and gives Z ≃0. This proves
(D.2.11a) and the stronger countable assertion. In particular nulliﬁcation at that countable
family, if expressed as an accessible localization, is the zero localization. The argument itself
only used the explicit closure class and mapping-space orthogonality.

Obtaining every trivial source from Ar-acyclic spaces. Put C0 = Σ∞B and E0 =

an implicit higher telescope conjecture, ﬁrst use [20, Section 10.11] to get K(1)∗B = 0 in
reduced homology. Snaith splitting makes K(1)∗QB = 0, since each extended power of C0
is K(1)-acyclic. To make the K(1) versus complex K/p interface explicit, Theorem 11.3
makes QB →∗a virtual K(1)-equivalence; immediately before Theorem 11.11, Bousﬁeld
identiﬁes virtual K(1)-equivalences with virtual K/p-equivalences. Theorem 11.11 then gives
zero v1-periodic homotopy for the connected H-space QB (the same implication summarized
in Corollary 11.12). Finally Φ1QB ≃LT(1)C0 gives the assertion. It follows that every object
of loc(C0) has the same two lower stable acyclicity properties.
We need a unit retract in SpK(2). The object E0 is dualizable by [54, Corollary 8.8]. The
explicit calculation of [73, Theorem 11.1(a)], with n = q = 2 and cyclic group of order p, gives
unreduced K(2)-rank p, concentrated in even degrees. The extension to p = 2 is explicitly
recorded in [52, Remark 4.4]; see also its Corollary 1.3. Therefore K(2)∗E0 has even reduced
rank p −1.
For odd p, apply the symmetric graded Künneth isomorphism to the categorical trace of
idE0. It gives multiplication by p −1 on K(2)-homology. Thus
SK(2)
coev

is an equivalence, and rescaling the second map gives a retraction onto the unit. At p = 2 no

one. Coevaluation is nonzero on K(2)-homology, since otherwise a duality triangle would
induce the zero map on K(2)∗E0. It is therefore an isomorphism between rank-one graded
modules, and hence an equivalence of local spectra. This proves in every case that
SK(2) is a retract of E0 ⊗DE0.

The proof covers every periodic target, even those with an unbounded Lie weight ﬁltration.
The ﬁnite ﬁltration was used only for a set of free generators of the category. It does not
imply that ordinary Ar-nullity is preserved by periodic localization, and therefore does not
settle (D.2.9b).

homotopy retraction. The stable counit is its stable retraction by the adjunction triangle
identity; any space-level representative would also be a bottom homology isomorphism, which
is impossible.
The proof above treats maps from one suspension of Ar. The corollary below extends
the low-degree homology conclusion to every suspension, but does not control successive
attachments in nulliﬁcation. No conclusion about the value of PArΣr+1B is inferred.

Proof. First consider the bottom target degree n + 1. For k ≥2 the source has no homology
in that degree, and k = 1 is (D.2.12a). For k = 0, restrict h along the unit η : X = ΣrB →
QX = Ar. Since X is (n −1)-connected and n ≥3, the Freudenthal suspension theorem
makes this unit an isomorphism on homology in degree n + 1. If h were nonzero in that
degree, the restricted map j = hη : X →Y would therefore satisfy
j∗(σr+1u) = σrv.
Applying Sq2 gives j∗(σr+1u2) = σrw. But Sq1(u2) = 0, whereas the Adem and top-square
identities give
Sq1 w = Sq1 Sq2 v = Sq3 v = v2 ̸= 0.
Here the nonvanishing is part of Serre’s polynomial-algebra calculation, and the Adem identity
is recorded in [75, §4, no. 32.2, p. 224]. Naturality of Sq1 is a contradiction.
Write aY , bY , cY for the classes in target degrees n + 1, n + 2, n + 3 dual to the suspended
u, v, u2. They satisfy
Sq1
∗bY = aY ,
Sq2
∗cY = aY .

next two degrees to be zero. In degree n + 4 there are two basis classes, dual to uv, w. The
latter maps to bY under Sq2
∗and the former maps to zero. Hence the image can involve only
the uv-dual class. The homology exact sequence of the coﬁber proves (D.2.12l).
Finally eHn+1(Y ; Z) = Z/2. The integral homology image of h in that group is zero: its
reduction modulo 2 injects into mod-2 homology by the universal coeﬃcient sequence, and
(D.2.12k) annihilates it. The integral coﬁber sequence gives the asserted bottom injection.
Homology takes the indicated small wedge to a direct sum, so the same vanishing and
coﬁber-sequence arguments prove the simultaneous version. No integral injection in degree
n + 3 is inferred from mod-2 injectivity, and the altered coﬁber has not been shown to satisfy
the same Steenrod and loop-Hopf-algebra hypotheses as Y .
D.3.1 (Proved). There is no largest non-total reversible localizer.
Two distinct
primes already give reversible localizers WFp, WFq having no common non-total reversible
upper bound. Both lie between WZ and W0. Here non-total means strictly smaller than all
functors; it is distinct from the technical properness condition of Part E.
Proof. The coeﬃcient-change theorem gives WZ ⊆WFp, and the actual map on degree-zero
homology over a ﬁeld detects a bijection of components, giving WFp ⊆W0. Reversibility
follows from the coﬁber criterion for ordinary ﬁeld homology. The same statements hold for
q.
Let Y = S1[1/p] be the telescope of degree-p circle maps, and let i : S1 →Y be its
initial-stage map. The space Y is a K(Z[1/p], 1) and has reduced integral homology only
in degree one, where it is Z[1/p]. Consequently it is Fp-acyclic. Its homotopy coﬁber C(i)
is connected and has reduced integral homology only in degree one, where it is Z[1/p]/Z.
Multiplication by q ̸= p is an automorphism of that group. The integral universal coeﬃcient
sequence therefore makes C(i) Fq-acyclic.
In any reversible upper bound, both Y and C(i) would be aspherical. Coﬁber two out
of three would make S1 aspherical. Desuspension would make S0 aspherical, and pointed
cell constructions would then make every pointed space aspherical. The empty-to-point map
has coﬁber S0, so the coﬁber criterion includes it as well. Thus the localizer would be all
functors. This proves the assertion without inferring totality merely from a full stable part,
which would be invalid.

R(L) = WZ[1/2].
They are distinct by A.4–A.5.

reversible localizer, and the requested set-size assertion for an arbitrary ﬁxed stable part,
remain active questions here.
D.3.3 (Proved). Every nonzero ﬁnite primary addition reaches the upper endpoint.
Fix a prime p. Let V be a ﬁnite connected pointed complex with nonzero reduced integral
homology, all of it p-primary torsion. Then

Consequently a proper intermediate localizer in the projective-space interval cannot make any

two, or of any higher ﬁnite chromatic type, cannot produce such an intermediate.

Σ∞V has some ﬁnite p-type n + 1 ≥1. Let d be the lowest degree with πdV ̸= 0, and choose
an odd integer m ≥3 with m −1 ≥d.

Suspension and desuspension now put M(Z/p, 2) in the aspherical class. Its ordinary
nulliﬁcation kills every simply connected space with p-primary homotopy groups, by [20,
Theorem 7.5]. The Serre-class theorem identiﬁes these with the simply connected spaces
having p-primary reduced integral homology. If X is any connected space with such homology,
its suspension is of this form; reversibility makes X aspherical as well. These are exactly the
aspherical spaces of WZ[1/p]. Applying the coﬁber criterion gives WZ[1/p] ⊆W and proves
(D.3.3.1).

Why the Moore cardinal parameter collapses. We strengthen the result just proved.

theorem. Also its connectivity tends to inﬁnity with q.

[Xi, ΩFq]∗
are ﬁnite. The Milnor sequence and the vanishing of lim1 for towers of ﬁnite groups identify
their inverse limit with [X, ΩFq]∗= 0. A tower of ﬁnite groups with zero inverse limit is
pro-zero: at any ﬁxed stage its descending images stabilize, and the stabilized image is the
image of the inverse limit, so is trivial. The same assertion holds with Xi replaced by any
ﬁxed suspension ΣrXi.
The Hilton–Milnor decomposition for a countable bouquet, obtained compatibly from its
ﬁnite subbouquets, is the weak product of copies of ΩFq, indexed by basic words of length q.
For any ﬁnite Xi, maps to this weak product factor through a ﬁnite subproduct. Thus it
identiﬁes the restriction tower [Xi, ΩY d
ℵ0]∗, as a tower of pointed sets, with the weak products
of the factor towers. For ﬁxed i, only ﬁnitely many values of q can be visible, by the dimension
of Xi and the connectivity of Fq. Choose j ≥i that kills the image of every one of those
ﬁnitely many factor types. It then kills every copy of each type, regardless of its word label.
Consequently

ℵ0]∗−→[Xi, ΩY d

This step uses the weak product itself, not an identiﬁcation of it with the full product. The
same proof applies to all the suspensions ΣrXi. These pro-zero towers have zero inverse limit
and zero lim1, so their Milnor sequences give

It says that every countable simplicial subcomplex of a P d(2)-null X is contained in a

The family with a cell-cardinality bound. Let Fκ consist of representatives of the simply
connected ﬁnite-dimensional CW complexes with at most κ cells, and put
N(Fκ) = {X : Map∗(X, Z) ≃∗for all Z ∈Fκ}.

equivalence, since κ is inﬁnite. Suppose ΣX ∈N(Fκ). Then ΩZ ∈RX for every Z ∈Fκ.

The stable part and the precise remaining question. Both null classes are closed under
pointed homotopy colimits. Desuspension and the mapping-space ﬁbre sequence give all three
coﬁber implications, as in A.3. Their coﬁber tests therefore deﬁne reversible fundamental
localizers. Intersect each with R(L), where L = loc(S/2). Miller’s theorem [68, Theorem A]
puts BZ/2 in each null class: all the targets used are ﬁnite-dimensional, including the possibly
enormous Moore wedge. Its reduced suspension spectrum belongs to L. Thus the intersection
contains Wrev, and its stable part contains T(Wrev) = L. The opposite containment follows
from the intersection with R(L), proving equality of stable parts.
The ﬁnite space P d(2) is in the upper endpoint’s aspherical class and is outside both new
null classes. For the single large wedge use its inclusion as one wedge summand, followed by
the retraction onto that summand; for Fκ use P d(2) itself as target. These maps are not null,
proving the strict upper inclusion in (D.3.4.1).
For the family Fκ, larger cardinal bounds initially yield smaller or equal null classes. The
complete countable-source and compactness argument in D.3.5 shows that all these interval
localizers are equal to WS ∩R(loc(S/2)). Thus cardinality cannot supply a separator within
this family. Its distinctness from Wrev remains the relevant unresolved issue.

Comparison of integral and 2-local sphere tests. For every X ∈P2 and every simply
connected nilpotent space Y , the natural map induces
Map∗(X, Y ) ≃Map∗(X, Y(2)).

Here is an arithmetic-square veriﬁcation, including the possible inﬁnite size of X. Such an X
is connected, rationally acyclic, and mod-q acyclic for every odd prime q. Apply the general
arithmetic fracture theorem [66, Theorem 13.1.4] ﬁrst with the set of all primes and then
with the singleton set {2}. The completion vertex in the ﬁrst square is Q
q Y ∧
q . Each factor
for q ̸= 2 has contractible pointed mapping space from X, since it is mod-q-homology-local
by [66, Theorems 11.1.1–11.1.2]. Both rational vertices have contractible mapping space
from X, since rational nilpotent spaces are rational homology-local. Mapping into the ﬁrst
square consequently gives Map∗(X, Y ) ≃Map∗(X, Y ∧
2 ). The second square gives the same
equivalence with Y(2) in place of Y . Naturality proves (D.3.5.5). These fracture theorems
require no ﬁnite-generation hypothesis. Only their special case Y = Sm is needed here.
Reduction of arbitrary sources to countable ones. Suppose now that X ∈N ∩P2,
with no cardinality assumption. G.7 supplies the countable enlargement property for N. The
class P2 has the same property inside X: for a countable simplicial subcomplex, adjoin the
ﬁnite supports of bounding chains in X for all its reduced Z[1/2]-cycles, and iterate. There
are only countably many such ﬁnite chains, since Z[1/2] is countable. Every cycle in the
resulting union bounds at the next stage. This also applies in reduced degree zero and ensures
connectedness.
Alternate the two countable enlargement constructions. Each of the two null classes
is closed under pointed homotopy colimits, so the sequential union lies in both classes.
Hence every countable collection of simplices of X lies in a countable subcomplex X′ with
X′ ∈N ∩P2. These subcomplexes form a ﬁltered system with homotopy colimit X. The use
of simplicial models and transfer to CW homotopy types is exactly as in G.7; it makes no
assumption that the singular complex of a countable CW complex is countable.
By (D.3.5.5), each X′ satisﬁes the integral sphere condition (D.3.5.2). The countable-
source assertion therefore makes X′ null into every simply connected ﬁnite-dimensional
target. Mapping the ﬁltered homotopy colimit into a ﬁxed target gives a homotopy limit of
contractible spaces. Thus the same nullity holds for X.
Conversely, Fκ contains every simply connected sphere. If X ∈P2 is Fκ-null, (D.3.5.5)
makes it null into every 2-local sphere, hence X ∈N. This proves all equalities in (D.3.5.1).
Finally the aspherical class of R(loc(S/2)) is P2, and the reversible coﬁber criterion turns
equality of the displayed null classes into the stated equality of fundamental localizers.

Proof. Fix X satisfying the left side of (D.3.6.1) and put RX = {Z : Map∗(X, Z) ≃∗}.

A smash-closed resolving family. Put M = P 3(p) and
E = {ΣiM∧q : i ≥0, q ≥1}.
This family is closed under suspension and smash product, and its members are simply
connected. We claim that every member of E belongs to RX.
The constructions of D.1.7–D.1.8 supply arbitrarily large integers D for which Ω(P D(p) ∨
P D(p)) ∈RX. For odd p, use the ﬁnite-bouquet conclusion of D.1.7. For p = 2, use the

for all m ≥1. For ﬁxed q, apply Hilton–Milnor to ΣK∧q ∨ΣK∧q. Its factors have the form
ΩΣK∧qj, and their connectivities tend to inﬁnity, so its loop space also belongs to RX. The
two-copy James-resolution argument of D.1.8, with K∧q as one letter, now puts every ﬁnite
bouquet of K∧q in RX.
Since K∧q = Σq(D−4)M∧q, the ﬁnite cone cubes of [82, Theorem 8] descend this entire
ﬁnite-bouquet family to ΣiM∧q whenever 0 ≤i ≤q(D −4). There are arbitrarily large
choices of D, so the asserted membership holds for every i, q. Theorem 7 of the same paper
now gives ΣE∨⊆RX, where the superscript denotes ﬁnite-type wedges. Because RX is a
strong resolving class, its Corollary 11 puts in RX every simply connected space with ﬁnite

made.

(D.3.6.5)
The assertion concerns spaces, not the inverse limit of Moore spectra.
Here are the precise pro-space tools. In [55, Theorems 3.3, 6.3, 6.7 and 7.2; Proposition 7.3],
a pro-space is ﬁbrant for the Fp-cohomology model structure if it is strictly ﬁbrant and
essentially levelwise {K(Fp, j) : j ≥0}-nilpotent. Proposition 3.5 there identiﬁes this last
condition with a ﬁnite tower of principal ﬁbrations with those ﬁbres. The Fp-homology
structure has fewer acyclic coﬁbrations, so an object ﬁbrant for the cohomology structure
is also ﬁbrant for the homology structure. Proposition 7.3 identiﬁes a homological ﬁbrant
replacement of a connected space with the Postnikov-truncated Bousﬁeld–Kan tower. The
cited numbering is that of the opened author preprint.
Apply these facts to the pro-space
Yr,N = PNMr
(r, N ≥1).
Each of its levels is simply connected and has only ﬁnitely many nonzero homotopy groups,
all ﬁnite p-groups. Its Postnikov tower is a ﬁnite principal tower with ﬁbres K(A, j) for
ﬁnite abelian p-groups A. Reﬁning each A by a ﬁnite ﬁltration with successive quotient Fp
reﬁnes this to a ﬁnite principal tower with ﬁbres K(Fp, j). Thus every level is nilpotent in
the precise sense required above. A strict ﬁbrant replacement Y ′ remains essentially levelwise
of this type, and so is ﬁbrant in both of Isaksen’s model structures. Equivalently, choose
a coﬁnal diagonal tower and its ordinary ﬁbration replacement; each replacement level is
weakly equivalent to the original ﬁnite Postnikov level.
The compatible map cSn →Y is an isomorphism on pro-homology with coeﬃcients in
Fp. Indeed for each ﬁxed degree, suﬃciently high Postnikov truncation does not change that
homology. The Moore space has one reduced mod-p class in degree n and one in degree
n + 1. The ﬁrst has identity transition, while the second has zero transition because the top
cellular degree is p. The second pro-group is therefore zero. This veriﬁes the homological
weak-equivalence hypothesis, including coeﬃcients in every Fp-module.
It follows that Y ′ is a homological ﬁbrant model of the completion of Sn. By Proposition 7.3
and Theorem 7.2 of the cited source, it is weakly equivalent between ﬁbrant objects to a
strict ﬁbrant model of {PNRNSn}N, where RNSn is the Nth Bousﬁeld–Kan Fp-tower stage.
Taking the derived inverse limit preserves that equivalence. To check this model-categorically,
the constant-pro-space functor preserves coﬁbrations and ordinary weak equivalences, and
hence is left Quillen from spaces to the homological pro-space structure; its right adjoint is
inverse limit. The derived limit of the latter tower is holimN RNSn = (Sn)∧
p : removing the
Postnikov truncations does not change the limit, since in each ﬁxed homotopy degree the
truncation is eventually an isomorphism, also one degree above it for the lim1 term. On the

A countable list for reversible classes generated by polyGEMs
D.3.7 (Proved). Abelian groups do not retain arbitrary-cardinal distinctions. Put
Cp∞= Z[1/p]/Z. There is a countable list of countable connected spaces
E = {BQ} ∪{K(Z(p), n), K(Fp, n), K(Cp∞, n) : p prime, n ≥1}
(D.3.7a)
with the following property. For every abelian group A and n ≥1, the smallest reversible
fundamental localizer containing K(A, n) →∗is generated reversibly by a subset of
{BQ} ∪{K(Z(p), n), K(Fp, n), K(Cp∞, n) : p prime}.
(D.3.7b)
The same conclusion with the universal list E holds for any class of connected polyGEM
generators, in particular for any class of connected GEMs or nilpotent ﬁnite Postnikov pieces.
Consequently these localizers are accessible, each has a countable CW complex as a single
reversible aspherical generator, and there are at most 2ℵ0 of them. These conclusions hold in
the ﬁxed small universe and use no large cardinal hypothesis.
More precisely, if A ̸= 0 is torsion-free and J = {p : pA = A}, then
min Rev(K(A, n)) = min Rev(K(Z[J−1], n)).
(D.3.7c)
For a nonzero p-primary torsion group A, its ordinary nullity, and hence its reversible closure,
is that of K(Cp∞, n) if A is divisible, and that of K(Fp, n) otherwise. The list is a list
of suﬃcient normal forms; no assertion that all its subsets generate distinct localizers is
intended.
Proof. We write Q = R(loc(HQ)) and write Rev(X) for the smallest reversible localizer with
X aspherical. Ordinary X-acyclicity implies asphericity for Rev(X). We use ordinary nullity
only where explicitly stated. Its acyclic objects are closed under retracts, pointed homotopy
colimits and ﬁbre extensions [20, beginning of Section 5]; in particular they are closed under
ﬁnite products.
Rational changes of abelian groups. First, Rev(K(Q, n)) = Q for every n ≥1: its suspension
spectrum is nonzero and rational, so D.2.5 applies to its reversible closure. If A ⊗Q ̸= 0, the
telescope of multiplication by 2, 3, 4, . . . on K(A, n) is K(A ⊗Q, n). The latter has K(Q, n)
as a retract. Thus
A ⊗Q ̸= 0
=⇒
Q ⊆Rev(K(A, n)).
(D.3.7d)
53

If 0 →U →V →D →0 is exact and D is a rational vector space, then K(U, n) →K(V, n)
is an HFℓ-equivalence for every prime ℓ. Indeed, in
K(U, n) −→K(V, n) −→K(D, n)
the base is mod-ℓacyclic and its action on the homology of the ﬁbre is trivial. For n = 1
the latter assertion follows from commutativity of the abelian groups; for n ≥2 the base is
simply connected. The Serre spectral sequence proves the assertion also at n = 1, without
a claim that a disconnected homotopy ﬁbre is aspherical. A rational kernel instead gives a
rational ﬁbre and the same homology conclusion. A map between connected spaces which is
a mod-ℓhomology equivalence for every ℓhas a homotopy coﬁber with uniquely divisible
integral homology, by the integral universal coeﬃcient sequences. Its suspension spectrum is
rational: the ﬁbre of its rationalization is bounded below and integrally homology-acyclic,
and hence zero by the stable Hurewicz theorem. Such a map is therefore in Q.
Consequently kernels or cokernels which are rational vector spaces may be discarded when
comparing reversible aspherical classes that already contain Q. All the comparisons below
involving large products use this fact, not closure under arbitrary products.
Torsion groups. Let 0 ̸= A be p-primary. If A is divisible, the elementary structure theorem
for divisible groups expresses it as a nonempty direct sum of copies of Cp∞. Direct-summand
retracts and ﬁltered colimits of ﬁnite products give equality of ordinary nullity with K(Cp∞, n).
If A is not divisible, then A/pA ̸= 0, since all other primes already act invertibly on A.
Thus
[K(A, n), K(Fp, n)]∗= Hn(K(A, n); Fp) = Hom(A, Fp) ̸= 0.
The exact dichotomy in Bousﬁeld [20, Lemma 9.13] says that K(Fp, n) is either local or
acyclic for any localization. It is not K(A, n)-null, so it is K(A, n)-acyclic. Conversely, A is
the ﬁltered union of its ﬁnite subgroups. A ﬁnite abelian p-group has a ﬁnite ﬁltration with
quotients Fp. The associated ﬁbre extensions of Eilenberg–Mac Lane spaces, followed by the
ﬁltered homotopy colimit, make K(A, n) K(Fp, n)-acyclic. This proof works for every n ≥1.
For a general torsion group A = L
p Ap, each K(Ap, n) is a retract of K(A, n), and the
latter is the ﬁltered homotopy colimit of the ﬁnite products of these factors. This proves the
torsion part of the claim using only the two stated types at each prime.
Two module facts, including the required realization. We record the algebraic and homotopical
details needed for a mixed group. Every Zp-module M, viewed as an abstract abelian group,
satisﬁes
K(M, n) is K(Zp, n)-acyclic.
(D.3.7e)
The discrete valuation ring Zp is a principal ideal domain, so M has a free resolution
0 →F1 →F0 →M →0 of length one. Each K(Fi, n) is a ﬁltered homotopy colimit of ﬁnite
products of K(Zp, n). In simplicial abelian group models, realize the bar construction for
the additive action of K(F1, n) on K(F0, n). Its terms are K(F0, n) × K(F1, n)j and are all
K(Zp, n)-acyclic. Its realization is K(M, n): the homotopy quotient has ﬁbre sequence
K(F0, n) −→K(F0, n)//K(F1, n) −→K(F1, n + 1),
and the homotopy exact sequence, with injective connecting map F1 →F0, leaves just M in
degree n. Homotopy-colimit closure proves (D.3.7e). This is a genuine bar quotient; it does
not use a two-out-of-three rule for arbitrary homotopy ﬁbres.
Next let F be a torsion-free abelian group, uniquely divisible by every prime other than
p. Its p-adic completion
bFp = lim
k F/pkF
54

is a torsion-free, separated and complete Zp-module, and bFp/p bFp = F/pF. For completeness,
torsion-freeness of F makes division by p unique modulo pk whenever an element vanishes
modulo p. Applied to compatible residue classes, this identiﬁes the kernel of bFp →F/pF
with p bFp and shows that p-torsion in the limit is zero. The same argument with pk gives all
the displayed completeness assertions.
Every torsion-free separated complete Zp-module M is the completion of a free Zp-module.
Choose lifts of a basis of M/pM and map the completion of the free module on this basis to
M. Successive ﬁnite expressions modulo p give surjectivity by p-adic convergence. An element
of the kernel is divisible by p in the source; torsion-freeness of the target puts its quotient
back in the kernel. Iteration and separatedness make the kernel zero. If M/pM ̸= 0, a chosen
coordinate of this completed free module exhibits Zp as a retract of M. The completed free
module need not be free as an abstract module. Only the preceding length-one resolution
uses abstract freeness.

is a rational vector space. Indeed, for each prime ℓ, the kernel and cokernel of multiplication
by ℓon either the sum or the product are exactly those on its ℓ-coordinate. The maps on

makes multiplication by ℓan isomorphism on (D.3.7h). Hence modulo Q the contribution
55

of the product is the join of the individual K(Cp, n) contributions: the sum is a ﬁltered
homotopy colimit of ﬁnite products, and each coordinate is a retract of the product.
Reduction at one prime. Fix p, set M = Cp, let T be its torsion subgroup, and put F = M/T.
The torsion in M is p-primary. Both M and F are uniquely divisible by all other primes, and
F is torsion-free. The long Ext sequence and (D.3.7g) give Ext1(Q, F) = 0, since Ext2
Z = 0.
If F/pF = 0, then F is a rational vector space. Thus K(T, n) and K(M, n) agree modulo
Q. The group T is reduced: a nonzero divisible subgroup would contain a copy of Cp∞and
receive a nonzero map from Q, contradicting (D.3.7g). When T ̸= 0 its ordinary nullity is
therefore that of K(Fp, n) by the torsion argument. When T = 0 the group M = F is rational
and (D.3.7g) makes M = 0.
Suppose instead that F/pF ̸= 0. The same exact sequence used in (D.3.7f), now applied
to the torsion-free group F, gives
0 −→DF −→F −→bFp −→0,
(D.3.7i)
where DF is a rational vector space. The other prime completions vanish, and the cokernel is
Ext1(Q, F) = 0. We used here the precise completion formula
Ext1(Cp∞, F) = lim
k F/pkF
for torsion-free F; more generally it holds for bounded p-torsion, as stated in Bousﬁeld [19,
Section 4, p. 41]. The subgroup DF is injective, so (D.3.7i) splits. The completed-free-module
argument above supplies a surjection bFp →Zp. Its composite gives a surjection h : M →Zp.
Every additive map h : M →Zp is Zp-linear. For r ∈Zp, choose integers ak ≡r (mod pk);
then h(rx) −akh(x) ∈pkZp for all k. Separatedness of the target gives h(rx) = rh(x).
Choosing x ∈M with h(x) = 1 therefore splits h by r 7→rx. Thus K(Zp, n) is a retract
of K(M, n), while (D.3.7e) gives the reverse ordinary acyclicity. In this case K(M, n) and
K(Zp, n) have the same ordinary nullity.
Finally Zp/Z(p) is a rational vector space. Multiplication by p is injective on the quotient
because Q ∩Zp = Z(p), and is surjective by subtracting an integer with the prescribed residue
modulo p; every other prime is already invertible on both groups. Consequently K(Zp, n) can
be replaced by the countable K(Z(p), n) in the presence of Q. This completes the reduction
of every mixed abelian group to (D.3.7b).
The torsion-free formula, arbitrary GEMs and polyGEMs. For a torsion-free group A, the
argument before reduction at one prime has Cp = bAp; it is zero exactly when A/pA = 0.
Every nonzero Cp is torsion-free and has a Zp retract, so its contribution is precisely the
K(Z(p), n) contribution modulo Q. This proﬁle is identical for A and Z[J−1], proving (D.3.7c).
A connected GEM G = Q
n≥1 K(An, n) is weakly equivalent to the homotopy colimit of
its ﬁnite partial products: at any ﬁxed homotopy degree all suﬃciently late new factors are
more highly connected. Each factor is a retract. Thus its reversible closure is the join of the
closures of its Eilenberg–Mac Lane factors. For any class of such GEM generators, apply
(D.3.7b) to all these factors and take the union of the resulting subsets of the single countable
list E. The wedge of that subset is a countable CW complex: each listed abelian group has a
countable simplicial Eilenberg–Mac Lane model. Its reversible closure is the required localizer,
accessible by B.4. There are only 2ℵ0 subsets of the list, proving the cardinal bound.
The extension to polyGEMs uses a precise ordinary-acyclicity theorem, not closure under
inverse limits. In the terminology of Chachólski et al. [33], a GEM is a 1-polyGEM, and an
s-polyGEM is a homotopy retract of the ﬁbre of a map from an (s −1)-polyGEM to a GEM.
Their Proposition 6.2(4) identiﬁes the ordinary acyclic classes generated by a polyGEM X
and by its free abelian group construction ZX. For connected X, Dold–Thom identiﬁes
56

ZX with the GEM Q
n≥1 K( eHn(X; Z), n). Mutual ordinary acyclicity gives equality of their
reversible closures, so the preceding countable-list reduction applies to every class of connected
polyGEMs. Finally, [33, Proposition 5.2] makes every connected nilpotent ﬁnite Postnikov
piece a polyGEM. These two cited statements impose no cardinality or ﬁnite-generation
bound.
The ordinary nullity classes obtained from rigid abelian groups in Stanley [79, Theorems 8.2
and 8.5] therefore cannot supply arbitrarily many reversible classes. Their unsuspended
K(A, n) separators do not survive this reduction. D.3.7 proves a set-size statement for all
classes generated by connected polyGEMs, including their intersection with any speciﬁed
stable-part ﬁbre. It does not assert that an arbitrary reversible localizer is generated by
polyGEMs, and does not settle the general set-size question in D.3.2.
Consequence for the projective-space interval. Let p be any prime. A reversible localizer
generated by connected inﬁnite-loop spaces and satisfying
Rev(BZ/p) ⊆W ⊆R(loc(S/p))
must equal Rev(BZ/p). Indeed, each aspherical generator X has trivial reduced Z[1/p]-
homology.
A connected inﬁnite-loop space is nilpotent, so the nilpotent homological
localization criterion [66, Theorems 6.1.1–6.1.2] gives πi(X)[1/p] = 0 for every i ≥1.
Choose its connected connective spectrum E, so X ≃Ω∞E and E[1/p] = 0. By Levy
[59, Theorem 3.2], X is already ordinarily BZ/p-acyclic. Thus every generator belongs to
the lower endpoint and W equals that endpoint. The same conclusion holds for connected
polyGEM generators, by replacing each by the ordinarily equivalent GEM just used in the
proof. Hence such generators cannot produce a strict intermediate in the interval of D.3.2.
D.3.8 (Proved). A countable bound for primary inﬁnite-loop presentations of a
ﬁxed stable part. Fix a prime p. Let {Xi}i∈I be a set or class of connected inﬁnite-loop

n ≥1 be the least degree in which some πnXi is nonzero, and put
L = loc{Σ∞Xi : i ∈I},
W = min Rev{Xi →∗: i ∈I}.
Then T(W) = L. If some πnXi is not divisible, equivalently some Hn(Xi; Fp) ̸= 0, then
W = min Rev{Ω∞E →∗: E ∈L ∩Ho(Sp)≥n}.
(D.3.8a)
If every πnXi is divisible, then

either by the nilpotent localization criterion or by its Postnikov tower degree by degree.

By (D.3.8c), every higher inﬁnite-loop generator on the right of (D.3.8b) is ordinarily
A-acyclic; the Prüfer-space generator was already shown to be so. This gives one inclusion
in (D.3.8b). For the other write Di = πnEi. Each Di is a direct sum of copies of Cp∞,
possibly zero. Since K(Cp∞, n) is W-aspherical, its suspension spectrum belongs to L. Its
ordinary acyclic class contains K(Di, n), and Proposition 3.1 then puts ΣnHDi in L as well.
Consequently Ei ∈L implies τ≥n+1Ei ∈L. In the Postnikov ﬁbre sequence
Ω∞τ≥n+1Ei −→Xi −→K(Di, n)
both the ﬁbre and the base are aspherical for the right side of (D.3.8b). The ordinary acyclic
class generated by their wedge is closed under ﬁbre extensions and is contained in that
reversible class. Thus Xi is aspherical there. This uses ﬁbre and base to obtain the total
space; it makes no inference of ﬁbre acyclicity from the other two terms.
Class families and the cardinal bound. For a class I, every individual member of loc{Σ∞Xi :
i ∈I} lies in the localizing subcategory generated by a small subfamily. Indeed the union
of those subcategories over all small subfamilies is itself closed under shifts, coﬁbers and
small coproducts: a small collection of small subfamilies is contained in their small union.
This union contains all the generators and therefore is L. Given an individual E in the
required connective range, choose such a small subfamily and adjoin one source Xj witnessing
the least degree and its indicated nondivisibility or divisibility case. The corresponding
preceding argument applied to its wedge makes Ω∞E ordinarily acyclic for that wedge, hence
W-aspherical. The Prüfer generator and the reverse Postnikov argument in the divisible case
use the same witness. Both formulas therefore hold in the class case. There are just two
displayed possibilities at each positive integer n, proving the countable bound for ﬁxed p and
L.
Finally suppose L = loc{Fa : a ∈J} for a small set J. Apply the small-subfamily
observation to each Fa and take the union of the resulting source subfamilies, together with
the indicated witness Xj. This is a small subfamily with the same stable part, least degree
and divisibility ﬂag. Its reversible closure equals W by the appropriate formula, and B.4
makes it accessible.
This ﬁxes the possible number of primary inﬁnite-loop presentations of a given stable
part. It neither determines which of the countably many displayed localizers coincide nor
decides the adjacent strictness test in D.2.9. In particular, it does not replace the nonlinear
suspension-nulliﬁcation test needed for the zero-ﬁnite-part separator.
5
The intrinsic ﬁbrations of integral homology
E.1 (Refuted). The proposed comma-category criterion. The equivalence in Part
E.1 is false. In fact, its stated suﬃcient conditions do not imply membership in FibWZ, even
for a map between ﬁnite posets with contractible ﬁbers and comma categories.
Proof. Let B = [2], and let E have four objects a, b, d, c and precisely the nonidentity arrows
a −→b ←−d −→c.
Deﬁne p(a) = 0, p(b) = p(d) = 1, and p(c) = 2. The strict ﬁbers are {a}, the interval d →b,
and {c}. The comma categories E/0, E/1, E/2 are respectively {a}, the span a →b ←d,
and E. Their nerves are trees. Every map in condition (i) is therefore an integral homology
isomorphism. The systems in condition (ii) are H0 = Z and Hq = 0 for q > 0, so (ii) holds
for every integral homology equivalence by its very deﬁnition.
59

Let v : [1] →[2] select the arrow 0 →2, and let u : [0] →[1] select its initial object. The
functor u is a weak homotopy equivalence over B. Its pullback along p is
{a} −→{a, c},
where the target is discrete.
This map is not an isomorphism on H0.
The ﬁle
code/EH5_fence_counterexample.py speciﬁes all objects, arrows, and compositions,
checks functoriality and the comma categories, and computes integral homology using
integer Smith normal forms. Its output gives ﬁber and comma Betti numbers (1, 0, . . .) and
pullback Betti numbers (2, 0). Thus the gap precedes the question about necessity of (ii):
the strict-pullback spectral sequence asserted in the prompt is not justiﬁed by its condition
(i).

We call M hypercentral if this ﬁltration exhausts M. The ﬁltration stabilizes by an ordinal
of cardinality at most |M|+, so no proper-class iteration is involved.
E.2 (Proved). An exact characterization of the coeﬃcient systems.
Let B be
a space of CW type, and let M be a local system of abelian groups on B. The following
conditions are equivalent:
1. For every integral homology equivalence u : Y ′ →Y over B, the map H∗(Y ′; u∗M) →
H∗(Y ; M) is an isomorphism.
2. For every component and basepoint b ∈B, the Zπ1(B, b)-module Mb is hypercentral.
If π1(B, b) is ﬁnitely generated, condition (2) is equivalent to the assertion that for every
m ∈Mb there is a ﬁnite r with Irm = 0, where I is the augmentation ideal. A uniform
bound r on all elements is unnecessary. This answers both speciﬁed ﬁrst cases B = S1 and
B = S1 ∨S1.
Proof. The submodules ZαMb deﬁne local subsystems on each component; each successive
quotient has trivial monodromy. An integral homology equivalence induces an isomorphism
with every constant abelian coeﬃcient group, by the integral universal coeﬃcient exact
sequence. The long exact homology sequences of a short exact sequence of local systems show
that the property in (1) is closed under extensions. Singular chains with local coeﬃcients
commute with ﬁltered unions of coeﬃcient modules.
Transﬁnite induction, using this
observation at every limit ordinal, proves (2)⇒(1). Restriction along Y →B does not aﬀect
the argument: the displayed ﬁltration still has trivial successive quotients after restriction.
For the converse, put G = π1(B, b) and let Z∞Mb be the stable value of the ﬁltration. If
m0 /∈Z∞Mb, stability implies that some g1 ∈G has (g1 −1)m0 /∈Z∞Mb. Recursively choose
gi so that
mi = (gi −1)mi−1 /∈Z∞Mb.
(E.2.1)
All mi are nonzero. Let T be a countable rose, with one loop for each gi, mapped to B by
those loops. Put V = T ∨S2. The universal-cover cellular chain group of V relative to T is a
60

free rank-one group-ring module in degree two. For each i, choose a map hi : V →V ﬁxing T,
whose action on this relative module is multiplication by gi −1. Such a map is obtained by
pinching the 2-sphere into two spheres, transporting the ﬁrst around the indicated loop, and
mapping the second with degree −1. Equivalently, π2(T ∨S2) is the free group-ring module
on the attached sphere, so the element gi −1 supplies the speciﬁed attaching map.
The projections V →T and their composites with hi are homotopic relative to T, because
T is aspherical. Choose these homotopies and form the mapping telescope Y = Tel(h1, h2, . . .),
with its resulting map to T and hence to B. The inclusion of the ﬁrst copy of T gives a
map u : T →Y over B. Relative integral homology of the telescope is the direct limit of Z
in degree two under the zero maps, and is therefore zero. Hence u is an integral homology
equivalence.
With the pulled-back system M, however, cellular relative chains give

E.3 (Proved). The homology spectral sequence under the correct hypothesis.
For a functor p : E →B, assume that for every n ≥0, every σ : [n] →B, and every vertex
i ∈[n], the strict-ﬁber inclusion
Eσ(i) −→E ×B [n]
(E.3.1)
is an integral homology equivalence. Then its ﬁber homology groups form local systems Mq on
BB. For every simplicial set K →NB there is a natural strongly convergent ﬁrst-quadrant
spectral sequence
E2
s,q = Hs(|K|; Mq) =⇒Hs+q(|NE ×NB K|; Z).
(E.3.2)
There is also the relative sequence for a map K′ →K over NB, with relative homology
on both sides. The local system Mq is the homology of the homotopy ﬁbers of Bp and is
canonically identiﬁed with b 7→Hq(E/b).
Proof. For a simplex σ : ∆[n] →K, put Pσ = NE ×NB ∆[n]. Its chain complex is a covariant
diagram on the simplex category ∆/K. The hypothesis implies that every map in this
diagram induces a homology isomorphism: compare a vertex of the source simplex with its
61

image vertex in the target simplex, and apply two out of three to (E.3.1). Hence its degree-q
homology is a local system on ∆/K and on |K|.
The augmentation from the homotopy colimit of the Pσ to NE×NBK is a weak equivalence.
One direct veriﬁcation uses the bisimplicial replacement: over a ﬁxed simplex x of the target,
the category of factorizations of its image through simplices of K has the identity factorization
as an initial object. Every augmented row is consequently a contractible resolution of the
indicated simplex. Taking diagonals proves the assertion. The normalized chains of this
replacement form a ﬁrst-quadrant double complex. Filtering by the simplex-category degree
gives (E.3.2); the homology of its horizontal complex is precisely homology with the displayed
local system. Taking mapping cones gives the relative version. There are only ﬁnitely many
terms on each total-degree diagonal, which proves strong convergence without any ﬁnite-type
assumption.
In particular, strict pullback along a weak equivalence of simplicial sets over NB is an
integral homology equivalence, because weak equivalences preserve homology with every local
system. To identify the ﬁbers, replace NB by a Kan complex S through a weak equivalence,
and pull its path ﬁbration ending at b back to NB. The resulting space Cb →NB is weakly
contractible, and NE ×NB Cb models the homotopy ﬁber of Bp. The map from the vertex b
to Cb is a weak equivalence over NB, so the preceding observation identiﬁes the homology
of the strict and homotopy ﬁbers. Pullback along the contractible simplex resolution of
B/b, or equivalently the same spectral sequence over N(B/b), identiﬁes this group with
Hq(E/b). The construction is natural under an arrow b →b′ and gives the stated monodromy
identiﬁcation. Finally compare NE →NB with an ordinary Kan-ﬁbration replacement over
a Kan replacement of NB. On every vertex ﬁber the comparison is the homology isomorphism
just established. Comparing (E.3.2) with the ordinary ﬁbration spectral sequence therefore
proves that the strict-to-derived pullback comparison is a homology equivalence for every base
K →NB. Applying the same comparison to mapping cones proves the relative assertion,
with its speciﬁed maps.
E.4 (Proved). An exact intrinsic description of FibWZ. A functor p : E →B belongs
to FibWZ if and only if (E.3.1) holds and every local system Mq(b) = Hq(Eb; Z) furnished by
E.3 is hypercentral. Thus the characterization uses all ﬁnite-simplex pullbacks and an explicit
transﬁnite module ﬁltration; it does not quantify over integral homology equivalences of bases.
Proof. If p is intrinsic, pull back the weak equivalence [0] i−→[n] over each σ to obtain (E.3.1).
We explain why its intrinsic property then also applies to simplicial homology equivalences
over NB. For K →NB the last-vertex functor ∆/K →B has nerve a simplicial set weakly
equivalent to K over NB. The comparison is the natural last-vertex map. Its strict pullback
along NE →NB is a homology equivalence by E.3. If u : K′ →K is a homology equivalence,
the functor ∆/u belongs to WZ. The intrinsic hypothesis and these two comparisons imply
that its simplicial pullback along NE →NB is a homology equivalence as well.
Suppose that some Mq is not hypercentral. Choose the telescope u : T →Y of E.2 for that
module. It can be represented over NB: choose a simplicial model over a Kan replacement S
of NB, replace its map by a Kan ﬁbration, and pull back along NB →S. Right properness
of the ordinary Kan model gives weak equivalences of these models to the original spaces,
compatibly with u. Consequently the preceding paragraph applies. In the relative spectral
sequence (E.3.2), formula (E.2.2), now for every Mj, puts the entire second page in column
s = 2. There are no possible nonzero diﬀerentials. Therefore
Hq+2(E ×h

(E.4.1)

where the notation denotes the pullback models just speciﬁed; the strict models have the
same homology by E.3. This contradicts preservation of u. Thus every Mq is hypercentral.
This one-column test is the step that prevents spectral-sequence diﬀerentials from concealing
a failure in an individual ﬁber homology group.
Conversely, assume (E.3.1) and hypercentrality. For any functor u : Y ′ →Y over B
in WZ, E.2 makes the maps of second pages in (E.3.2) isomorphisms. Strong convergence
and the comparison theorem give an integral homology equivalence E ×B Y ′ →E ×B Y .
This is exactly the deﬁning condition for FibWZ. The argument includes empty ﬁbers and
disconnected bases; all coeﬃcient modules are then interpreted componentwise, and the zero
module is hypercentral.
E.5 (Proved). The trefoil obstruction R4′.
If BB ≃S1, the basepoint map ∗→B has
no factorization as an integral homology equivalence followed by an intrinsic WZ-ﬁbration.
The same assertion holds for homology with coeﬃcients in any ﬁeld. In particular the trefoil
obstruction R4′ is valid.
Proof. Suppose such a factorization has total space E, so that BE is acyclic and connected.
The ﬁnite-simplex consequence of intrinsic preservation gives the homology spectral sequence
of E.3, also over a ﬁeld. Let k denote Z or a ﬁeld, and put S = π0F, where F is the homotopy
ﬁber. Then H0(F; k) = k[S] is a permutation module for π1B = ⟨t⟩. The edge terms over
the circle give
H0(E; k) = k[S]t,
H1(E; k) ↠k[S]t.
(E.5.1)
The ﬁrst equality makes the action on S transitive. The second makes its invariant submodule
zero. A ﬁnite orbit has the nonzero invariant sum of its basis vectors, over any of these
coeﬃcient rings. Thus S is an inﬁnite orbit, and k[S] ∼= k[t, t−1] with its regular action.
Let T be the trefoil exterior and a : T →S1 its abelianization map. The complement
has integral homology that of a circle, for example by Alexander duality, so a is a homology
equivalence. Its group has the Wirtinger presentation
π1T = ⟨x, y | xyxy−1x−1y−1⟩,
a∗(x) = a∗(y) = t.
For clarity the required Alexander module is computed here, rather than inferred merely from
an Alexander polynomial. The inﬁnite cyclic covering cellular complex through dimension
two has
d1 = (t −1, t −1),
d2 =
 
1 −t + t2
−(1 −t + t2)
!
.
Since t −1 is not a zero divisor, ker d1 is generated by (1, −1). Consequently
H1(T; k[t, t−1]) ∼= k[t, t−1]/(t2 −t + 1) ̸= 0.
(E.5.2)
The two Fox derivatives follow by diﬀerentiating the six letters of the relator; they are
also checked in code/EH5_trefoil.py. The polynomial has nonzero leading and constant
coeﬃcients in every ﬁeld, so it is never a Laurent unit. The standard three-crossing Wirtinger
calculation and its Alexander matrix are independently displayed in EH5 [1, Example 2.35].
Only the group presentation is needed for this H1 calculation; no asphericity theorem for
knot complements is being assumed.
After pulling E →S1 back along a, the term E2
1,0 of its spectral sequence is (E.5.2). No
diﬀerential enters or leaves this term in a ﬁrst-quadrant homology spectral sequence. Thus
the new total space has nonzero H1, whereas E has zero H1. This contradicts intrinsic
preservation of a. Categories of simplices and E.3 supply the same contradiction for strict
categorical pullbacks.
63

There is also a shorter integral check from E.4: the regular module Z[t, t−1] has no nonzero
invariant elements, hence its transﬁnite central ﬁltration is zero. It cannot be one of the ﬁber
modules of an intrinsic WZ-ﬁbration.
E.6 (Proved).
The localization-unit criterion R10(a).
Let W be an accessible
fundamental localizer, and let ηX : X →LX be its localization unit on spaces. Then W is
proper if and only if every homotopy ﬁber of every ηX is W-aspherical.
Proof. For an accessible proper localizer, Cisinski’s Cisinski [36, Theorem 6.1.11] identiﬁes
W with the localizer generated by its aspherical objects. The associated localization is
therefore nulliﬁcation. Its local spaces are closed under dependent sums over a local base:
apply Map(A, −) to such a sum for each nullifying object A, and use Map(A, Z) ≃Z ﬁrst
on the base and then on every ﬁber. Thus the units of the associated modality have W-
aspherical homotopy ﬁbers. Equivalently, in the right proper localized simplicial model, pull a
localization unit back along the path ﬁbration of its local target. That is a localized ﬁbration,
because it is an ordinary ﬁbration between local spaces. Right properness identiﬁes its total
space, the unit ﬁber, with a point in W.
Conversely let P be the localization generated by all W-aspherical objects. It exists and is
accessible by Cisinski [36, Proposition 6.1.8]. Suppose every unit ﬁber is W-aspherical, hence
P-aspherical. For a P-local space Z, write the domain of a unit as the homotopy colimit of
its ﬁber diagram over its full target space. Then
Map(X, Z) ≃holim
y∈LX Map(ﬁby ηX, Z) ≃holim
y∈LX Z ≃Map(LX, Z).
(E.6.1)
The middle equivalence is natural in paths and higher coherences of LX; it is not a product
over the set of points. Thus every unit is a P-equivalence. Naturality of units and two out of
three imply W ⊆P, while P ⊆W by deﬁnition. Hence W = P, and Theorem 6.1.11 gives
properness. This checks the entire localization-ﬁber assertion used in R10(a).

Proof. We ﬁrst prove the size assertion which makes this criterion bounded.
Suppose
K →V
p−→B is a homotopy factorization of the required kind. Replace p by an ordinary Kan
ﬁbration and represent the ﬁrst map strictly over B. The Kan-ﬁbration version of E.4, with
the same Serre spectral sequence and telescope proof, says that every Hq(Vb; Z) is hypercentral.
Start with the simplicial subset V0 ⊂V generated by the image of K. Inductively construct
Vn ⊂Vn+1 by making these three choices in V :
1. For every horn in Vn over a simplex of B, include the simplices of one ﬁller supplied by
p.
2. For every integral simplicial cycle in Vn which bounds in V , include the ﬁnite support
of one chain bounding it.
3. For every vertex b of B and every integral simplicial cycle in (Vn)b which bounds in Vb,
include the ﬁnite support of one chain bounding it inside Vb.
Take the simplicial closure of all these choices at each stage. There are at most λ choices:
horns have ﬁnitely many nondegenerate simplices, integral chains have ﬁnite support and
integer coeﬃcients, there are countably many degrees, and |B0| ≤λ. Consequently |Vn| ≤λ
for every n.

(E.8.6)
Indeed a cycle is supported at one ﬁnite stage, and a boundary in the indicated ambient

every class of H∗(V ) comes from H∗(K), and K →V already factors through V∞. Thus
K →V∞is an integral homology equivalence. The second injection is a map of monodromy

homology module of V∞is hypercentral. No surjectivity on ﬁber homology is needed. We
have produced a member of the catalogue through which the desired factorization passes.
For completeness, an upper central ﬁltration on a set of at most λ elements cannot have
a strict successor increase at every stage below λ+. At its ﬁrst stationary stage it remains
stationary. Hence hypercentrality is exactly (E.8.1). Labeling the simplices of a set of size at

E.8.1 (Proved). The boundary cases for the factorization problem.
For connected
CW spaces the following assertions hold.
Every map to a connected space Y admits a
factorization as an integral homology equivalence followed by a homotopy map with the
intrinsic preservation property if and only if H1(Y ; Z) = 0. In particular the basepoint map
∗→Y admits such a factorization if and only if H1Y = 0. If both X and Y are HZ-local,
then f : X →Y admits such a factorization if and only if its homotopy ﬁbers have hypercentral
homology as π1Y -modules. For any factorable map, H1f is surjective. The converse fails:
the retraction S1 ∨S1 →S1 is not factorable.
Proof. A hypercentral integral permutation module Z[S] has trivial action on S. Indeed
restrict to the cyclic subgroup generated by any g. Hypercentrality restricts, and E.2 says
that (g −1) acts locally nilpotently. If gs ̸= s, the elements (g −1)n[s] are nonzero for every
n: for an inﬁnite orbit this follows by their distinct extreme basis vectors, and for a ﬁnite
orbit by tensoring with C and diagonalizing its cyclic permutation, which has a nontrivial
root-of-unity eigenvalue. Thus gs = s for every g, s.
For a connected total space E over connected Y , the action on π0F is transitive, with
stabilizer the image of π1E →π1Y .
E.4 therefore makes the ﬁber connected and this
fundamental-group map surjective. In a factorization X
u−→E →Y with u a homology
equivalence, H1u is an isomorphism, proving surjectivity of H1f. The basepoint case gives
the necessary condition H1Y = 0.
Suppose conversely that H1Y = 0. Attach 2-cells to Y along a set of generating loops,
obtaining a simply connected space Y2. The relative group H2(Y2, Y ) is free on those cells.
Since H1Y = 0, the map H2Y2 →H2(Y2, Y ) is onto. By Hurewicz choose based 2-spheres
representing lifts of its basis, and attach 3-cells along them. The resulting j : Y →Y + has
simply connected target, and its relative cellular complex is the identity map between the
free groups in degrees three and two. Hence j is an integral homology equivalence. Every
simply connected space is HZ-local: the groups K(A, n) are local by the universal coeﬃcient
theorem, the Postnikov tower of a simply connected space is built from them by homotopy
ﬁbers, and local spaces are closed under homotopy limits. Thus j is an HZ-localization unit.
Its homotopy ﬁbers are acyclic. To verify this directly, use the Serre spectral sequence over
the simply connected base Y +. If q > 0 were the least degree with nonzero reduced ﬁber
homology, then the term E2
0,q could only be hit by a transgression from the base row. Every
base-row class survives because H∗Y →H∗Y + is onto in every degree. Thus E2
0,q would give
a nonzero kernel of HqY →HqY +, a contradiction. The ﬁber is connected by the homotopy
exact sequence.
For any f : X →Y , form the canonical homotopy pullback
E = Y ×h
Y + LHZX,
(E.8.8)
68

using the localized map LHZX →Y +. The projection E →LHZX has the acyclic ﬁbers just
proved, so is a homology equivalence by the Serre spectral sequence. The canonical X →E
is consequently a homology equivalence by two out of three. The other projection E →Y is
a pullback of a ﬁbration over the simply connected space Y +. Its ﬁber homology has trivial
monodromy, and E.4 gives the intrinsic preservation property. This also handles disconnected
X, componentwise; an empty source uses the empty total space.
Now assume that both endpoints of f are HZ-local, and let X
u−→E
p−→Y be a requested
factorization. The restriction map
MapY (E, X) −→MapY (X, X),
where these are derived mapping spaces in the slice over Y , is an equivalence. It is the map of
homotopy ﬁbers of the two restriction maps with targets X and Y , both equivalences because
these targets are local and u is a homology equivalence. Thus the identity of X extends to
r : E →X over Y , with ru ≃idX over Y . The map f is a homotopy retract of p in the slice.
Preservation of homology equivalences under homotopy base change is closed under such
retracts, so E.4 applies to f. The converse is its ordinary ﬁbration replacement.
Finally suppose the projection X = S1 ∨S1 →S1 had a factorization, with ﬁber F
and total space E. The ﬁber is connected by the ﬁrst paragraph. Over the circle the Serre
spectral sequence has only columns zero and one, so H2E = 0 implies H1(F)t = 0. A
nonzero hypercentral module has a nonzero invariant element, by taking the ﬁrst nonzero
stage of its central ﬁltration. Hence H1F = 0. The degree-one sequence then gives H1E ∼= Z,
contradicting H1E ∼= H1X ∼= Z2.

The target L1Z is KU-local since every KU-equivalence is a KU(p)-equivalence. The source
and target are p-local nilpotent spaces. For a prime q ̸= p they are mod-q acyclic: their
reduced homology groups are Z(p)-modules, so multiplication by q is invertible. Lemma
10.6 of Bousﬁeld [20] then makes them KU/q-acyclic. Their map is a rational and a KU/p
equivalence because it is a KU(p)-equivalence. The arithmetic detection statement in Bousﬁeld
[20, Theorem 10.1] makes it a KU-equivalence. Thus (E.9.5) computes LKUZ.

loop criterion identiﬁes W lc

the speciﬁed unit after looping as
ΩX −→ΩLΣfX ≃LfΩX.
(E.9.6)
Here ΩX ≃Z. The map induces an isomorphism on components and, by (E.9.5), both loop

simply connected. The homotopy exact sequence, using π2ΩX = 0, now gives
π2F = π1ΩF ∼= Cp∞.
(E.9.7)
The space K(Cp∞, 1) is L1-local by Bousﬁeld [22, Theorem 7.4]: for a connected Postnikov
H-space that theorem preserves the p-localized homotopy group in degree one, removes
torsion only in degree two, and rationalizes degrees above two. It is consequently KU-local as
well. The nonzero identity homomorphism π1ΩF →Cp∞represents a nonconstant component
of Map∗(ΩF, K(Cp∞, 1)). Thus ΩF is not KU-acyclic. The loop criterion says exactly that
F is not W lc
KU-aspherical. Applying E.6 proves nonproperness.
All spaces in (E.9.1)–(E.9.7) admit small simplicial models. Passing to their categories of
simplices therefore supplies the same localization-unit obstruction for fundamental localizers
on Cat; the conclusion does not rest on a ﬁnite-dimensional or ﬁnite-type approximation.
6
The literal axioms: absolute localization is weaker
F.1 (Refuted). Absolute Theorem A does not imply relative Theorem A, even
with Loc (8) and (9). There is a strongly saturated class W † of small-category functors
satisfying XVI Loc (2), (3), (8), and (9), but satisfying neither Loc (3 bis) nor Loc (6′ a).
A ﬁnite counterexample to relative Theorem A has a three-object indexing poset and a map
from a 77-object poset to a 53-object poset. For a base with a terminal object, in particular
[1], relative Theorem A is tautological.
70

Proof. For an abelian group A, write Tor(A) for its torsion subgroup. Deﬁne W † to consist of
the functors u : X →Y satisfying the following four requirements: Π1(Bu) is an equivalence
of fundamental groupoids; Hn(Bu; Z) is an isomorphism for every n ≥3; H2(Bu; Q) is an
isomorphism; and Tor H2(Bu; Z) is an isomorphism. No local coeﬃcient systems occur in
this deﬁnition.
Each requirement is the inverse image of a class of isomorphisms, or of equivalences of
groupoids. Consequently W † satisﬁes two out of three, the rule involving uv and vu, and
retract closure. It is strongly saturated as well: each of the indicated functors factors through
Cat[W †−1], so any arrow invertible there passes every test. It contains W∞.
We ﬁrst identify its aspherical objects. If X →e belongs to W †, then BX is nonempty,
connected, and simply connected. Its homology vanishes above degree two. The group
A = H2(BX; Z) has A ⊗Q = 0 and Tor(A) = 0. The ﬁrst equality says that every element
of A is torsion, and the second therefore implies A = 0. Thus BX is simply connected and
acyclic. A ﬁrst nonzero homotopy group would contradict the Hurewicz theorem, and hence
BX is weakly contractible. The converse is immediate. In particular a category with a
terminal object is W †-aspherical. If u : X →Y has all comma categories X/y W †-aspherical,
they are weakly contractible, so Quillen’s Theorem A puts u in W∞⊆W †. This is exactly
the absolute Loc (3), with the required variance. The theorem used is [71, §1, Theorem
A]: contractibility of all the indicated comma-category classifying spaces implies that the
speciﬁed map BX →BY is a homotopy equivalence. Its opposite-category form gives the
same assertion for the other comma convention.
Here are completely speciﬁed ﬁnite witnesses. Let Cm be the polygonal simplicial circle
with vertices 0, . . . , m−1 and edges {i, i+1}, indices modulo m, and let Lm = Cm ∗{am, bm},
where the last two vertices span no edge. This is the simplicial suspension of a polygon, and
its realization is S2. The simplicial map
h : L8 −→L4,
i 7−→i mod 4,
a8 7−→a4,
b8 7−→b4
has degree two. Indeed, the oriented integral fundamental chain is

h∗z8 = 2z4.
Let P and Q be the posets of nonempty faces of L8 and L4, ordered by inclusion, and let
f : P →Q send a face to its image under h. Their nerves are the barycentric subdivisions
of the displayed spheres; P has 50 elements and Q has 26. The functor f belongs to W †,
since its only nonzero positive homology map is multiplication by two on H2 = Z, which is a
rational isomorphism, and both torsion groups are zero.
