For completeness we verify both extra size axioms. Nerves commute with ﬁltered colimits,
since an n-simplex is a ﬁnite composable string of arrows. Fundamental groupoids commute
with ﬁltered colimits: paths and the elementary relations between paths are ﬁnite data.
Integral and rational homology commute with ﬁltered colimits of simplicial sets, by exactness
of ﬁltered colimits of abelian groups. Finally, Tor commutes with ﬁltered colimits. If a
representative a is torsion in the colimit, some equation na = 0, n ≥1, already holds at
a later stage, and equality of two torsion representatives is likewise witnessed at a later
stage. Thus every test deﬁning W † commutes with ﬁltered colimits, and ﬁltered colimits of
equivalences of groupoids are equivalences. This proves Loc (8).
We give the bounded-subcategory argument for Loc (9), rather than treating it as a formal
consequence of the word “accessible”. Fix u : X →Y in W † and countable subcategories
X0 ⊆X, Y0 ⊆Y with uX0 ⊆Y0. A countable enlargement can supply all witnesses to
the following countable collection of requests: paths witnessing essential surjectivity of the
fundamental groupoid; lifts of its arrows and witnesses to their equality; ﬁllings of integral
cycles in degrees at least three; rational cycle lifts and ﬁllings in degree two; and lifts and
ﬁllings of torsion classes in degree two. Every request is a ﬁnite chain, a ﬁnite zigzag, or
a ﬁnite homotopy of zigzags, together with an integer denominator or a torsion equation.
Because u passes the relevant global test, the requested witness exists in X or Y and uses
only ﬁnitely many arrows. Adjoin all these arrows and close under identities and composition;
the enlargement remains countable. Iterate the construction countably many times. Every
ﬁnite request in the union occurs at a ﬁnite stage and is answered at a later stage, so the
restricted union functor belongs to W †. In the torsion-surjectivity request we also adjoin a
ﬁnite chain witnessing that the chosen source class is torsion; this point prevents replacement
of torsion by rational homology.
The countable W †-subarrows of u therefore form a ﬁltered coﬁnal family of countable
subarrows, and their colimit is u. There is a small set of isomorphism representatives of
all functors between countable categories. Consequently W † is the ﬁltered-colimit envelope
of its members in this set. Together with Loc (8), this is precisely XVI Proposition 5.3.1
and Corollary 5.3.2’s formulation of Loc (9), with the countable bound. In fact the same
saturation of a countable family gives an ℵ1-ﬁltered presentation.
Finally, if the base S has terminal object s, then S/s ∼= S over S. Hence X/s ∼= X,
Y/s ∼= Y , and v/s ∼= v. The hypothesis at s already asserts the conclusion. The suggested
base [1] cannot witness failure.
72

F.1.1 (Proved). Forward gluing and ﬁltered colimits recover relative Theorem A.
Let W satisfy the literal XVI Loc (1), (2), and (3). If W also satisﬁes Loc (6′ a) and Loc (8),
then it is a fundamental localizer: in particular it satisﬁes relative Theorem A, Loc (3 bis).
Loc (9) is unnecessary for this implication. Thus the assertion discussed in XVI manuscript
page 118, footnote 56, holds under weaker size hypotheses than those stated there.
Proof. By Cisinski [37, Theorem 6.1.18], W contains W∞. We verify the simplicial-localizer
axioms directly, thereby avoiding an implicit use of relative Theorem A in a homotopy-colimit
argument.
Write i∆X = ∆/X for the category of simplices of a simplicial set X, and set
V = {f : X −→Y : i∆f ∈W}.
The class V has two out of three and contains all weak homotopy equivalences, because
the natural comparison B(∆/X) ≃|X| identiﬁes the weak homotopy type of a category of
simplices. In particular V contains all maps having the right lifting property against all
monomorphisms.
We next prove that a monomorphism in V remains in V after every pushout. Let X ,→Y
be such a monomorphism, let X →Z be arbitrary, and put T = Y ⨿X Z. Apply the
double-collage construction to the span of categories
B
f←−A
g−→C,
A = ∆/X,
B = ∆/Y,
C = ∆/Z.
Thus Mf has the disjoint A and B layers, with arrows a →b equal to B(fa, b) and no
arrows in the other direction; deﬁne Mg in the same way. Their union P = Mf ⨿A Mg has
no arrows between the B and C layers. The two pieces are full sieves covering P. The
retractions Mf →B and Mg →C are left adjoints and hence belong to W∞. Since f ∈W,
two out of three gives A →Mf ∈W. Loc (6′ a) now gives Mg →P ∈W, and consequently
C →P ∈W.
Every chain in P lies in one of its two sieve pieces, so
NP = NMf ⨿NA NMg.
This is a homotopy pushout after realization: both attaching maps are inclusions of simplicial
subsets. The collage retractions identify its homotopy type with the homotopy pushout of
BB ←BA →BC. The compatible functors from the three layers to ∆/T give a canonical
functor P →∆/T. It is a weak homotopy equivalence, since the original simplicial pushout
Y ⨿X Z is a homotopy pushout when X →Y is a monomorphism. Composing C →P with
this weak equivalence proves ∆/Z →∆/T ∈W, as required.
The functor i∆commutes with ﬁltered colimits. Its objects are simplices and its arrows
are simplicial operators satisfying one equality, so both objects and arrows, and their deﬁning
relations, are ﬁnite data in such a colimit. Loc (8) therefore makes V closed under ﬁltered
colimits of arrows. It follows that its monomorphisms are closed under every small transﬁnite
composition. More explicitly, at a successor stage use composition and at a limit ordinal λ
apply ﬁltered-colimit closure to the arrows X0 →Xβ, β < λ. Transﬁnite induction supplies
all these arrows before the limit step.
We have checked exactly the three axioms of a ∆-localizer in Cisinski [37, Deﬁnition 1.4.1]:
two out of three, inclusion of trivial ﬁbrations, and closure of the monomorphisms in the class
under pushouts and transﬁnite compositions. Every ∆-localizer is regular by that source’s
Proposition 3.4.25. All its representables are weakly contractible, so it is a test localizer.
Theorem 4.2.15 and Remark 4.2.16 therefore associate to V the fundamental localizer N−1V
on categories. These results do not assume accessibility.
73

Finally N−1V = W. For every category A the natural last-vertex functor ∆/NA →A is
a weak homotopy equivalence. Its naturality square for u : A →B, together with W∞⊆W
and two out of three, gives
u ∈W
⇐⇒
i∆Nu ∈W
⇐⇒
Nu ∈V.
Hence W itself is the fundamental localizer just obtained. This proves relative Theorem A
without any invocation of Loc (9), a model structure for W, or an unproved gluing theorem
for W-equivalences.
F.2 (Proved). The reverse single-square axiom alone forces integral homology
equivalences.
Let W satisfy the literal XVI Loc (1), (2), and (3). If it satisﬁes Loc (6′
b), then WZ ⊆W. In particular the same conclusion holds under the cube axiom Loc (6 b),
including for nonconnected sources. Neither relative Theorem A nor forward gluing is needed.
Proof. Cisinski’s Theorem 6.1.18 [37] says exactly that the weak homotopy equivalences
form the least class satisfying the weak-fundamental-localizer axioms, namely the indicated
saturation, terminal-object, and absolute-Theorem-A axioms.
Thus W∞⊆W under
the present assumptions. The proof of that theorem uses its Proposition 6.1.16 and the
contractibility of horns; it does not insert relative Theorem A into the hypotheses.
For a functor g : A →B use the collage Mg = A ⨿B with mixed arrows a →b given by
B(g(a), b) and with no arrows from B to A. Its retraction r : Mg →B is left adjoint to the
inclusion of B, and ri = g for the inclusion i : A →Mg. Adjoin a point t receiving one arrow
from every object of A, with no arrows between B and t, and denote the result by K(g). The
full subcategories Mg and A▷= A ∪{t} are sieves covering K(g), with intersection A. On
nerves their union is literal: every chain lies in the sieve containing its ﬁnal object. Their
realizations form a homotopy-pushout square, since the two inclusions of the intersection are
CW-subcomplex inclusions. The speciﬁed retraction BMg →BB and contraction BA▷→∗
identify BK(g) with the homotopy coﬁbre C(Bg). This checks the part of POLES B.3 used
here without invoking its additional assertion about W-maps of coﬁbres.
Suppose K(g) →e is in W. Then A▷→K(g) is in W by two out of three, because
the cone category has a terminal object. Applying Loc (6′ b) to the displayed sieve cover
gives A →Mg in W, and composition with r ∈W∞gives g ∈W. We have proved only the
implication we need:
K(g) is W-aspherical
=⇒
g ∈W.
Now let g be an integral homology isomorphism. If A is empty, H0(BB; Z) = 0 forces
B empty too, so there is nothing to prove. Otherwise H0(Bg) being invertible implies
that the actual function π0(BA) →π0(BB) is bijective: it induces the stated isomorphism
between free abelian groups on those sets. This argument applies to arbitrary inﬁnite sets of
components. The coﬁbre C = BK(g) is connected, because every component of BB meets
BA and hence is joined to the cone point. The augmented mapping-cone homology sequence
gives eH∗(C; Z) = 0. The unreduced suspension of this connected CW complex is simply
connected by van Kampen and acyclic by the suspension homology sequence, hence weakly
contractible by Hurewicz. It follows that K(K(g) →e) →e is in W∞. Apply the one-way
criterion ﬁrst to K(g) →e and then to g. This proves the assertion using Loc (6′ b) alone.
The manuscript’s degenerate cube proves Loc (6 b)⇒Loc (6′ b), so this also checks
R8. Alternatively its two-cone argument can be made insensitive to disconnectedness as
follows. For a nonempty homology equivalence Bg, the double suspension Σ2Bg is a homology
equivalence between simply connected CW complexes, hence is a weak equivalence. Apply
the reverse cube to the map of two-cone covers of ΣBg, and then to the covers of Bg. At
74

each application the two cone maps have contractible source and target and so are in W∞;
the union map has already been shown to be in W. These two applications give g ∈W.
This avoids silently applying the simply connected Whitehead theorem to a suspension of a
disconnected space.
6.1
Strict higher dimensions and the audit of 317
We write Mn for the structure asserted in 317, Ln = cnSd2, Rn = Ex2Nn, and BnX = |NnX|.
Here n = ∞means strict globular ω-categories. The symbols Wn in this subsection mean the
weak equivalences of Mn; they do not mean the truncation localizers also denoted by Wn in
the historical discussion.
F.3a (Proved). Audit of the higher Thomason transfer in 317.
The arguments
in the supplied source of 317 establish the proper combinatorial structure and the Quillen
equivalence Ln ⊣Rn that are used below. The following audit records the interfaces on which
the transfer depends. It concerns the supplied mathematical proof; no Lean ﬁle was supplied
or checked.
Proof. The strict reﬂection τ≤n identiﬁes parallel n-cells which bound (n + 1)-cells and then
imposes the resulting congruence. This is the left adjoint to inclusion of strict n-categories
in strict ω-categories. In particular it is a reﬂection, not the operation of forgetting cells
above dimension n. Section 2 of 317 applies the directed-chain construction to strong Steiner
complexes. The exact external results needed here are Steiner’s Theorem 5.11, giving the
equivalence on complexes with loop-free unital bases, and Theorem 6.1, giving the atomic
presentation, together with Ara–Maltsiniotis Theorem A.15 and A.16: the Gray tensor
extends the tensor on strong Steiner complexes, preserves all small colimits in each variable,
and has the two internal homs. These statements were opened in [2, 80]. The same appendix,
Proposition A.29, says that an internal hom into an n-category is an n-category. Thus the
reﬂected cylinder bijection used in 317 has the required codomain in every ﬁnite dimension;
it is not an assumption that nCat is closed under the unreﬂected Gray tensor.
For completeness we checked the two chain calculations that turn this machinery into
relative homotopies. The positive Alexander–Whitney diagonal on an ordered simplex is
natural for all order maps, including degeneracies; composing with a map to the interval gives
the simplicial homotopy induced by a right Gray cylinder. For the relative prism, if f ≤g
agree on a sieve A ⊂E and g is constant outside A, the cross term on a basis simplex is
h[w0, . . . , wp] = [f(w0), . . . , f(wp), g(wp)].
Repeated entries are interpreted as zero. On the part lying in A this is zero in positive degree
and the interval factor is sent through its augmentation. On the remaining part, canceling the
alternating faces gives dh = hd + (−1)p(G −F), with F, G the endpoint chain maps. This is
exactly the signed tensor diﬀerential. The remaining penultimate-face terms coincide because
g is constant on the new part and A is a sieve. Consequently the cylinder is stationary on the
entire higher subcategory Un(A), as required for gluing along an arbitrary attaching functor.
This checks Section 3, including its stationarity condition, rather than only the eﬀect on
vertices.
In Section 4 the slice functors taking the zero and one ﬁbres of an n-category over [1]
preserve colimits: their right adjoints adjoin respectively a terminal object over one and an
initial object over zero. The object functor likewise preserves colimits, using the chaotic
category as its right adjoint. The initial and ﬁnal vertex segments of an oriental are full; this
follows from the endpoints of its atomic generators and the retractions clamping vertices to
the segment. These facts compute the labelled pieces of a pushout even when its attaching
75

functor identiﬁes objects. For a cover by full sieves, an oriental simplex lies in the member
containing its last vertex, since every vertex has an arrow to that vertex. The dual statement
uses the ﬁrst vertex for cosieves. Thus Street nerves of these covers are actual simplicial
unions along monomorphisms. This veriﬁes the gluing hypothesis used in the next two
sections.
For Section 5, let A ⊂E be a sieve with right adjoint r : E →A. If E −A has greatest
element x, the maps equal to the identity on A and constant respectively at x and r(x)
on the new part give the relative zigzag idE ≤g ≥k. The prism just checked remains
stationary after pushing out against any Un(A) →X, and gives the retraction homotopy
proving couniversality. For a ﬁnite new part, deleting two distinct maximal new elements gives
the two full pieces needed for induction and simplicial gluing. For an inﬁnite new part, ﬁnite
subsets closed under ir form a ﬁltered family; the attached category X is retained in every
member. Nerves preserve these ﬁltered colimits because each oriental is ﬁnitely presented.
The same induction, starting with a greatest element, proves the unit NP →NncnNP for
every poset P.
Section 6 applies this to the ﬂag posets of subdivided boundaries and horns. Their
inclusions are sieves, and on the cosieve generated by an old ﬂag the largest old subﬂag is the
stated right adjoint. First attaching this cosieve gives the couniversal weak equivalence. The
remaining pieces form full cosieve covers, so their nerves give the monomorphic gluing square.
It follows both that a pushed-out subdivided horn is a weak equivalence and that a boundary
attachment preserves a weak equivalence between the objects to which it is attached. These
are the two diﬀerent assertions used for transfer and for left properness.
The presentability appendix treats strict categories as models of a ﬁnitary essentially
algebraic theory. Its presentation construction simultaneously imposes equations and creates
compositions whenever the equations make their boundaries match. Thus it avoids the
incorrect procedure of merely quotienting a previously constructed free category. All formation
rules have ﬁnitely many premises, also in the countable-sorted ω-case. Finite presentations
therefore give a small dense set of ﬁnitely presentable objects, and ﬁltered colimits are
computed cellwise. This supplies the smallness and ﬁltered-colimit assertions used in Section 7.
Finally LnI-injectives and LnJ-injectives are respectively R−1
n (TrivFib) and R−1
n (Fib)
by adjunction. Relative LnJ-cell maps are weak equivalences by the horn assertion and
ﬁltered-colimit closure. The small-object factorizations and the usual arrow-retract argument
consequently give both weak factorization systems. Right properness follows because Rn
preserves pullbacks and detects weak equivalences, and simplicial sets are right proper. Left
properness follows by the boundary-attachment assertion, transﬁnite composition and retracts.
The unit after Sd2 is proved by boundary induction, using the already checked poset unit on
each cell; composing with the subdivision comparison gives the derived adjunction unit. Since
Rn detects weak equivalences, this proves the Quillen equivalence. These are the contents of
317, Sections 2–7 and Appendix A, used here. At n = 1 the model structure and properness
also agree with Thomason’s Theorem 4.9 and Corollary 5.5 [83].
F.3b (Proved). The correspondence for set-generated localizations. Restriction
along Cat ⊂nCat gives an inclusion-preserving bijection between the weak-equivalence classes
of left Bousﬁeld localizations of Mn at small sets of maps and the accessible fundamental
localizers of Cat. For an accessible fundamental localizer W, the inverse is
W (n) = {f : i∆Nn(f) ∈W},
(F.3.1)
where i∆K = ∆/K is the category of simplices. Equivalently Bn(f) belongs to the spatial
localization corresponding to W. The same assertion holds if “at small sets” is replaced by
“combinatorial”.
76

Proof. We use two precise existence statements.
Cisinski’s Theorem 4.2.15 identiﬁes
fundamental localizers modelled by a test category with its test localizers and preserves
accessibility. For the test category ∆, its inverse is i−1
∆; the ordinary nerve gives the forward
class on Cat, by Remark 4.2.16 and the test-functor statement used there. Theorem 1.4.3
identiﬁes accessible simplicial localizers with the coﬁbrantly generated model structures
having monomorphisms as coﬁbrations. Thus accessible fundamental localizers correspond
exactly to set-generated localizations of spaces. We use this speciﬁc form of [38].
Barwick’s Theorem 4.7 states that a left proper combinatorial model category localized
at a small set of homotopy classes has a left proper combinatorial model structure, with the
original coﬁbrations, the ﬁbrant local objects as ﬁbrant objects, and the local equivalences as
weak equivalences [10]. The theorem uses derived mapping spaces and does not require the
original model category to be simplicial. It applies to Mn by F.3a.
Let S be a small set of simplicial maps generating the spatial localization associated to W.
Every simplicial set is coﬁbrant, so the derived images of its maps under Ln are represented
by the actual maps LnS. For a ﬁbrant Z in Mn, the derived mapping adjunction gives
Maph
Mn(LnK, Z) ≃Maph
sSet(K, RnZ).
It follows, for each map in S and each such Z, that Z is LnS-local exactly when RnZ is
S-local. The Quillen equivalence in F.3a is essentially surjective on ﬁbrant homotopy types.
Thus testing a map of Mn against every local object is exactly testing its image in spaces
against every S-local object. This proves formula (F.3.1) and its existence.
Conversely, a set of maps in Mn represents a small set of maps in spaces under the
same equivalence. The mapping-space argument in the opposite direction proves that its
local-equivalence class is the inverse image of that spatial class. On an ordinary category,
Nn is exactly the ordinary nerve. The natural last-vertex comparison i∆NA →A is a weak
equivalence; its compatibility with functors shows that the restriction in (F.3.1) is exactly
W. In particular no choices of replacements change this class. This proves surjectivity and
injectivity.
For the last assertion, a combinatorial localization has a small set J′ of generating trivial
coﬁbrations. Its ﬁbrant objects are precisely the original ﬁbrant objects local with respect to J′:
one direction follows from the lifting property, and the other from the characterization of local
equivalences by derived mapping spaces and the two weak factorization systems. Equivalently,
apply the standard generating-trivial-coﬁbration proof of [60, Proposition A.3.7.4], after
replacing the derived mapping spaces by cosimplicial resolutions. This proof uses no simplicial
enrichment of Mn. Hence this localization has the same weak equivalences as localization at
J′.
F.3c (Refuted as an unrestricted ZFC assertion).
A nonaccessible model
localization.
Suppose the indexing universe U contains no measurable cardinal.
In
every strict dimension 1 ≤n ≤∞, there is a left Bousﬁeld localization of Mn whose
weak-equivalence class restricts to a nonaccessible fundamental localizer of Cat. Thus the
unrestricted wording in Part F.3, which does not require the localized model structure to
be combinatorial, has a counterexample in this ZFC-compatible setting. Under Vopěnka’s
principle for the relevant U-classes, the unrestricted correspondence is a bijection. The
unconditional size-correct correspondence is F.3b.
Proof. Put Aλ = Zλ/Z<λ for inﬁnite U-small cardinals λ, where Z<λ consists of the vectors
whose support has cardinality less than λ, and let A be their class. A group is reduced
when it admits no nontrivial homomorphism from any Aλ. Reduced groups are closed under
subgroups, products and extensions. For the extension assertion, a map Aλ →G into an
77

extension of two reduced groups ﬁrst has trivial image in its quotient and then trivial image
in its kernel.
For a U-small group G let
R(G) =
\
{ker(a) : a : G →H, H reduced},
PG = G/R(G).
This construction is small: it suﬃces to use quotient images of G, and their normal kernels
form a subset of its power set. The diagonal embedding into the product of these reduced
quotients shows that PG is reduced. Every map from G to a reduced group factors uniquely
through PG, giving a functorial reﬂector. It applies to groupoids by quotienting each isotropy
group by its characteristic radical; conjugation carries these radicals to one another, so the
arrow torsors and composition descend.
We need the following additional fact to produce an actual model structure, rather than
merely a homotopy reﬂector:
R(R(G)) = R(G).
(F.3.3)
Indeed R(R(G)) is characteristic in the normal subgroup R(G), and hence normal in G. The
quotient G/R(R(G)) is an extension of the reduced groups P(R(G)) and PG, so is reduced.
The universal property of R(G) forces R(G) ⊆R(R(G)). The reverse inclusion is immediate.
Thus P(R(G)) = 1.
Let Π(n)
1 X be the fundamental groupoid obtained by reﬂecting a strict n-category to an
ordinary category and inverting all arrows, and regard
QnX = PΠ(n)
1 X
as a strict n-category concentrated in invertible dimensions zero and one. There is a natural
strict functor X →QnX. The zero, one and two simplices of the Street nerve give exactly
the same presentation of the fundamental groupoid: its edges are the 1-cells, and a triangular
2-simplex imposes the relation between its two composite boundary paths. Consequently
Π1(BnX) ≃Π(n)
1 X,
BnQnX ≃NPΠ1(BnX).
The functor Qn preserves Wn, and its unit is idempotent up to the canonical isomorphism.
Its local homotopy types are precisely the 1-types with reduced fundamental groups.
We apply the following exact form of the Bousﬁeld–Friedlander criterion, Stanculescu’s
Theorem 1.1 [78]. For a model category and a coaugmented endofunctor Q, assume that
Q preserves weak equivalences, that QηX is a weak equivalence and ηQX is monic in the
homotopy category, and that Q-equivalences are preserved by pullback along original ﬁbrations
between original ﬁbrant Q-local objects. Then there is a left Bousﬁeld model localization with
the original coﬁbrations and the Q-equivalences as weak equivalences. No small generating
family is assumed. We opened the corrected primary version, including its factorization proof.
The ﬁrst two hypotheses were just veriﬁed. For the third, an original ﬁbration between
ﬁbrant objects has strict pullbacks which Rn = Ex2Nn identiﬁes with homotopy pullbacks
of spaces. It therefore suﬃces to check the asserted property for a homotopy pullback of a
Q-equivalence X →B along E →B, where E, B are local 1-types. Work on one component
of E. The map on fundamental groups g : G →H induced by X →B is a surjection
with kernel R(G): since H is reduced it factors as G →PG ∼
−→H. Surjectivity makes the
homotopy pullback connected over this component. Since π2B = 0, its fundamental group is
G ×H K −→K,
K = π1E.
This projection is surjective with kernel R(G). Every map from this pullback group to a
reduced group kills its kernel, by (F.3.3), and hence factors uniquely through K. Since K
78

is reduced, applying P to the projection is an isomorphism. The same argument on each
component proves the required Q-equivalence. The criterion now constructs the promised
model localization of Mn. In particular its existence does not rely on an unproved transfer
of a proper-class reﬂector to a model structure.
It remains to verify that the restriction WQ to Cat is a fundamental localizer and is
nonaccessible. Mapping into all reduced groupoids detects PΠ1-equivalences, by the reﬂector
universal property and Yoneda for groupoids. These are exactly the derived mapping-space
tests against the local 1-types. For any ﬁxed target space such tests deﬁne a fundamental
localizer: the two elementary axioms are immediate, and relative Theorem A follows from
BA ≃hocolims∈S B(A/s) and the corresponding formula for B, since mapping into the ﬁxed
target turns this homotopy colimit into a homotopy limit. Intersecting these classes over the
local targets proves that WQ is fundamental.
Proposition 6.2 of Casacuberta–Scevenels–Smith states that the absence of measurable
cardinals is equivalent to Z being A-reduced [30]. The statement relativizes to U: a nonzero
map Aλ →Z would produce a measurable cardinal at most λ. Therefore every WQ-map
induces isomorphisms on integral cohomology in degrees zero and one. Degree zero follows
from the bijection of components; degree one follows, on each component, from Hom(PG, Z) =
Hom(G, Z).
Suppose a U-small set S generated WQ. Choose an uncountable regular κ ∈U larger than
the total number of simplices in the sources and targets of NS. The functor Tκ(B) = Bκ/B<κ
on abelian groups is exact: both products and the union of the bounded-support subproducts
preserve short exact sequences, and the quotient does so by the snake lemma. If a free chain
complex C∗has fewer than κ basis elements in total, the interchange of its two coordinate
sets gives
Hom(C∗, Aκ) ∼= Tκ Hom(C∗, Z).
For the kernel assertion here, the union of the supports of fewer than κ basis values still has
size less than κ; this is exactly where regularity is used. Thus Hj(C∗; Aκ) ∼= TκHj(C∗; Z).
Applying this in degrees zero and one to NS shows that Z = K(Aκ, 1) is S-local. Indeed its
mapping spaces have components H1(−; Aκ), fundamental groups H0(−; Aκ), and no higher
groups; all their components are translates.
The ﬁxed-target test against Z is a fundamental localizer containing S, so it must contain
WQ. But the one-object groupoid Aκ →∗belongs to WQ, because PAκ = 1. Its mapping-
space test against Z fails: the identity of K(Aκ, 1) and the constant map induce diﬀerent
maps on π1, since Aκ ̸= 0. This contradiction proves nonaccessibility. It also gives a direct
bounded proof of the required form of the obstruction in [30, Theorems 6.3–6.4].
Finally an arbitrary model localization has its original-ﬁbrant local objects as a full
homotopy subcategory, and its weak equivalences are detected by derived mapping spaces
into them. This follows using the same coﬁbrant replacements and original cosimplicial
resolutions: the coﬁbrations and trivial ﬁbrations have not changed, and an original resolution
remains a resolution in the localized structure. After the equivalence with spaces in F.3a,
restriction to Cat is therefore a fundamental localizer by the preceding relative-Theorem-A
argument. Under the class form of Vopěnka used in Part H, Cisinski’s Scholie 4.2.17 makes
it accessible. F.3b then supplies its set-generated higher model, and the mapping-space
tests make the weak-equivalence classes equal. This proves the positive assertion under that
principle. The two additional set-theoretic hypotheses are being distinguished explicitly;
automatic accessibility in ZFC has not been inserted into the statement of 317.
F.3d (Proved). Reversibility and spectral classes transport exactly. A full sieve of
a strict n-category is the full subcategory on a set of objects closed under taking the source of
a 1-cell whose target is in that set. A two-sieve Mayer–Vietoris square consists of a cover
79

X3 = X1 ∪X2 of this kind and X0 = X1 ∩X2. For a map of two such squares, higher Loc (6 b)
is the assertion
f1, f2, f3 ∈W (n)
=⇒
f0 ∈W (n).
For the localizers in F.3b this holds exactly when W satisﬁes ordinary Loc (6 b). For a
localizing subcategory L of spectra put
Rn(L) = {f : Σ∞C(Bnf) ∈L}.
(F.3.2)
Then W (n) = Rn(L) if and only if W = R(L). Therefore the accessible classiﬁcation question,
including the negative answer of Part B, has the same answer in every strict dimension.
Proof. The last-vertex argument in the audit shows that applying Nn to a two-sieve cover
gives a union of simplicial subsets. Applying i∆gives a cover by full ordinary sieves: every
face or degeneracy of a simplex lying in one member still lies in that member. Thus a higher
Mayer–Vietoris cube becomes an ordinary Mayer–Vietoris cube, with its four comparison
functors exactly i∆Nnfi. Formula (F.3.1) proves the implication from ordinary Loc (6 b) to
the higher version. Conversely ordinary categories and their full sieves are special cases of
the higher ones, so restriction proves the reverse implication. This argument also proves the
correspondence for the forward gluing axiom and for the reverse single-square axiom, without
mistaking an arbitrary algebraic pushout for a homotopy pushout.
The category-of-simplices comparison gives a natural weak equivalence B(i∆NnX) ≃BnX.
It is natural in the speciﬁed map f, hence induces an equivalence of its homotopy coﬁbers,
including the convention for empty sources. Formula (F.3.2) is consequently exactly the
inverse-nerve class of R(L). Restriction gives necessity, and (F.3.1) gives suﬃciency. In
particular applying F.3b to the accessible counterexample of Part B produces the corresponding
counterexample in every dimension. No assertion that arbitrary Rn(L) is set-generated is
needed for this equivalence.
F.3e (Proved).
What properness gives for the ﬁbration axioms.
For every
1 ≤n ≤∞, right properness in 317 implies Loc (7 F) for Wn, with strict pullbacks and with
FibWn = {p : every base change along p preserves Wn}.
For n = 1, W1 = W∞also satisﬁes Grothendieck’s stronger Loc (7) and Loc (7 D). Their
proof uses the simplex test for the ordinary nerve, in addition to properness. In higher
dimensions, the direct conclusion of the proper model structure is the same Loc (7 F); 317
supplies no higher deﬁnition of the Cat-ﬁbration or perfect-functor classes occurring in the
literal other two axioms.
Proof. Every model ﬁbration lies in FibWn by right properness. Factor any strict n-functor
as a model trivial coﬁbration followed by a model ﬁbration. This gives a Wn-map followed
by a member of FibWn, the existence assertion in Loc (7 F). Composition and strict base
change preserve this class by its deﬁnition. Every map X →∗is in it, because the Street
nerve preserves products and products with a ﬁxed simplicial set preserve weak equivalences.
Isomorphisms are in it; pullbacks of its maps and of its weak equivalences exist and have the
required properties. Finally factoring the diagonal provides the path-object axiom. These
veriﬁcations give the stated Brown category of ﬁbrations, including the terminal and empty
cases.
For ordinary categories, let p : E →B satisfy Grothendieck’s weak-ﬁbration test (Ff),
XVI ms. 27: pullback preserves weak equivalences between categorically contractible bases.
80

Every ordinal category [m] is contractible. Hence, for every simplex [n] →B and order map
[m] →[n], (Ff) gives a weak equivalence
E ×B [m] −→E ×B [n].
Since N[m] = ∆[m] and N preserves pullbacks, these are exactly the simplex pullback tests in
Rezk’s Theorem 4.1(4), specialized to the topos of sets [74]. That theorem makes Np sharp,
which by deﬁnition means that all its base changes preserve weak equivalences. Taking nerves
again shows p ∈FibW∞. Thus Fibweak
W∞⊆FibW∞, the strongest weak-ﬁbration formulation of
Loc (7) at XVI ms. 32. The Cat-ﬁbrations with homotopically constant transports and the
perfect functors are subclasses of this weak-ﬁbration class, so the displayed inclusion gives
Loc (7) in its Cat-ﬁbration formulation and Loc (7 D). For the former subclass one can check
(Ff) directly by the Grothendieck construction of the ﬁbre diagram; for the latter it is the
smooth-and-proper base-change test in the deﬁnition of perfect functors. Thus no claim that
an arbitrary model ﬁbration is a Grothendieck Cat-ﬁbration is being made.
For n > 1, an oriental is not the ordinal category [m], and NnOm is not ∆[m]. Thus
repeating the preceding proof with orientals would replace the actual simplex pullbacks by
diﬀerent ones and would require a new comparison theorem. Moreover the manuscript’s
classes CatﬁbW and ParfW are classes of ordinary functors, not deﬁnitions on strict n-functors.
If they are transported by the ordinary category-of- simplices construction, the corresponding
nerve-based statements follow from the preceding paragraph by deﬁnition; that does not
identify an intrinsic higher Cat-ﬁbration class. The exact dimension-independent implication
of 317 itself is therefore the proved Loc (7 F). Left properness gives pushout invariance
along model coﬁbrations, and supplies no additional assertion about those stronger ﬁbration
classes.

Stable derivators and unstable suspension
G.1 (Proved). Every strong stable derivator has a stable coﬁber localizer. For
every strong stable derivator D, with the spectrum action speciﬁed below, the class
LD = {V ∈Ho(Sp) : V ⊗M = 0 for every M ∈D(e)}
is a localizing subcategory and
WD = R(LD).
Consequently the reversible localizer WS of Part A is not the constant-coeﬃcient localizer of
any strong stable derivator.
Proof. We use the canonical action of the derivator of spectra.
Cisinski–Tabuada, [40,
Theorem A.11 and Appendix A.3], give the following precise universal property: for a small
category A and a triangulated derivator D, restriction along the spectral Yoneda embedding
identiﬁes cocontinuous morphisms HO(SptA) →D with D(Aop). For A = e it assigns to M
the cocontinuous morphism V 7→V ⊗M taking the sphere spectrum to M. The theorem is
A.11 in the published paper; the opened arXiv version numbers the same statement A.15.
The paper’s strong triangulated derivators include the strong stable derivators considered
here. No presentation of D by a model category is assumed.
This action is exact and preserves arbitrary coproducts in the spectrum variable. Its
kernel for a ﬁxed M is therefore triangulated and closed under coproducts, and intersecting
these kernels gives the claimed localizing subcategory. For a small category A, preservation
of homotopy colimits identiﬁes
(Σ∞(BA)+) ⊗M ∼= (pA)!p∗
AM,
pA : A →e.
81

Indeed the spectrum on the left is the homotopy colimit over A of the constant sphere
spectrum, and its action takes this diagram to the constant diagram of value M. In the
presheaf convention one uses Aop; canonical reversal of a nerve simplex gives the natural
homeomorphism of classifying spaces needed to write the displayed formula with BA.
For u : A →B, adjunction identiﬁes the comparison on morphisms between constants
with
HomD(e)((pB)!p∗
BM, N) −→HomD(e)((pA)!p∗
AM, N).
Thus the deﬁnition at I ms. 61 says, by Yoneda and for every M, that the speciﬁed map
Σ∞(BA)+ ⊗M −→Σ∞(BB)+ ⊗M
is invertible. Its coﬁber is Σ∞C(Bu) ⊗M, since both suspension spectra and the action
preserve the indicated homotopy pushout. In a stable category a map is invertible exactly
when its coﬁber is zero.
This is the asserted equality, including empty categories and
disconnected spaces. Part A proves that WS is not ﬁxed by RT, whereas every R(L) is ﬁxed
by RT by the Galois identities checked there; hence WS cannot be WD.
G.2 (Proved). Every accessible fundamental localizer has an ordinary coeﬃcient
realization.
Let W be an accessible fundamental localizer, in the relative-Theorem-A
convention. The derivator HOTW of W-local spaces satisﬁes
WHOTW = W.
The realizing derivator need not be stable.
Proof. Cisinski’s Theorems 4.2.15 and 1.4.3 construct the corresponding accessible localization
of simplicial sets; let L : S →SW be its reﬂector. First W is stable under product with
any small category. For f : A →B and C, apply relative Theorem A over C to f × C. Its
comma-category comparison at c is f × (C/c), whose projection to f is an objectwise weak
equivalence because C/c has a terminal object. Two out of three supplies the hypotheses.
Passage through the test-category correspondence gives the same statement for products
with spaces.
If Z is local, then Map(K, Z) is local for every space K: testing a map f ∈W against
it is the same as testing f × K against Z. In particular each mapping space between local
objects is local. A map in W consequently induces bijections on all hom-sets between constant
diagrams in HOTW . Conversely, taking the ﬁrst constant to be the terminal space gives, for
every local Z, the bijection
[BB, Z] −→[BA, Z].
By the localization adjunction these are the maps [LBB, Z] →[LBA, Z]. Yoneda in Ho(SW )
makes the speciﬁed LBu an isomorphism, which is precisely u ∈W. This checks the previously
asserted realization rather than assuming that every abstract derivator comes from localized
spaces. The construction also covers the total localizer.
G.3 (Proved). Suspension for the sphere localizer is conservative, neither faithful
nor full. In the pointed WS-homotopy category, suspension is conservative, neither faithful
nor full. Thus it has the same behavior in any pointed derivator of WS-local spaces, as in the
question. These assertions also hold for the accessible smallest reversible localizer containing
RP ∞→∗constructed in Part B.
82

Proof. For a reversible localizer, the coﬁber criterion and desuspension closure give, for every
pointed map f,
Σf ∈W
⇐⇒
f ∈W,
since C(Σf) ≃ΣC(f). Every arrow in the formal pointed localization is represented by a roof
X
f−→Z
w
←−Y with w ∈W. To see the existence of such roofs, replace a map to be pushed out
by its mapping-cylinder coﬁbration and use the homotopy pushout. Cobase change preserves
W, because it preserves the homotopy coﬁber. This moves any inverse in a ﬁnite zigzag
past the following forward arrow; iteration produces the claimed roof. If its suspension is
invertible, strong saturation says Σf ∈W, and the displayed implication gives f ∈W. For
WS, strong saturation also follows directly from its mapping-space description established
below; for the accessible localizer of Part B it follows from its localization model. Equivalently,
in a local-space presentation, derived suspension is LΣ, and the same computation on a
representing map proves conservativity directly.
We need an actual local target to detect failure of faithfulness. For an unpointed map
f : X →Y in WS, apply the pointed mapping-space functor to the coﬁber sequence of
f+ : X+ →Y+. Coﬁber rotation gives the ﬁbre sequence

Consider the Whitehead square a = [ι2, ι2] : S3 →S2. Its composite with S2 →S2
(2) is
nonzero: π3(S2) = Z is generated by the Hopf map η, and a = ±2η, so localization gives
the nonzero element ±2 ∈Z(2). For clarity, the coeﬃcient two can be read directly from
the attaching map of the top cell of S2 × S2: after folding the two 2-sphere summands, the
square of the degree-two cohomology generator has coeﬃcient two. Its Hopf invariant is
therefore two. The Hopf ﬁbration identiﬁes π3(S2) with π3(S3) = Z, and its generator has
Hopf invariant one. These are the speciﬁc classical sphere facts used here; see [46, Example
4.51, pp. 380–381, and the proof of Corollary 4J.4, p. 474].
Since S2
(2) is local, maps into it factor through the WS-localization, so the image of a
there is not zero. But Σa = 0. Indeed the Whitehead product is the attaching map in
the coﬁber sequence S3 →S2 ∨S2 →S2 × S2; after suspension the inclusion of the wedge
has a homotopy retraction, given by the two suspended projections and the suspension
comultiplication. Exactness of this coﬁber sequence makes the suspended attaching map null,
and composing with the fold gives Σa = 0. Thus suspension is not faithful. For the smaller
accessible reversible localizer from Part B, the same sphere remains local because its weak
equivalences form a subclass of WS, and the same argument applies.
Fullness already fails in the formal localization; a right adjoint to suspension need not
be assumed for this conclusion. Let i : S2 →C(a) be the coﬁber inclusion. Since Σa is
nullhomotopic, the coﬁber of Σa splits as ΣS2 ∨Σ2S3. Under the natural identiﬁcation
C(Σa) ≃ΣC(a), projection onto the ﬁrst summand gives a homotopy retraction r : ΣC(a) →
ΣS2 of Σi. If localized suspension were full, there would be a morphism g : C(a) →S2 with
83

Σg = r. Consequently Σ(gi) = id, and conservativity would make gi invertible. But ia = 0 in
the coﬁber sequence. It would follow that a = (gi)−1g(ia) = 0, contradicting the preceding
paragraph. This proves nonfullness independently of the reﬂector construction established in
G.4 and G.6–G.7.
The comparison with R9 is now precise. Its nontriviality hypothesis applies, since S2
(2)
is a noncontractible local object. We conﬁrm the step of GALOIS B.6 used here. In this
pointed coeﬃcient category, the identity and zero endomorphisms of S2
(2) are distinct. For
a nontrivial acyclic group G, the coherent free G-object obtained by left Kan extension
from the unique object of BG has regular permutation action on the coproduct indexed by
G. Assigning the identity to one summand and zero to the others detects a nonidentity
permutation. Thus it cannot be constant, and BG →∗is not a locally constant coeﬃcient
equivalence. Its suspension is weakly contractible, since BG is connected and integrally
acyclic. The map of double-cone covers from BG to the point therefore has both cone maps
and the total map in W lc, but not its intersection map, refuting Loc (6 b). The map of covers
(BG, BG, Cw, Cw) →(BG, Cv, Cw, ΣBG) has its intersection and total maps in W lc, while
BG →Cv is not, refuting Loc (6 c). These are precisely the two separate countercubes in
GALOIS B.6. Hence its locally constant coeﬃcient localizer fails both axioms even though
the underlying WS-homotopy suspension is conservative. The coﬁber tests deﬁning WS must
not be identiﬁed with this locally constant coeﬃcient class.

The proof is G.6–G.7 below.
Consequently every

The group D2 is a nonzero rational vector space. Multiplication by an odd integer is
invertible on both groups in the quotient. Multiplication by two on D2 is onto because one
may subtract an integer from a 2-adic integer to make it divisible by two; it is one-to-one

induced maps make K(Q, n −2) a homotopy retract of Pn, so it is local. Odd n can be
arbitrarily large. Taking loop spaces, which are homotopy limits of local objects, gives locality
of K(Q, d) for every d ≥0.
It follows that every map in WS is a rational homology equivalence. Indeed locality of all
these Eilenberg–Mac Lane spaces gives isomorphisms on ordinary rational cohomology in all
nonnegative degrees. The universal coeﬃcient theorem over the ﬁeld Q identiﬁes these maps
with the linear duals of the corresponding homology maps; a linear map of vector spaces is an
isomorphism if its dual is, including for inﬁnite-dimensional vector spaces. Thus WS ⊆WQ.

Proof. We give a strict compact model and a compactness argument for small subcomplexes.
The proof does not identify the homotopy category of an arrow category with the arrow
category of a homotopy category.
Compact Kan models. Let K = ∆n/∂∆n, n ≥2, and let R be the reduced free simplicial
F2-module functor, regarded as a monad on pointed simplicial sets. The standard Bousﬁeld–
Kan resolution has cosimplicial terms
Xq = Rq+1K
(q ≥0).
The precise construction and its identiﬁcation with F2-completion are in [23, §7.7,
pp. 1032–1033].
Each simplicial degree of each Xq is ﬁnite, since K has ﬁnitely many
87

simplices in each degree and a free vector space on a ﬁnite set over F2 is ﬁnite. Also each Xq
is a Kan complex, being a simplicial abelian group.
Here is a direct check that this cosimplicial resolution is Reedy ﬁbrant. Its codegeneracies
si are linear for the outer R-module structures, as are all cofaces di with i ≥1. A matching
tuple in degree q is determined by compatible elements (a0, . . . , aq−1) in degree q −1. Start
with c = 0. If sic = ai for i < j, replace c by
c + dj+1(aj −sjc).
The identities sjdj+1 = id and sidj+1 = djsi for i < j show that this corrects the jth equation
and preserves the previous ones: the latter assertion uses siaj = sj−1ai. This construction
is linear and simplicial. Thus each matching map is a split surjection of simplicial abelian
groups, hence a Kan ﬁbration. In cosimplicial degree zero the assertion is just the Kan
property already noted.
Totalization is right Quillen for the Reedy structure; see the statement immediately
before [23, Proposition 8.1, p. 1034], or check the horn lifting adjunction against the Reedy
coﬁbrations ∆• ⊗Λk[m] →∆• ⊗∆[m]. Therefore

Each factor is ﬁnite: the product of the two standard simplices has ﬁnitely many nondegenerate

Thus Zn is a simplicial compact metrizable Hausdorﬀspace with underlying Kan complex.
This argument supplies an actual strict simplicial model, not just compact topologies on

A bounded extension lemma. Suppose Z is any pointed Kan complex whose levels have
compact metrizable Hausdorﬀtopologies and continuous simplicial operators. For a pointed
simplicial set A, the set

is a closed subspace of the product of the target levels indexed by simplices of A, hence is
compact Hausdorﬀ. For countable A it is metrizable. Restrictions are continuous.
Fix a pointed simplicial set X and a countable pointed simplicial subset A ⊆X. Let CA
be the image of HZ(X) →HZ(A). This is compact and closed. For A ⊆L ⊆X obtained by

Indeed a compatible assignment on A belongs to every image on the right precisely when

are closed conditions with the ﬁnite intersection property. Compactness gives a simultaneous
solution, which is the required map X →Z.
Choose a countable basis of HZ(A). The basic open sets whose closures miss CA cover
its complement: compact metrizable spaces are regular, so the basis may be chosen to have

many of the sets CL,A already have intersection disjoint from U. Their ﬁnite union of added

We have proved that every countable simplicial subset of a member of c

the realization of a countable simplicial set has countably many cells, and every countable

subset. Starting with a simplicial model of an arbitrary CW homotopy type and then using

The rational condition and conclusion. The class of connected rationally acyclic spaces
has the same countable enlargement property. Given a countable subcomplex of a rationally
acyclic X, there are only countably many ﬁnite rational chains and cycles on it. Adjoin the
ﬁnite supports of bounding chains in X for all its reduced cycles, and repeat countably. Every
cycle in the resulting union occurs at some stage and bounds at the next; the union is therefore
rationally acyclic. This includes reduced degree zero and so also ensures connectedness.
If X ∈N, G.6 says that X is both completed-sphere-null and rationally acyclic. Alternate
the two countable enlargement constructions just proved. Their common sequential union
belongs to both classes, because both are closed under pointed homotopy colimits. It therefore
belongs to N, again by G.6. Thus N = Nℵ1. B.2 applies to both null classes, proving the
stated accessibility and the explicit generating sets. Cisinski’s localization construction used
in G.2 now gives the reﬂector and its pointed derivator in U, so the suspension and coeﬃcient
conclusions of G.3 are unconditional ZFC statements for the full WS.

Accessibility of locally constant coeﬃcient theories
H.1 (Proved). Products of derivators and intersections of localizers.
Let I ∈V
be a set and let Di be derivators on U-small categories with values in V-categories. Their
pointwise product is a derivator, and
W lcQ
i∈I Di =
\
i∈I
W lc
Di.
This includes the empty product.
Proof. All restrictions and Kan extensions are taken coordinatewise. The coproduct-to-
product comparison of Der 1, the detection of isomorphisms of Der 2, the two adjunctions
of Der 3, and the pointwise mate isomorphisms of Der 4 are therefore products of their
corresponding comparisons in the Di. A product of isomorphisms is an isomorphism, and all
the indicated products exist in the larger universe. This veriﬁes the four axioms without a
strongness assumption.
An object of the product derivator is locally constant precisely when every coordinate
is locally constant, so its locally constant category is the product of the locally constant
categories. Each such category is nonempty: the terminal object, constructed by right Kan
extension from the empty category, is constant and hence locally constant. For functors
Fi : Ci →Ei between nonempty categories, Q Fi is an equivalence if and only if all Fi are
equivalences. To recover full faithfulness of one factor, ﬁll the other coordinates by chosen
objects and their identity morphisms; injectivity and surjectivity of the resulting hom-set
map give the two required properties. To recover essential surjectivity, ﬁll the other target
coordinates with objects already in the corresponding images. This proves the asserted
equality. The empty product is the terminal derivator, whose localizer is all functors, as is
the empty intersection.
H.2 (Proved). Vopěnka’s principle gives accessibility in the indexing universe.
Assume Vopěnka’s principle for U-classes: every V-class of U-small structures which is proper
relative to U satisﬁes the principle. Then W lc
D is generated as a fundamental localizer by a
U-small set, for every derivator in the stated two-universe setting.
Proof. We check the arbitrary-derivator input of GALOIS B.3. For a small category T and a
subcategory R, the full subprederivator of DT inverting the R-arrows has both adjoints to
its inclusion. Cisinski’s Theorem 3.6 identiﬁes it with cocontinuous morphisms out of the
simplicial-presheaf derivator localized at R. The inclusion of the local simplicial presheaves
preserves homotopy colimits, because these are pointwise and a pointwise colimit still inverts
R. Precomposition with the two cocontinuous adjoints gives the left projector; the opposite
derivator gives the right projector, and the same argument on every shift makes them
morphisms of derivators. These are the steps of [39, Theorem 3.6, Lemma 3.9, and Theorem
3.13] actually required here.
For a Grothendieck opﬁbration T →S, apply the right projector to the vertical arrows.
Restriction to a ﬁbre commutes with this projector: the left adjoint of ﬁbre restriction takes
a locally constant object to a vertically locally constant object, since its value at x ∈Tt is the
coproduct of the values on the transports of x, indexed by arrows t →s. Taking right adjoints
gives the claimed commutation. For a transport-preserving map of opﬁbrations, right Kan
extension commutes with ﬁbre restriction; the inclusion of the strict-ﬁbre comma category in
the total comma category has the left adjoint given by cocartesian transport. The unit and
counit of restriction followed by projected right Kan extension can consequently be tested
90

ﬁbrewise. If all ﬁbre restrictions are equivalences, Der 2 makes the global unit and counit
isomorphisms. Equivalence on the vertically constant categories restricts to equivalence on
the fully locally constant categories, since the transport maps are reﬂected as isomorphisms
by the ﬁbre equivalences. Finally any A →S has the canonical replacement
R
s∈S(A/s) →S;
its forgetful functor to A has an adjoint section and induces equivalence on locally constant
objects. This proves relative Theorem A with precisely the Der 1–4 hypotheses in the question.
Saturation is immediate from equivalence of the speciﬁed restriction functors, and a terminal
object gives the usual adjoint contraction. Thus W lc
D is a fundamental localizer.
Cisinski’s Scholie 4.2.17 states that, under Vopěnka, every fundamental localizer is
accessible. Its proof uses Theorem 4.2.15 to pass to the simplicial-presheaf localizer for the
test category ∆, Scholie 1.4.6 to obtain a small generating family there, and the accessibility-
preserving inverse correspondence in Theorem 4.2.15 to return to Cat. Apply these three
statements inside the indexing universe U. The resulting generators are U-small, despite the
coeﬃcient categories living in V. No bound on the size of the family of coeﬃcient objects
is inserted. The explicitly stated class form of Vopěnka is relevant: an internal ﬁrst-order
scheme with no permission to use the externally supplied derivator as a class parameter
would not by itself justify this application.
H.3 (Proved).
A nonaccessible locally constant localizer when there are no
measurable cardinals. Suppose that U contains no measurable cardinal. There exists a
derivator on all U-small categories with values in V-categories for which W lc is not generated
by a U-small set. It can be chosen as the derivator of a complete and cocomplete, locally
U-small inﬁnity-category of localized 1-types.
Proof. For each inﬁnite cardinal κ ∈U, put
Aκ = Zκ/Z<κ,
where the denominator consists of functions supported on fewer than κ coordinates, and let
A be the class of these groups. A group G is A-reduced when every homomorphism Aκ →G
is trivial. We use exactly Proposition 6.2 of [29]: in the absence of measurable cardinals,
Z is A-reduced. The statement relativizes to U, since a nonzero homomorphism Aκ →Z
would yield a measurable cardinal at most κ. The paper’s Theorem 6.4 concerns a homotopy
idempotent functor; the additional derivator construction and the shift from π1 to π2 below
are necessary here. The reﬂector itself is also covered by [35, Theorem 3.3 with n = 1, and
Section 6.1]; that theorem does not by itself compute the locally constant coeﬃcient localizer
used below.
We ﬁrst construct the coeﬃcient category with its size bounds. For a U-small group G, let
R(G) be the intersection of the kernels of all homomorphisms from G to A-reduced groups.
One may use only quotient images of G: reduced groups are closed under subgroups, and all
such quotient images have size at most |G|. There is a U-small set of these quotients. The
group G/R(G) embeds into their product, so it is reduced, since products and subgroups
of reduced groups are reduced. This constructs the reﬂector PAG = G/R(G) within U. Its
universal property is immediate from the deﬁnition of R(G).
The assignment is functorial: for a homomorphism v : G →H, composing every map from
H to a reduced group with v gives v(R(G)) ⊆R(H). Apply the quotient at every isotropy
group of a groupoid. Conjugation along an arrow x →y identiﬁes R(Aut(x)) with R(Aut(y)).
Thus these subgroups form a normal subgroupoid; quotient each arrow torsor by its source
subgroup, equivalently by its target subgroup. Composition is well deﬁned, and the object
set is unchanged. For a reduced groupoid H, every functor G →H factors uniquely through
91

this quotient, and the same is true of natural isomorphisms. Thus it deﬁnes a reﬂector of
inﬁnity-categories of 1-types. Compose it with 1-truncation to obtain
L : SU −→C,
LX = NPAΠ1X,
where C is the full inﬁnity-category of U-small 1-types with reduced isotropy groups. Its
object collection and all its diagram homotopy categories are V-small; its mapping spaces are
U-small.
The reﬂector preserves products. For groups, a homomorphism G × H →K into a
reduced group descends on both commuting factors to PAG and PAH, and their descended
images still commute. The product PAG × PAH is itself reduced, proving the assertion by
the universal property. The groupoid and space statements follow componentwise and from
product preservation of Π1. The category C is closed under all U-small homotopy limits: it is
the orthogonality class of S2 →∗and all BAκ →∗. Its colimits are obtained by applying L
to space colimits. Therefore it is complete and cocomplete for the required shapes, and
DC(I) = Ho Fun(NIop, C)
satisﬁes Der 1–4. More explicitly, functors on a disjoint union are tuples of functors, an
equivalence of natural transformations is detected at each object, both Kan extensions
exist because the required comma diagrams are U-small, and their pointwise formulas are
precisely Der 4. All these Kan extensions have values in C by its established completeness
and cocompleteness. Presentability in U is not assumed.
We compute its locally constant localizer. On a connected pointed space (X, x), evaluation
Ux : Fun(X, C) →C is conservative, since paths identify all point evaluations, and it preserves
colimits, which are computed pointwise. It has a left adjoint. The inﬁnity-categorical Barr–
Beck theorem, [63, Theorem 4.7.3.5], says that a right adjoint is monadic if it is conservative
and preserves geometric realizations of the simplicial objects split by it. Here all geometric
realizations are preserved. The free-evaluation monad is
TX(M) = (ΩxX) ⊗M = L(ΩxX × M) = LΩxX × M.
All equalities refer to the canonical maps, and the multiplication is induced by concatenation
of loops. For a pointed map f : X →Y , restriction is consequently restriction of modules
for the speciﬁed monad map TX →TY . It is an equivalence if and only if that monad map
is an equivalence. Necessity also follows directly by identifying the left adjoints of the two
evaluation functors; evaluation of their comparison at M = ∗gives LΩf. Suﬃciency is the
equivalence of module categories of equivalent monads.
This calculation concerns inﬁnity-categories, whereas the question uses ordinary categories.
The distinction causes no loss here. Restriction preserves all cotensors. Full faithfulness on
homotopy categories, tested against the cotensors NK for every U-small space K, gives
bijections [K, Map(M, N)] for every K, so Yoneda makes the speciﬁed mapping-space
comparison a weak equivalence.
Essential surjectivity is unchanged on passage to the
homotopy category. Thus the two equivalence tests agree. For disconnected spaces, the initial
and terminal objects of C distinguish missing and repeated components; equivalence forces a
bijection on components. The connected calculation then applies to each factor. We have
proved
f ∈W lc
DC
⇐⇒







(H.3.1)

There is no condition on higher homotopy groups. In particular K(Aκ, 2) →∗is in this class
for every κ, since the quotient unit Aκ →PAAκ is itself a homomorphism to a reduced group
and is therefore trivial.
Every map in (H.3.1) induces integral cohomology isomorphisms in degrees zero, one, and
two. The ﬁrst two degrees follow from π0 and π1. To see the third on a connected component,
ﬁlter the bar resolution of π = π1X tensored with the cellular chains of the universal cover.
The ﬁrst-quadrant cohomology spectral sequence has entries Hp(π; Hq( eX; Z)). Since the
universal cover is simply connected, its row q = 1 vanishes and its row q = 2 is Hom(π2X, Z).
The terms of total degree two therefore give the natural exact sequence
0 −→H2(π; Z) −→H2(X; Z) −→Homπ(π2X, Z) −→H3(π; Z).
Because Z is reduced, reﬂection gives Hom(PAB, Z) = Hom(B, Z) naturally in B. Thus
(H.3.1) makes all terms other than H2(X; Z) isomorphic in the comparison of these exact
sequences, including the group actions; a diagram chase gives the remaining isomorphism.
Disconnected cohomology is the product of these component calculations.
Suppose now that a U-small set S of functors generates W lc
DC. Choose a regular uncountable
κ ∈U larger than the total number of simplices in the nerves of the sources and targets
of every member of S. For an abelian group B, write Rκ(B) = Bκ/B<κ. This functor is
exact: products of surjections are surjective, and a lift of a vector supported on fewer than κ
coordinates can be chosen with the same support. Applying the snake lemma to the inclusion
of the bounded-support sequence in the product sequence gives exactness of the quotient
sequence.
If C∗is a free chain complex with fewer than κ basis elements in total, there is a natural
degreewise isomorphism
Hom(C∗, Aκ) ∼= Rκ
 Hom(C∗, Z)
.
Surjectivity follows by lifting each basis value to Zκ. For injectivity, fewer than κ basis values
have supports of size less than κ; their union still has size less than κ by regularity. The
isomorphism commutes with the diﬀerential. Exactness of Rκ then gives
Hn(C∗; Aκ) ∼= RκHn(C∗; Z).
Apply this to the normalized nerve-chain complexes of all members of S. Their integral
cohomology comparisons in degrees zero, one, and two are isomorphisms, so the same is true
with coeﬃcients Aκ. It follows that Z = K(Aκ, 2) is local for every member of S: the mapping
spaces into this Eilenberg–Mac Lane space are 2-types with homotopy groups H2−i(−; Aκ)
for 0 ≤i ≤2; all components are translates of one another.
For any ﬁxed space Z, the class of functors inducing equivalences on mapping spaces
into Z is a fundamental localizer. To check relative Theorem A directly, use the natural
decomposition BA ≃hocolims B(A/s) and the analogous decomposition for B; mapping
into Z changes this homotopy colimit to a homotopy limit. Its other two axioms follow
immediately.
Consequently a space local for S inverts the whole fundamental localizer
generated by S. In particular it inverts K(Aκ, 2) →∗. This is impossible: the induced map
on components of mapping spaces would identify the identity of K(Aκ, 2) with its constant
map, whereas Aκ ̸= 0, as the constant function 1 has support κ. This contradiction proves
nonaccessibility.
H.4 (Proved).
Represented derivators give exactly the ﬁrst two truncation
localizers.
Let C be complete and cocomplete for U-small diagrams. For its represented
derivator, W lc = W1 when C is not a preorder up to equivalence; W lc = W0 when C is a
preorder with more than one isomorphism class; and W lc is all functors when C is equivalent
to the terminal category.
93

Proof. Locally constant C-valued diagrams on X factor uniquely through the fundamental
groupoid Π1X; natural transformations factor as well. Thus their category is Fun(Π1X, C),
with the speciﬁed restriction functor.
Suppose ﬁrst that there are distinct arrows r, s : a →c in C. For every nonempty set
T, distinct projections cT →c are distinct: prescribing r and s in the corresponding two
coordinates gives a map from a separating them. For a left G-set T, deﬁne ρg : cT →cT by
ptρg = pg−1t for every product projection pt. These maps form a G-action. Taking the regular
left G-set T = G gives a faithful action, since a nonidentity translation moves a coordinate
and distinct projections are distinct.
Consider a homomorphism ϕ : G →H. If restriction Fun(BH, C) →Fun(BG, C) is
essentially surjective, every G-action is isomorphic to a restricted H-action.
Its kernel
therefore acts trivially on the faithful permutation action, so ϕ is injective. Identify G with
its image in H. If it is a proper subgroup, let H act on cH/G by permutation of the left cosets,
and give c the trivial action. Projection onto the coordinate G ∈H/G is G-equivariant. It is
not H-equivariant, since some element of H moves that coset and distinct projections are
distinct. Hence restriction is not full. Equivalence of the action categories therefore forces ϕ
to be an isomorphism; the converse is immediate.
For a general functor of groupoids, failure of surjectivity on components is detected by two
natural transformations that diﬀer by r and s on a missed component. Failure of injectivity on
components is detected by a diagram taking nonisomorphic objects of C on two components
that merge. Such objects exist: if every object were isomorphic to a terminal object, every
hom-set would be a singleton, contrary to the chosen r, s. Thus equivalence of the restriction
categories ﬁrst forces a bijection of components and then reduces to the group calculation.
This proves W lc = W1.
If C is a preorder, replace it by its equivalent poset. Every group action is trivial, so
the local-system category is Cπ0X. When this poset is nontrivial, its initial and terminal
elements are distinct. For a noninjective component function, a tuple taking these two
diﬀerent values on a pair of merged components cannot lie in the essential image of restriction.
For a nonsurjective component function, choose two target tuples which agree on its image
and take terminal and initial values, in that order, on a missed component. There is no
arrow between the target tuples in that direction, while their restrictions are equal. Hence
restriction is not fully faithful. Conversely a bijection of components gives an isomorphism of
product categories. The terminal-category case is immediate.
9
Group-quotient localizers
Write Ca for the coeﬃcient groups in LM8, B.6: respectively all abelian groups, all metabelian
groups, groups nilpotent of class at most c, ﬁnite ℓ-groups, the groups U(Q) for ﬁnite-
dimensional unipotent algebraic groups U/Q, and all virtually abelian discrete groups.
Throughout this section an a-equivalence of groups means a homomorphism v : G′ →G such
that
Hom(G, H) −→Hom(G′, H)
is bijective for every H ∈Ca.
(H5.0.1)
A map of spaces is in Wa precisely when it is bijective on components and its component
group homomorphisms satisfy (H5.0.1). This formulation includes the continuous or algebraic
universal properties of the two completions; it never replaces a completion by its underlying
abstract group.
H5.1 (Proved). The groupoid orthogonality used here. For a homomorphism v : G′ →
G and a group H, condition Hom(G, H) ∼= Hom(G′, H) is equivalent to Fun(BG, BH) →
94

Fun(BG′, BH) being an equivalence. Consequently (H5.0.1), with the separate component
condition, is exactly the deﬁnition of LM8, B.6.
Proof. The functor groupoids have homomorphisms as objects and conjugating elements of
H as arrows. If restriction is bijective on objects, an element conjugating two restrictions
conjugates their unique extensions, proving full faithfulness. Conversely, essential surjectivity
lets one conjugate an extension so that its restriction equals a prescribed homomorphism
exactly. If two exact extensions have equal restrictions, fullness lifts the identity conjugating
element; that same element is the identity in H, so the extensions are equal. This is also
Casacuberta et al. [31, Theorem 1.3].
The checked calculation in INDLOCAL, B.6 is the unpointed version: its product of sets
Hom(−, H)/H is precisely the set of components of this functor groupoid. Its B.3 follows
from the canonical ﬁrst truncation and, for W0, the component reﬂection. For B.4 the relevant
classical class consists of group maps that are isomorphisms on H1(−; Z) and epimorphisms
on H2(−; Z). Bousﬁeld’s group-localization theorem and realization lemma are Bousﬁeld [16,
Theorems 5.2, 5.5 and Lemma 6.1]. The realization lemma attaches 1- and 2-cells to realize
the group map, then 3-cells to kill the free, spherical relative H2. Thus a homology-invariant
groupoid representable is orthogonal to the group-localization units; conversely the Hopf exact
sequence puts the fundamental-group map of every homology equivalence in the indicated
group class. This proves the asserted description of the local groups. It does not identify an
arbitrary localizer with the maps detected by its small representables.
H5.2 (Refuted). Reverse coﬁbration and homology containment are diﬀerent.
None of the six localizers Wa satisﬁes Loc (6 b). Nevertheless
WZ ⊂Wa
⇐⇒
a ∈{ab, nilc, pro-ℓ, unipQ}.
(H5.2.1)
Thus the asserted equivalence in Part H.5(ii) is false.
Proof. First consider the four-point poset P with two minimal elements, two maximal elements,
and all four possible strict comparisons between these levels. Its nerve is a circle. Add two
incomparable maximal cone vertices u, v, both above all of P, and call the result X. Put
U = P ∪{u} and V = P ∪{v}. These are sieves in X with X = U ∪V and U ∩V = P. The
nerves of U, V are contractible and BX ≃S2. They are therefore Wa-aspherical. The circle
BP is not: every coeﬃcient class contains a nontrivial group and Hom(Z, H) is then nontrivial.
This is the reverse coﬁbration counterexample. The complete poset tables and integral Smith
forms occur in code/outputs/EH5_group_witnesses.json; the Betti numbers are (1, 1) for
P, (1) for each cone, and (1, 0, 1) for X.
Every nilpotent group H has BH integral-homology local. Here is a direct veriﬁcation
of the fact needed in this argument. All K(A, n), n ≥1, are integral-homology local by
the universal coeﬃcient theorem and the formula for their mapping-space homotopy groups.
A central extension 1 →A →H →Q →1 expresses BH as the homotopy ﬁber of its
extension-class map BQ →K(A, 2). Induction on a ﬁnite central series proves the assertion,

unipotent group in characteristic zero is nilpotent. Hence every integral homology equivalence
is inverted by each coeﬃcient target, giving the four inclusions.

Proof. Locality for S2 →∗says exactly that every component is a 1-type. For a 1-type

H5.4 (Proved). Accessibility for ﬁnite and virtually abelian tests. The pro-ℓand
virtually abelian localizers are accessible. In each case a generating set consists of S2 →∗and
all its group equivalences BG′ →BG with |G′|, |G| ≤γ, for a single U-cardinal γ constructed
below. These maps are then represented by categories of simplices.
Proof. We ﬁrst prove a bounded-generation lemma for a compact Hausdorﬀgroup C. Let
v : G′ →G induce a bijection Hom(G, C) →Hom(G′, C), and start with subgroups A ⊆G′,
B ⊆G, v(A) ⊆B. There is a countable enlargement procedure with the following three
requirements.
First, for each homomorphism A →C not extending to G′, adjoin ﬁnitely many elements
of G′ on whose generated extension it already fails to extend. Such a ﬁnite obstruction exists
96

because homomorphisms into C are the closed solution sets of the multiplication equations in
the compact product CG′. Do the same for B ⊆G. Second, enlarge B to contain the image
of the new A. Third, for each b ∈B and distinct c, d ∈C, consider the compact space of pairs
φ, ψ : G →C with φ(b) = c, ψ(b) = d. No such pair agrees on all of v(G′), by injectivity of
restriction. The closed equalities φ(v(a)) = ψ(v(a)), a ∈G′, therefore have empty intersection
in that compact space. Finitely many of them already have empty intersection. Adjoin these
ﬁnitely many a to A.
Repeat these operations countably, and write A∞, B∞for the unions. Every map A∞→C
extends to G′: each restriction to a stage extends, and the compact spaces of global extensions
form a nested family with nonempty intersection. Similarly every map B∞→C extends to
G. Surjectivity of Hom(B∞, C) →Hom(A∞, C) now follows from the global equivalence v.
For injectivity, extend two maps on B∞globally. If they agree on A∞but diﬀer at some b
with values c, d, the third enlargement at a stage containing b gives a contradiction. Thus
the restricted map is a C-equivalence.
This enlargement has a bound depending only on |A| + |B| + |C| + ℵ0. At a stage of size
at most λ ≥|C| + ℵ0, there are at most 2λ maps and pairs to consider; adjoining the ﬁnite
witnesses and generating subgroups has that same bound. Iterating the power-set bound
countably gives a cardinal b(λ) ∈U. To obtain a directed presentation with one uniform
bound, perform the construction coherently for every ﬁnite subset S of G′ ⨿G. Induct on
|S|, requiring its chosen enlargement to contain the ﬁnitely many enlargements for proper
subsets of S. If λ0 = |C| + ℵ0 and λr+1 = b(λr), then all chosen submaps have size at most
γ = supr<ω λr ∈U. They form a ﬁltered diagram whose colimit is v. Nerves commute with
ﬁltered colimits of groups, and ﬁltered colimits of simplicial sets compute their homotopy
colimits. Hence every C-equivalence is generated by the indicated bounded maps. Together
with S2 →∗this proves the lemma for the associated fundamental localizer.
For pro-ℓuse the compact group which is the product of representatives of all ﬁnite
ℓ-groups. Testing its Hom functor is equivalent to testing all its factors, so the lemma applies.
For the virtually abelian case we verify that compact targets of bounded size suﬃce. Let
K be the set of all compact Lie groups that are ﬁnite extensions of tori, viewed as abstract
groups. Every virtually abelian group belongs to the closure of K under small limits. To
see this, write 1 →A →H →F →1 with A abelian and F ﬁnite. The normal abelian
subgroup can be obtained by intersecting the ﬁnitely many conjugates of an abelian subgroup
of ﬁnite index. Embed the F-module A equivariantly into D = (R/Z)I with F permuting
coordinates: use all characters of A into the injective cogenerator R/Z, together with their
F-translates. Push the extension out along A ,→D to obtain HD. Its underlying set is
D × F, with multiplication given by the original action and a 2-cocycle. With the product
topology it is a compact group.
The group HD is the inverse limit of its quotients formed by projecting D onto ﬁnite
F-stable coordinate sets. Each quotient is a ﬁnite extension of a ﬁnite-dimensional torus,
and is therefore in K. The quotient HD/A splits as (D/A) ⋊F, since the deﬁning cocycle
has values in A. Embed the F-module D/A into a second permutation product of circles D′.
Then H is the equalizer of the two homomorphisms
HD ⇒D′ ⋊F :
(d, f) 7−→(d, f),
(d, f) 7−→(0, f).
(H5.4.1)
The target D′ ⋊F is again an inverse limit of members of K. This proves the limit assertion.
Since orthogonality of group homomorphisms is preserved by limits of target groups, testing
K is equivalent to testing all virtually abelian groups. Conversely every member of K is
virtually abelian as an abstract group, so no additional test has been introduced. Now take
the compact product of representatives of K and apply the proved lemma. All its factors
have cardinality at most the continuum, so the product and the resulting bound remain in U.
97

This direct group argument avoids a delicate point in the opened proof of Przeździecki
[70, Theorem 19]. The asserted equivalence between the homotopy category of arrows and
arrows in the homotopy category, following its Deﬁnition 10, is false in general: it forgets the
homotopy speciﬁed in a homotopy-commutative square. Our argument uses only closed sets
of actual group homomorphisms, and does not require that equivalence or the aﬀected arrow
category reduction.
H5.5 (Proved). None of the six cases is a stable inverse image. No Wa in the list is
of the form R(L). In fact T(Wa) = Ho(Sp), so its stable closure R(T(Wa)) is all maps.
Proof. Every Wa contains S2 →∗, whose unreduced homotopy coﬁber is S3. A stable
localizing subcategory containing its suspension spectrum contains the sphere and hence
every spectrum. But S1 →∗is not in any of the six classes, as checked in H5.2. Thus none
is the all-map class, proving both assertions.

Proof. We ﬁrst record why condition (1) handles strictness. For a simplex σ : ∆[n] →K
